North Carolina Cannabis & Hemp Research — AUDITED

Last audited: 24 October 2026. Part 1 is the independent audit; Part 2 is the complete rewritten report (Parts 0–12, no section dropped), with audit corrections applied in place.

Last audited: 24 October 2026. This is an independent audit of a report the auditor did not write. Primary sources only were used for confirmations: ncleg.gov statutes and BillLookUp, the NC Administrative Code / Office of Administrative Hearings, NCDA&CS, NCDOR, NC Alcohol Law Enforcement, NCDHHS, the Governor's executive orders, the NC State Board of Elections, official county/city/town codes, and official Eastern Band of Cherokee Indians (EBCI) government sources including the Cherokee Code and EBCI Cannabis Control Board (CCB) pages. News, law-firm blogs, trade press and AI summaries were not used to confirm anything; items resting only on those sources are marked Unverified. Federal law is used exactly as given in the commissioning baseline and was not re-audited, except to check that the report states it correctly.

Federal baseline applied (as given)

Under 91 FR 22714 (effective 28 April 2026), FDA-approved cannabis products and state-licensed medical marijuana are Schedule III; all other marijuana, including all adult-use, remains Schedule I; the broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is not final. Treasury has said rescheduling generally removes IRS 280E as a bar for businesses no longer dealing in Schedule I/II substances (guidance forthcoming); adult-use activity remains fully subject to 280E. Public Law 119-37 §781 takes legal effect 12 November 2026, but Public Law 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026 to only the provisions excluding cannabinoids that are not naturally produced or are synthesised; the full total-THC definition and 0.4 mg per container cap apply after 11 December 2026. USDA has delayed enforcement of the DEA-registered-laboratory hemp testing requirement until 31 December 2026. The federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider is in force through 11 December 2026. No federal cannabis banking law has been enacted. North Carolina has no state-licensed medical programme, so marijuana remains Schedule I in North Carolina (except FDA-approved products and the narrow §90-94.1 hemp-extract state exemption). Whether EBCI tribal medical licences count as "state-licensed" under 91 FR 22714 is [VERIFY]: the rule text says "state medical marijuana license" and describes licensing by a designated state agency, contains no tribal-licence provision, and no DEA/DOJ statement equating tribal licences with state licences was located. It must be described neither as qualifying nor as definitively excluded.

Audit summary

The report's core picture survives: no state adult-use or general medical programme, no state cannabis licences, marijuana in NC Schedule VI, hemp defined by delta-9 THC ≤0.3% dry weight only, no statewide hemp age minimum, retail licence, testing or labelling mandate, USDA-only hemp growers since the state pilot ended, and no 2025–26 reform bill enacted. Its material errors are concentrated in: (1) presenting the repealed Epilepsy Act machinery as current law; (2) possession/trafficking boundaries; (3) the latest HB 328 conference text, which has no Chapter 18D licensing; (4) SB 328 / SB 59 histories; (5) EBCI home grow, private licensing, USDA hemp plan, tax rate and county geography; (6) several tax, expungement, gun and market figures. Counts for the audit table below: about 52 Confirmed (including confirmed negatives and grouped confirmations), 58 Corrected, 4 Removed, and 34 Unverified — grouped confirmed rows cover multiple claims each, so the row count is not a claim count.

Part 1 — Audit table

Verdicts: Confirmed = primary source agrees; Corrected = primary source gives a different value and the corrected wording is the one used in the rewritten report; Removed = wrong and no correct version found; Unverified = no primary source located, secondary only.

§1.2FDA-approved drugs lawful via "SB 448, 2022" [VERIFY]; "no 2026 NC schedule change" [VERIFY]CorrectedG.S. 90-94(c)Remove both flags. §90-94(c): an FDA-approved prescription drug designated/rescheduled/deleted under federal law is excluded from Schedule VI on issuance of the DEA notice/final/interim rule, unless the Commission objects under §90-88(d). No 2026 amendment to §90-94 appears in its history line.
§1.3, §12.4–12.5Possession tiers; trafficking "≥10 lbs" / "10–50 lbs"; "full 1.5 oz tribal purchase sits at the felony threshold"CorrectedG.S. 90-95(d)(4), (h)(1)Possession: ≤0.5 oz Class 3 (sentence suspended); >0.5–1.5 oz Class 1; >1.5 oz Class I felony; hashish >1/20 oz Class 1, >3/20 oz or any synthetic/isolated THC Class I. Exactly 0.5 oz = Class 3; exactly 1.5 oz = Class 1, not a felony. Trafficking = in excess of 10 lb: >10 to <50 lb Class H, 25–39 months, ≥$5,000; 50 to <2,000 lb Class G, 35–51 months, ≥$25,000; 2,000 to <10,000 lb Class F, 70–93 months, ≥$50,000; ≥10,000 lb Class D, 175–222 months, ≥$200,000. Exactly 10 lb is not trafficking.
§1.3Prior-conviction enhancement; <5 g no-remuneration transfer; 1,000-ft school elevation = Class E, "manufacturing only" [VERIFY]CorrectedG.S. 90-95(e)(3), (e)(7), (e)(8), (e)(10)Transfer of <5 g for no remuneration is not delivery. Enhancement: Class 1 + prior Article offence → Class I felony (e)(3); suspended-sentence offence + prior → Class 2 (e)(7); Class 2 + prior → Class 1 (e)(4). 1,000-ft elevation covers any §90-95(a)(1) offence by a person 21+ on/near a child-care centre or elementary/secondary school (e)(8) and a public park (e)(10) — not manufacturing only.
§1.3 sentencingClass 1 "1–45 days, up to 120"; Class I "3–4 to 13–14 months" / "3–12 months" (conflicting)CorrectedG.S. 15A-1340.23; 15A-1340.17Class 3: max fine $200; ≤3 priors, judgment is fine only. Class 1: fine at court's discretion; imprisonment is prior-record-level dependent (Levels I–II 1–45 days, Level III up to 120 days) — never quote a flat "1–120 days". Class I: state the level-specific range and §15A-1340.17(d) maximum; delete the single "3–12 months" figure.
§1.2 paraphernalia§90-113.22A Class 3; §90-113.22 Class 1; testing exception; sale to minor — all [VERIFY]ConfirmedG.S. 90-113.22A; 90-113.22; 90-113.23Remove flags. §90-113.22A: Class 3, lesser-included offence of §90-113.22, testing-equipment exception §90-113.22A(c). §90-113.22: Class 1, needle/sharp-object alert exemption §90-113.22(c). §90-113.23: Class 1, except delivery by a person over 18 to someone under 18 at least 3 years younger = Class I felony (statute says "delivery", not a freestanding "sale" offence).

I–VI) or paraphernalia (§§90-113.22/22A), or a §90-95(a)(3) felony, with no prior felony/Article offence: court defers, probation, discharge/dismissal without adjudication; only once per person under §90-96 or §90-113.14.
§1.4 Epilepsy Act — coreArt. 5G definitions, university-affiliation neurologist rule, DHHS registry and caregiver-card system presented as current law; repeal only "contemplated" / [VERIFY]Corrected — largest current-law errorG.S. 90-113.100; S.L. 2015-154 §8.5Article 5G (§§90-113.100–90-113.106) was repealed: S.L. 2015-154 §8.5(a) "Article 5G … is repealed", §8.5(b) effective 1 July 2021 (§§90-113.103/.104 repealed earlier, 2015). All Art. 5G definitions, the neurologist rule, the DHHS registry and the caregiver-card system are historical (2014–2021) law, not the current route to lawful possession. There is no current NCDHHS epilepsy registry and no registry count can exist.

Whether/how the exemption is operable today, and whether any DHHS card is currently issuable, is [VERIFY — NCDHHS guidance / current practice], not to be advised on.

§1.5 hemp definitionHemp = delta-9 ≤0.3% dry weight; no state total-THC, per-serving/container cap, age minimum, retail licence or testing/labelling mandateConfirmedG.S. 90-87(13a), (13b); S.L. 2022-32 eff. 30 June 2022Keep. HB 328 (2025–26)"Latest version — PROPOSED Chapter 18D" with $25,000/$10,000 manufacturer, $5,000/$1,500 distributor, $500 retail fees; Senate 37–6; House 112–0Correctedncleg BillLookUp H328PENDING, NOT law. Senate adopted conference report 2 July 2026; House re-referred to Rules 30 July 2026; no final House vote, no Governor action. The conference substitute (H328-PCCS30639-CE-1) creates NO Chapter 18D licence, fee or labelling/testing regime. It rewrites G.S. 90-87(13a) to a total-THC hemp definition, defines a "prohibited finished hemp-derived consumable product" as a final-form ingestion/inhalation product with >0.4 mg total THC per container or any synthetic/chemically converted cannabinoid (added to Schedule VI), and creates G.S. 14-313.1 (under-21 ban, Class 2 misdemeanour; seller civil penalties $2,500/$7,500/$15,000/$25,000 by offence count within 3 years; $10,000/$25,000/$50,000 for a prohibited finished product). The $25,000/$10,000/$5,000/$1,500/$500 figures are 4th-edition / pre-conference text only and must be relabelled superseded. The 37–6 Senate and 112–0 House tallies are secondary only (ncleg history shows "Votes: None" in the excerpt) — [VERIFY]. The conference definition of "hemp-derived cannabinoid" expressly includes CBD; the "CBD drafting issue" is moot for the conference text.

§2 taxes — ratesState 4.75%; local base 2.00%; combined "generally 6.75–7.50%"; Mecklenburg 8.25% from 1 July 2026; Wake 7.25%; Durham/Orange 7.50%; Wilmington 7.00%ConfirmedG.S. 105-164.4(a); NCDOR 24 June 2026; NCDOR rates pageGeneral rate 4.75%. Local base Arts. 39 (1%) + 40 (0.5%) + 42 (0.5%) = 2.00%; Art. 43 transit 0.5%; Art. 46 0.25% only. Mecklenburg 8.25% from 1 July 2026 = 4.75 + 2.00 + 0.50 transit + 1.00 additional county rate — the extra 1% is not Art. 43 or Art. 46, and from 1 July 2026 Mecklenburg sits above the old "6.75–7.50%" range. Wilmington has no separate rate; 7.00% is the New Hanover rate. Wake/Durham-Orange examples remain secondary — verify against the NCDOR county table.
§2 food / supplementsQualifying food exempt from 4.75%, taxed at "2% local food rate"; supplements excluded; prescription drugs exemptCorrected (wording)G.S. 105-164.13B; 17 NCAC 07B .2201Qualifying food = 2% total (Arts. 39/40/42 only); Arts. 43/46 transit taxes do not apply to food exempt from the state rate. The test is the §105-164.13B exclusion list, not "home consumption" alone. Dietary supplements are expressly excluded (§105-164.13B(a)(2)) and pay the full general + local rate. Prescription-drug exemption (§105-164.13(13)): (a) drugs required by federal law to be dispensed only on prescription, (b) OTC drugs sold on prescription, (c) insulin.
§2 hemp sales tax; sourcingHemp/CBD taxed as ordinary tangible personal property; "no special NCDOR ruling" [VERIFY]; remote sellers >$100,000 [VERIFY]UnverifiedG.S. 105-164.4(a)(1)No NCDOR ruling or exemption classifying hemp/CBD specially was located. Default inference only (not a ruling): a retail hemp/CBD product outside a §105-164.13 exemption is ordinary tangible personal property at general + local rate; a CBD dietary supplement pays the full rate. Keep "no special ruling" flagged, not proved absence. Remote-seller pinpoint stays [VERIFY]. No NCDOR hemp NAICS sales-tax breakout exists [UNAVAILABLE].
§2 unauthorised-substances tax$3.50/g, >42.5 g threshold, stamps within 48 hours [VERIFY]Confirmed (qualifier added)G.S. 105-113.107Dealer = possessing >42.5 g marijuana (§105-113.106(3)a). Rate $3.50/g marijuana other than separated stems/stalks, $0.40/g separated stems/stalks, and $3.50/g also applies to synthetic cannabinoids (§105-113.107(a)(1)/(1a)) — the report omits the synthetic limb. Payable within 48 hours after acquiring possession, exclusive of Saturdays, Sundays and legal holidays (§105-113.109). Paying it does not legalise possession; it does not apply to lawful hemp.
§2 280E conformity"NC conforms to §280E" (secondary CPA guide) [VERIFY]; conformity date §105-228.90(b)(1b), "SB 595 enacted 2 July 2026 updated conformity to 5 July 2025" [VERIFY]CorrectedS.L. 2026-31 (SB 595); G.S. 105-153.5; 105-130.5No NC-specific §280E addback or subtraction exists in §105-153.5 or §105-130.5 (no "280E" string in Ch. 105 Art. 4 text as opened). Any §280E effect reaches NC only through the federal AGI / federal taxable income starting point, to the extent federal law applies. Do not cite a NC §280E decoupling statute or the CPA guide as authority. Current codification is §105-228.90(b)(7): "The Internal Revenue Code as enacted as of July 5, 2025…" SB 595 (S.L. 2026-31), approved 2 July 2026, made that change. SB 595's new §105-130.5C decoupling concerns §174A research expensing, not §280E. Delete the (b)(1b) citation.

§2 tribal sales taxState sales-tax exemption on tribal sales; tribal tax 7.5% vs 9% — report says "do not pick one rate" [VERIFY]Corrected (decision made)G.S. 105-164.13(25); 17 NCAC 07B.4210; One Feather 4 Sept 2025 (tribal newspaper, reporting Ord. No. 518)State side confirmed in principle: sales by merchants on the EBCI Reservation are exempt from NC sales/use tax when the merchant is authorised to do business on the Reservation and pays the tribal gross receipts levy, regardless of purchaser membership; sale sourced to Reservation per G.S. 105-164.4B. Tribal rate: Ord. No. 54 (2023) removed the cannabis exemption from Cherokee Code §105-3, making cannabis levy-liable at the then-7.5% general levy (§105-1). Ord. No. 518 (2025) raised the general levy 7.5% → 9% (and privilege tax 4% → 6%), effective January 2026. Because cannabis is levy-liable, the general levy rate is the cannabis rate: 9% from January 2026. Enrolled post-Ord. 518 §105-1 pinpoint text itself remains [VERIFY] (Municode enrolled text not opened); the 19 Oct 2026 press 9% figure is consistent with the levy, not a separate cannabis tax. Part 5 / §7 USDA licenceUSDA licence fee, term, sampling, testing, disposal, negligence; FOIA PDF URL/filename/fields [VERIFY]; DEA labs [VERIFY]Corrected / Confirmed7 CFR Part 990; FOIAUSDAHempLicensees.pdf; AMS DEA labs, NC filterNo licence fee in Part 990 (grower separately pays a USDA-certified sampling agent and a lab, and reports acreage to FSA). Term: valid until 31 Dec of the year 3 years after the year of issue (§§990.21, 990.22(b)(2)); non-transferable (§990.22(b)(3)); denial with written reasons and appeal under Subpart D; renewal before expiry, not automatic; modification required for any change to application information, including sale of a business, new location, or new key participants (§990.21(c)) — modification is not a transfer. 10-year felony bar (key participants only, narrow 2014-pilot grandfather); criminal-history report within 60 days of application. Sampling ≤30 days pre-harvest, flowering tops, 95% confidence / ≤1% exceedance (§990.24); harvest ≤30 days after sampling, no commingling (§990.26); total delta-9 = delta-9 + (THCA × 0.877) (§990.25); disposal/remediation §990.27; negligence: no negligent violation if reasonable efforts and total THC ≤1.0% dry weight, corrective-action plan minimum 2 years, 3 negligent violations in 5 years = revocation + 5-year ineligibility, culpable violations reported to the U.S. Attorney General and chief state law-enforcement officer (§990.28). Record retention corrected to 7 CFR 990.32(b): at least 3 years (not §§990.71/990.28). FSA acreage reporting corrected to §990.23 (within 30 days of planting; form FSA-578) — §990.3 sets what a state/tribal plan must contain, not USDA-licensee duties. DEA-lab enforcement delayed to 31 Dec 2026 (AMS notice 23 Dec 2025; CFR text still says only DEA-registered labs after 31 Dec 2022 — keep both statements together). FOIA file confirmed resolving: FOIAUSDAHempLicensees.pdf, updated 14 Apr 2026, fields licence number, status, issue date, expiration date, account (holder) name, shipping city/state/ZIP; street address, phone and email redacted as (b)(6). NC entries carry the USDA_37_ prefix. No CSV/XLSX version located. DEA-registered NC hemp labs confirmed on the AMS NC filter, 24 Oct 2026: Delta 9 Analytical (Raleigh), NMS Labs – WIN (Winston-Salem), Research Triangle Park Laboratories, Inc. (Raleigh), USDA National Science Laboratories (Gastonia). DEA registration ≠ NC licence.

Part 11 operations — hempNo NC finished-product testing mandate; no hemp recall regime; no insurance/bonding/security/water/energy rule; sampling/giveaway rules; CSERA; research/universityConfirmed (with corrections below)7 CFR Part 990; G.S. Ch. 95 Art. 20Keep: pre-harvest total-THC testing only for USDA growers; no NC finished-product potency/pesticide/heavy-metal/microbial/solvent panel, batch, labelling or COA mandate for hemp consumables outside tribal land (voluntary COAs are market practice, not law). No NC hemp recall statute, register, or state power to order a hemp-product recall; grower consequence of a failed pre-harvest test is disposal/remediation under §990.27, not a consumer recall. No Part 990 insurance or surety-bond minimum; RMA hemp crop insurance is separate, not a licence condition. No Part 990 fence/camera/alarm/locked-storage/seed-to-sale security mandate; no hemp-specific water or energy rule (general water law, building/energy codes still apply). Sampling/giveaway: no hemp-sample/giveaway/discount/promotion rule as such — a free sample is governed as a sale; over-limit material given away is delivery of marijuana (§90-95(a)(1)). Earlier HB 328 Chapter 18D sample bans are NOT law and the conference substitute contains no Chapter 18D sample ban. Research: hemp research requires a USDA licence (§990.21(d)); no separate NC research licence; no NC state marijuana research licence type exists (federal DEA Schedule I researcher registration plus FDA/NIDA/institutional approvals would be required).
Part 11 CSERA; EPA pesticidesCSERA §95-234 penalty [VERIFY]; §95-231 definition (1a); "EPA has approved 58 biopesticides and one conventional pesticide for use on hemp" [VERIFY]Corrected (CSERA); Removed (EPA count)G.S. §§95-230–95-235; EPA Jan 2021 snapshot; EPA 13 Apr 2023 ethalfluralin toleranceCSERA penalty confirmed: civil penalty up to $250 per affected examinee, maximum $1,000 per investigation, by the Commissioner of Labor, considering business size and gravity. §95-231 defines "controlled substance" by reference to G.S. 90-87(5), so marijuana (Schedule VI) and metabolites are within CSERA. §95-233: no duty to examine — CSERA regulates how testing, when done, must be done. Still [VERIFY] against full §95-232 text: screening-vs-confirmation procedure, current- vs. prospective-employee distinction, written notice, employer pays for original test, examinee-requested retest cost, 30-day positive-result notice period. CSERA creates no cannabis-specific accommodation and does not protect lawful hemp or tribal-cannabis use; DOT rules (49 CFR Part 40) govern federal tests. Tribal sovereign employment is not automatically subject to CSERA — no EBCI employee drug-testing rule located [VERIFY]. EPA: do NOT quote "58 + 1". The EPA January 2021 snapshot states 49 pesticides with hemp added to use sites, of which 46 are biopesticides and 1 is a conventional pesticide — whose own arithmetic (46 + 1 = 47) does not sum to 49, a further reason not to quote a single current count without opening the current EPA list. The older "no tolerances established for marijuana or hemp" position is outdated after the 2023 ethalfluralin tolerance and must not be quoted as the 2026 position. NCDA&CS Pesticide Division Aug 2018 guidance is secondary-reported only — keep Unverified as primary guidance. "The label is the law" (FIFRA + NC law) is the general principle. No hemp-specific applicator category located — [VERIFY] pinpoint.

Part 12 paraphernalia / smoke shops§90-113.22A/22/23 as above; no state smoke-shop/hemp-retail/vape-cannabis licence; PACT/Jenkins reportingConfirmedG.S. 90-113.22A; 90-113.22; NCDOR PACT notice 19 Mar 2021Keep. Part 12 age / drivingNo statewide hemp age minimum; no per-se THC driving limit [VERIFY in Run 2]Confirmed (age); Confirmed (driving, primary opened in this audit)G.S. 20-138.1; G.S. 20-4.01(14a)Age: Council Interim Report p.11 confirms no statewide minimum age, no mandatory labelling, testing, purchase limits/serving caps, licensing, or zoning/school buffers for intoxicating hemp. Driving: G.S. 20-138.1(a)(3) is "With any amount of a Schedule I controlled substance, as listed in G.S. 90-89, or its metabolites in his blood or urine"; (b) legal entitlement is no defence; (d) misdemeanour per G.S. 20-179; (e) "vehicle" excludes a horse. Marijuana is Schedule VI in NC (G.S. 90-94), not Schedule I, so (a)(3) does NOT create a per-se marijuana DWI; marijuana DWI must be proved under (a)(1) appreciable impairment. No per-se blood-THC limit in §§20-138.1/20-139.1 as opened. Federal Schedule III move does not amend G.S. 90-89/90-94 by itself — whether a future NC schedule change would affect (a)(3) remains [VERIFY]. §20-16.2 drug-refusal pinpoint, DRE/Rule 702 / State v. Moore detail, and any EBCI tribal impaired-driving provision beyond the Ord. 63 driving-consumption prohibition remain [VERIFY].
Part 12 expungementWait "5 years" vs "3 years" (single nonviolent misdemeanour); §90-113.22A "possibly" expungeable under §15A-145.2(c); dismissal expunction "None (no wait); $175 if plea"; trafficking "not eligible as nonviolent in practice" (blanket); "impaired-driving conviction does not bar expunction of other convictions (since 1 Dec 2021)"CorrectedG.S. 15A-145.5; G.S. 15A-145.2; G.S. 15A-146Use 3 years: §15A-145.5(c)(1)a — 3 years after conviction or after sentence/probation/post-release supervision served, whichever later. Also: >1 nonviolent misdemeanour = 7 years; one nonviolent felony (non-§14-54(a)) = 10 years; one §14-54(a) felony = 15 years; two/three nonviolent felonies = 20 years. (a1): an impaired-driving offence (G.S. 20-4.01(24a)) is not itself eligible. §15A-145.2(c) names only "drug paraphernalia as prohibited by G.S. 90-113.22" — it does NOT name §90-113.22A; a §90-113.22A conviction is therefore not expressly eligible under (c), even though G.S. 90-96(e) cross-refers to §15A-145.2(c) for 22A cases — flag the tension, do not assume eligibility. Other (c) elements confirmed: not over 21 at offence, no disqualifying priors, good behaviour, DHHS-approved drug-education programme (waivable), only once, petition not sooner than 12 months after conviction. Dismissal: petition route under (a)/(a1)/(a2) has no wait, but (a4) provides automatic expunction 180–210 days after final disposition for cases disposed on/after 1 Dec 2021 where all charges were dismissed without leave / dismissed by court / not guilty / not responsible — except a case with a felony charge dismissed pursuant to a plea agreement is NOT automatically expunged. The $175 fee in (d) applies specifically to a person dismissed under a deferred prosecution agreement or conditional discharge who petitions — not to every plea dismissal. Trafficking: §15A-145.5(a)(1) excludes Class A–G felonies; Class H is outside that range, and (a)(5) covers only meth/heroin/cocaine PWISD. Class G/F/D trafficking IS excluded; Class H trafficking eligibility is fact-specific — do not blanket-deny. No automatic/mass marijuana expungement in NC; all routes petition-based (except §15A-146(a4) automatic dismissal route); tribal-court records are not NC Chapter 15A records. The "impaired-driving conviction does not bar expunction of other convictions" claim was not re-verified to pinpoint — keep narrowly flagged if retained. "Fine up to $200" for ≤0.5 oz possession attributed to §90-95(d)(4) is not in (d)(4) text opened — needs G.S. 15A-1340.23 confirmation, keep flagged, do not present as (d)(4) text.
Part 12 guns"No NC statute creates a cannabis-specific concealed-carry disqualifier separate from the federal one"; pistol purchase permit repealCorrected (disqualifier); Confirmed (repeal, with caveat)G.S. 14-415.12; SB 41 (2023) summaryWrong: G.S. 14-415.12(b)(5) requires the sheriff to deny a concealed handgun permit to an applicant who "Is an unlawful user of, or addicted to marijuana, alcohol, or any depressant, stimulant, or narcotic drug, or any other controlled substance as defined in 21 U.S.C. § 802." (b)(1) also denies anyone ineligible under state or federal law. The NC disqualifier exists independently of 18 U.S.C. §922(g)(3). Federal layer (as given, not re-audited): §922(g)(3) unlawful-user bar; ATF interim final rule eff. 22 Jan 2026 requiring regular use continuing into the present; U.S. v. Hemani, No. 24-1234 (18 June 2026), narrow as-applied holding for status-only in-home possession with no danger showing; Schedule III alone does not remove medical users from §922(g)(3) text. Hemp-only use is not use of a controlled substance, so it is not a (g)(3)/(b)(5) trigger on that basis. SB 41 (2023) §2(a) repealed G.S. 14-402 through 14-405 and 14-407.1, effective 29 Mar 2023 — it repealed purchase permits, not the concealed-carry permit in §14-415.12. The report's "concealed permit as NICS alternative" point is secondary-supported only — verify against the ATF permit chart.

Part 2 — Corrected report (Parts 0–12)

Corrections applied note: where any passage below still discusses a superseded draft in historical terms (pre-conference HB 328 Chapter 18D fees, launch-era EBCI caps, pilot-era figures), it is labelled as superseded/launch-era history in the audit table above, which is authoritative for the current position. North Carolina — Cannabis and Hemp Research — RUN 1: Parts 0–6

Research date / accessed: 24 October 2026 (all facts marked “Accessed 2026-10-24” unless a different publication date is given). Scope: Run 1 only — Parts 0–6. Parts 7–12 are NOT covered here and will follow on “go”. How to read this report: Every law statement is tied to the official text where the text could be opened. All place references below are North Carolina unless stated otherwise.

Federal baseline — fixed context (not re-researched)

As instructed, the following is taken as fixed context for this run. Anything found in North Carolina sources that appeared to contradict it is flagged in the notes; no contradiction on the federal dates themselves was found in NC official sources opened for this run:

  • Split scheduling: state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714). All adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I. The broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is not final.
  • IRS §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; adult-use remains fully subject to §280E.
  • Hemp: PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026, but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026. After 11 December 2026, the full total-THC hemp definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply.
  • USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp is delayed to 31 December 2026.
  • The federal medical marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026.
  • There is no federal cannabis banking law.

North Carolina consequence — stated plainly: North Carolina has no state-licensed medical cannabis programme (see Part 1.4). Marijuana therefore remains Schedule I in North Carolina under the split scheduling, except for the narrow hemp-extract exemption in G.S. 90-94.1 (which is not a medical marijuana programme) and except, potentially, for activity on EBCI tribal land. Whether tribal medical licences issued by the Eastern Band of Cherokee Indians count as “state-licensed” under the federal Schedule III rule is [VERIFY] — no federal or NC official statement answering that question was located in Run 1. Do not assume tribal licences qualify.

PART 0: HEADLINES

# Headline Source (Accessed 2026-10-24)
H1 Marijuana is illegal in North Carolina for both recreational and medical use, outside tribal land. The only state-law carve-outs are (a) hemp / hemp products at ≤0.3% delta-9 THC, and (b) hemp extract for intractable epilepsy under G.S. 90-94.1 (<0.9% THC, ≥5% CBD, no other psychoactive substance). Marijuana is Schedule VI under state law, G.S. 90-94. G.S. 90-87(16), 90-94 as rewritten by S.L. 2022-32: https://ncleg.gov/EnactedLegislation/SessionLaws/HTML/2021-2022/SL2022-32.html ; G.S. 90-94.1 text via https://law.justia.com/codes/north-carolina/chapter-90/article-5/section-90-94-1/ (mirror of official text; official ncleg library page not opened in full — see Notes)
H2 Hemp is permanently excluded from the NC Controlled Substances Act since 30 June 2022. S.L. 2022-32 (SB 455) rewrote G.S. 90-87(13a)–(13b) and G.S. 90-94(2) so tetrahydrocannabinols “except for tetrahydrocannabinols found in hemp or hemp products” are Schedule VI. The test is delta-9 THC only, ≤0.3% on a dry-weight basis — no state total-THC test, no per-serving mg cap, no statewide age minimum, no state retail licence as of 24 Oct 2026. S.L. 2022-32, §§1–3, eff. 30 June 2022: https://ncleg.gov/EnactedLegislation/SessionLaws/HTML/2021-2022/SL2022-32.html
H3 The state hemp pilot is dead; growers are USDA-only. The NC Industrial Hemp Pilot Program (former Ch. 106, Art. 50E) ended 31 Dec 2021; Art. 50E was repealed eff. 30 June 2022. From 1 Jan 2022 NC growers must hold a USDA Domestic Hemp Production Program licence. NCDA&CS “no longer offers registration services for hemp processors” and says it no longer has authority to license producers or processors. NCDA&CS, Hemp in NC: https://www.ncagr.gov/divisions/plant-industry/hemp-nc ; NCDA&CS press release 16 Aug 2021: https://www.ncagr.gov/news/press-releases/2021/08/16/nc-will-wrap-hemp-pilot-program-federal-regulatory-program-takes-effect ; NC State Extension 5 Jan 2022 (rev. 5 Nov 2024): https://hemp.ces.ncsu.edu/news/all-nc-hemp-growers-must-now-be-licensed-through-usda/ ; USDA plan list, updated 17 Feb 2026, lists NC as “USDA Hemp Producer License”: https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review
H4 HB 328 — the live hemp bill — is NOT law. HB 328, “Regulate Hemp-Derived Consumables” (filed 6 Mar 2025), passed the House 112–0 in its early form (16 Apr 2025, secondary report), was rewritten in the Senate, failed concurrence in the House 95–18 (21 Apr 2026, secondary report), went to conference, and the Senate adopted the conference report 37–6 on 2 July 2026. As of 24 Oct 2026 it sits re-referred to House Rules, Calendar, and Operations (30 July 2026) and has not had a final House vote, has not gone to the Governor, and is not enacted. Its conference form would move NC to a total-THC standard, cap finished products at 0.4 mg total THC per container, ban synthetic/chemically converted cannabinoids as hemp, and set age 21. Its own draft effective dates (15 July / 12 Nov 2026 in secondary summaries) have already slipped past in part — do not rely on them. UNC Legislative Reporting Service, H328 status “Re-ref Com On Rules… (Jul 30 2026)”: https://lrs.sog.unc.edu/comment/11092 ; HB 328 4th edition (Senate Health Care substitute, 17 June 2025) opened: https://www.ncleg.gov/Sessions/2025/Bills/House/PDF/H328v4.pdf ; conference-report summaries: https://raleighdispensaries.com/reports/december-11-triangle-hemp-shops ; https://www.donahuedefense.com/blog/nc-hemp-thc-law-changes-2026/ ; https://beardbrospharms.com/articles/north-carolina-lawmakers-rush-to-rewrite-hemp-rules-with-house-bill-328/ — conference text itself not opened in full [VERIFY]
H5 Other 2025–26 hemp bills are pending or dead, none enacted. HB 607 (Ch. 18D licensing, ALE, $15,000 manufacturer licence, 10 mg/100 mg caps as filed) — pending in House Rules since 1 Apr 2025, proposed 1 July 2026 date passed unenacted. SB 328 (age-21 only) — re-referred to House Rules 23 June 2025, no movement since. SB 265 (Dept. of Revenue licensing, $250–$15,000 fees, 21+, 75 mg serving discussion in secondary summaries) — failed crossover / dead for the session in secondary tracking. None of these creates a licence, age limit or cap today. HB 607 summary: https://lrs.sog.unc.edu/bill-summaries-lookup/H/607/2025-2026%20Session/H607 ; SB 265 / SB 328 tracking via https://www.cannabisregulations.ai/cannabis-and-hemp-regulations-compliance-ai-blog/north-carolina-thca-laws-licensing-2026 (secondary — bill-status dates cross-checked to LRS where available; see Notes)
H6 No state medical cannabis programme; Compassionate Care Act repeatedly Senate-passed, House-killed. SB 711 (2021–22) passed the Senate 35–10 on 6 June 2022 and died in the House; SB 3 (2023) passed the Senate, was re-referred in the House 18 May 2023 and died; HB 1011 (2025–26, House version, filed 15 Apr 2025) was referred to House Rules 16 Apr 2025 and has not moved. The 2025 House version omits the 15% tax in earlier Senate versions (secondary, MPP 22 Apr 2025). No medical licences, no patient registry for marijuana, no reciprocity. H7 Eastern Band of Cherokee Indians (EBCI) is the only lawful marijuana market in NC geography. Tribal referendum 7 Sept 2023 passed 2,464–1,057 (≈70%). Ord. No. 63 (2024) approved as amended 27 June 2024, signed by Principal Chief Michell Hicks same day. Great Smoky Cannabis Co. (Qualla Enterprises, LLC): medical sales 20 Apr 2024; tribal-member adult-use 4 July 2024; all adults 21+ from 7 Sept 2024. Products bought there remain illegal under NC law once they leave the Qualla Boundary. H9 Governor’s Advisory Council on Cannabis (Exec. Order No. 16, 3 June 2025 — Council letter says 3 June; secondary reports say 4 June [VERIFY date]) issued an Interim Report 2 Apr 2026 recommending legalising adult-use 21+ through licensed retail, with a total-THC standard, and estimating NC’s unregulated cannabis market at ~$3.2 billion/year (citing the 2023 U.S. Cannabis Report) plus an ~$1 billion intoxicating-hemp market (secondary summary of Council discussion). Governor Stein endorsed adult-use legalisation after the interim report. The Council has no law-making power; final report was due 31 Dec 2026. H10 Public opinion (secondary, dated): SurveyUSA for WRAL/WSOC, 7–11 Oct 2026, n=900 adults: 74% medical legalisation, 15% oppose; 63% adult-use legalisation, 26% oppose, 11% undecided (credibility interval ~±4.2 points). H11 Federal cliff — 11 Dec 2026. Even if HB 328 never passes, the federal hemp redefinition (PL 119-37 §781, as delayed by PL 119-103 §2019) applies in NC from 11 Dec 2026 under the fixed baseline: total-THC test + 0.4 mg per container cap. NC state law as it stands today would not shield a product that fails the new federal definition. Retailers and growers should plan on that date, not on any NC bill date. Secondary NC analyses agree on the practical effect (most THCA flower fails a post-decarboxylation total-THC test; no grandfather clause in the federal change as summarised). Fixed federal baseline (task); NC analysis: https://www.donahuedefense.com/blog/nc-hemp-thc-law-changes-2026/ ; https://www.cannabisregulations.ai/cannabis-and-hemp-regulations-compliance-ai-blog/north-carolina-thca-laws-licensing-2026
H12 No NC cannabis excise tax; hemp products pay ordinary sales tax. State sales tax 4.75% + local 2.00–2.75% (combined generally 6.75–7.50%; Wilmington/New Hanover 7.00% as an example). A proposed 10.5% hemp-derived consumable excise (H563, 2023–24, Art. 5K) was never enacted — its 1 July 2025 effective date in the bill text never took effect. No NC §280E decoupling statute was located. See Part 2. Sales-tax structure: https://taxfoundation.org/research/all/state/north-carolina-local-tax-revenue/ ; H563 fiscal note: https://www.ncleg.gov/Sessions/2023/FiscalNotes/House/PDF/HFN563v6.pdf

PART 1: LAWS

1.1 Legal status — in one paragraph, from statute

Recreational: ILLEGAL. Medical (marijuana): ILLEGAL — no programme. Low-THC/CBD hemp: LEGAL within the §90-87 definition. Hemp extract for intractable epilepsy: LEGAL only as an exemption from penalties, not as a sales programme.

Marijuana is “all parts of the plant of the genus Cannabis … but shall not include … hemp or hemp products” (G.S. 90-87(16), S.L. 2022-32) and is listed in Schedule VI, G.S. 90-94(1), with tetrahydrocannabinols in Schedule VI “except for tetrahydrocannabinols found in hemp or hemp products” (G.S. 90-94(2)). Possession, manufacture, sale/delivery and possession with intent are unlawful “[e]xcept as authorized by this Article” (G.S. 90-95(a)). No article of Chapter 90 authorises recreational or general medical marijuana sales, cultivation or possession. The only marijuana-adjacent state authorisation is the hemp-extract exemption, G.S. 90-94.1, and the former pilot-study articles (Art. 5G, §§90-113.100 et seq.) — neither creates a dispensary system. On EBCI trust land, tribal law — not NC law — authorises the Great Smoky programme (Part 4.3; full tribal law in Run 2, Part 10).

1.2 Every state statute and regulation on cannabis and hemp

Method note: The table below lists every Chapter 90 / related provision that actually governs cannabis or hemp and that could be confirmed from an opened official or library text. Former Art. 50E (Ch. 106) is included as repealed history because it explains the pilot’s end. General provisions that merely mention a controlled substance in passing (e.g. forfeiture, drug-free workplace) are noted in §1.2 note, not given full rows, to avoid inventing a cannabis-specific rule where none exists. Last-amended dates are the session-law dates shown on the opened text; where the ncleg library page for a section could not be opened in full, the date is given from the session law cited in that section’s history line and flagged.

Citation Subject Plain-English summary Verbatim operative text (operative portion only) Last amended / effective Official link (Accessed 2026-10-24)
G.S. 90-87(13a) “Hemp” defined Cannabis sativa (L.) and any part, incl. seeds, derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, if delta-9 THC ≤0.3% dry-weight. Anything above that is not hemp. “‘Hemp’ means the plant Cannabis sativa (L.) and any part of that plant, including the seeds thereof and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of not more than three-tenths of one percent (0.3%) on a dry weight basis.” Rewritten by S.L. 2022-32, eff. 30 June 2022 https://ncleg.gov/EnactedLegislation/SessionLaws/HTML/2021-2022/SL2022-32.html
G.S. 90-87(13b) “Hemp products” defined All products made from hemp — the list is non-exhaustive (cloth, cordage, fibre, food, fuel, paint, paper, particleboard, plastics, seed, seed meal and seed oil for consumption, verified propagules from hemp varieties). “‘Hemp products’ means all products made from hemp, including, but not limited to, cloth, cordage, fiber, food, fuel, paint, paper, particleboard, plastics, seed, seed meal and seed oil for consumption, and verified propagules for cultivation if the seeds originate from hemp varieties.” S.L. 2022-32, eff. 30 June 2022 Same as above
G.S. 90-87(16) “Marijuana” defined All parts of genus Cannabis, seeds, resin, compounds/manufactures/salts/derivatives/mixtures/preparations — excluding mature stalks, fibre from stalks, oil/cake from seeds, other stalk/seed compounds (except resin), sterilised seed incapable of germination, and excluding hemp or hemp products. “‘Marijuana’ means all parts of the plant of the genus Cannabis, whether growing or not; the seeds thereof; the resin extracted from any part of such plant; and every compound, manufacture, salt, derivative, mixture, or preparation of such plant, its seeds or resin, but shall not include the mature stalks of such plant, fiber produced from such stalks, oil, or cake made from the seeds of such plant, any other compound, manufacture, salt, derivative, mixture, or preparation of such mature stalks (except the resin extracted therefrom), fiber, oil, or cake, or the sterilized seed of such plant which is incapable of germination. The term does not include hemp or hemp products.” S.L. 2022-32, eff. 30 June 2022 Same as above
G.S. 90-88, 90-89, 90-90, 90-91, 90-92, 90-93 Schedules I–V Marijuana is not in Schedules I–V under NC law; it is Schedule VI only. These sections matter only to show what marijuana is not — and to locate the Schedule III provision that the federal split-scheduling debate sometimes confuses with NC law. No NC schedule change following the federal 2026 Schedule III order was located [VERIFY — see Notes]. — (no cannabis-specific operative text; schedules list other substances) Various; no 2026 cannabis amendment located ncleg library, Ch. 90 Art. 5 — section pages not individually opened [VERIFY]
G.S. 90-94 Schedule VI The NC schedule for marijuana and (non-hemp) THC. Commission must find no currently accepted medical use in the US, or relatively low abuse potential / need for further study. Lists (1) Marijuana, (2) Tetrahydrocannabinols except hemp THC. Former (3) repealed by S.L. 2017-115, s. 8, eff. 1 Dec 2017. “This schedule includes the controlled substances listed… The following controlled substances are included in this schedule: (1) Marijuana. (2) Tetrahydrocannabinols, except for tetrahydrocannabinols found in hemp or hemp products.” (plus the Commission-findings preamble quoted in S.L. 2022-32) S.L. 2022-32, eff. 30 June 2022 https://ncleg.gov/EnactedLegislation/SessionLaws/HTML/2021-2022/SL2022-32.html
G.S. 90-94.1 Hemp-extract exemption See §1.4. Not a licence, not a sales programme. See §1.4 verbatim. History line: (2014-53, s. 3; 2015-154, s. 1; 2018-36, s. 1) — i.e. last substantive amendment 2018 https://law.justia.com/codes/north-carolina/chapter-90/article-5/section-90-94-1/ (text mirror; official library PDF not opened — [VERIFY] pinpoint)
G.S. 90-95 Violations; penalties The criminal core. See §1.3 table. Manufacture/sell/deliver/possess with intent, counterfeit, and simple possession are unlawful except as authorised. Schedule VI sale/manufacture is a Class I felony (sale of Sch. III–V is Class H; Sch. VI manufacture/sale provisions are set out in (b) — see note on the 2025 bill draft that would have rewritten (b); that draft is NOT law). Trafficking in marijuana at >10 lb, G.S. 90-95(h)(1). Transfer of <5 g marijuana for no remuneration is not “delivery”. “Except as authorized by this Article, it is unlawful for any person: (1) To manufacture, sell or deliver, or possess with intent to manufacture, sell or deliver, a controlled substance; (2) To create, sell or deliver, or possess with intent to sell or deliver, a counterfeit controlled substance; (3) To possess a controlled substance.” Long history; trafficking tiers and possession tiers as summarised in §1.3 are the current text as reproduced in multiple secondary analyses of G.S. 90-95(d) and (h) — full current ncleg text of 90-95(d)/(h) not opened line-by-line in Run 1 [VERIFY]; do not cite a pinpoint sentence beyond the (a) text above without checking the library PDF in Run 2. ncleg library — https://library.ncleg.gov/ (search G.S. 90-95) — direct section PDF not opened

G.S. 90-113.22A Possession of marijuana drug paraphernalia Knowing use / possession with intent to use paraphernalia to plant/propagate/cultivate/grow/harvest/manufacture/compound/convert/produce/process/prepare/test/analyse/package/repackage/store/contain/conceal marijuana, or to introduce it into the body, is a Class 3 misdemeanour. Testing-equipment exception for persons who use / intend to use, and for harm-reduction organisations. “It is unlawful for any person to knowingly use, or to possess with intent to use, drug paraphernalia to plant, propagate, cultivate, grow, harvest, manufacture, compound, convert, produce, process, prepare, test, analyze, package, repackage, store, contain, or conceal marijuana or to inject, ingest, inhale, or otherwise introduce marijuana into the body.” … “A violation of this section is a Class 3 misdemeanor.” History: (2014-119, s. 3(b); 2019-159, s. 2.2) https://library.ncleg.gov/EnactedLegislation/Statutes/PDF/BySection/Chapter_90/GS_90-113.22A.pdf (library PDF via search result; opened via search snippet — full PDF not paginated in Run 1, text confirmed verbatim)

Definitions: caregiver, caregiver registration card, database, Department (DHHS), hemp extract (by cross-reference to §90-94.1(a)), intractable epilepsy (seizure disorder not responding to ≥3 treatment options as determined by a neurologist), neurologist (licensed, board-certified, affiliated with UNC-Chapel Hill, ECU, Duke or Wake Forest neurology departments in the original text — later amendments broadened hospital affiliation [VERIFY current text]), patient, pilot study. DHHS must create a secure electronic online registry accessible to law enforcement to verify caregiver registration and preventing duplicate active patient registration by multiple neurologists. Note on “every” statute: NC has no freestanding “Cannabis Act”, no medical-cannabis chapter, no adult-use chapter, and no hemp-retail chapter in force. The list above is the operative state cannabis/hemp code in substance: Ch. 90 Art. 5 (definitions, Schedule VI, penalties, hemp-extract exemption, paraphernalia, Art. 5G registry) plus the repealed Ch. 106 Art. 50E history, plus tax Chapter 105 (Part 2) and the general provisions cross-referenced in §§1.3/1.8. If a reader finds a cannabis rule elsewhere (e.g. a local ordinance, a food-safety rule applied to a hemp food, or a federal rule), it is not a state cannabis statute and is treated as such in Parts 3 and 11.

1.3 Possession, cultivation, paraphernalia, public consumption, product types

Possession — marijuana (G.S. 90-95(d)(4) as summarised from the current statute; pinpoint text [VERIFY] as noted in §1.2):

>1.5 oz (and any amount of hashish above its own thresholds — see below) Class I felony Presumptive 3–4 months minimum to 13–14 months maximum at lowest prior-record level in secondary sentencing summaries; other summaries give 3–12 months depending on record — range conflict [VERIFY against G.S. 15A-1340.17 grid] Same; conflict noted in Interim Notes
Hashish (extracted resin): >1/20 oz Class 1 misdemeanour As above Same (via H766 draft restating prior law: https://webservices.ncleg.gov/ViewBillDocument/2019/3347/0/DRH30336-ML-123)
Hashish: >3/20 oz; or any quantity of synthetic THC / THC isolated from resin Class I felony As above Same
>10 lb Trafficking in marijuana, G.S. 90-95(h)(1) — mandatory minimums, judge has no discretion to suspend below the minimum See trafficking table below Same

Trafficking — marijuana, G.S. 90-95(h)(1):

Weight Felony class Mandatory minimum–maximum prison (secondary sentencing summaries) Minimum fine
>10 lb to <50 lb Class H 25–39 months $5,000
50 lb to <2,000 lb Class G 35–51 months $25,000
2,000 lb to <10,000 lb Class F 70–93 months $50,000
≥10,000 lb Class D 175–222 months $200,000

Source for trafficking tiers: https://legalclarity.org/drug-trafficking-in-north-carolina-laws-and-penalties/ and https://sativauniversity.com/learn/north-carolina-marijuana/ (secondary; statute pinpoint [VERIFY]).

Sale / manufacture / possession with intent — marijuana (Schedule VI): G.S. 90-95(a)(1) makes manufacture, sale/delivery, or possession with intent to manufacture/sell/deliver unlawful except as authorised. The Schedule VI punishment in G.S. 90-95(b) is a Class I felony for manufacture/sale/delivery offences (with the <5 g no-remuneration transfer exception: “The transfer of less than 5 grams of marijuana for no remuneration shall not constitute a delivery” — text as reproduced in the 2025 S937 draft restating §90-95(b); current pinpoint [VERIFY]). Manufacturing within 1,000 feet of a school/child-care centre by a person 21+ is elevated to a Class E felony in secondary summaries of §90-95 — [VERIFY pinpoint] before relying on it. Cultivation: there is no home-grow allowance, no plant-count allowance, and no medical cultivation allowance under state law. Any cultivation of marijuana (as opposed to USDA-licensed hemp) is “manufacture” under §90-95(a)(1) and is charged on weight, not plant count, in the tiers above. Prior drug convictions can enhance a Class 3 to Class 2 and a Class 1 to Class I felony in secondary summaries — [VERIFY].

Paraphernalia: §90-113.22A (marijuana-specific) = Class 3 misdemeanour (verbatim in §1.2). General paraphernalia (§90-113.22) = Class 1 misdemeanour in secondary summaries; sale/delivery of paraphernalia to a minor ≥3 years younger = Class I felony in secondary summaries [VERIFY]. The testing-equipment exception in §90-113.22A(c) protects drug-checking equipment for persons who use/intend to use and for harm-reduction organisations — it does not legalise marijuana paraphernalia generally.

Public consumption: No state statute authorises public consumption of marijuana. Because possession itself is unlawful except as authorised, public use is unlawful on the same basis, and is additionally exposed to local ordinances, park rules, and (on federal land, e.g. Great Smoky Mountains National Park adjoining the Qualla Boundary) federal law. No state consumption-lounge, on-premises-consumption or “social consumption” licence exists. On the Qualla Boundary, tribal law governs consumption on tribal land (Run 2, Part 10/12); off tribal land, NC law applies in full. Smoking/vaping of hemp products in public is not separately banned by a state hemp statute — it is governed by generally applicable smoking rules (e.g. workplace / public-building smoking restrictions, local ordinances) and by the product’s THC status: if the product is in fact marijuana (>0.3% delta-9), §90-95 applies regardless of how it is labelled.

Product types — what is legal under state law today (hemp only):

Product type State-law status today Why / caveat
Hemp flower (≤0.3% delta-9, dry weight) Legal as hemp / hemp product Includes THCA-rich flower that tests ≤0.3% delta-9 before decarboxylation — this is the exact product the federal total-THC change from 11 Dec 2026 will remove from federal hemp protection. State law alone does not ban it today.
Hemp edibles, beverages, tinctures, topicals (≤0.3% delta-9) Legal as hemp products, unregulated as to potency/serving at state level No state mg/serving cap, no state testing/labelling mandate for hemp consumables today. Federal food/drug law (FDA) still applies to health claims, food adulteration and dietary-supplement marketing — see UNC SOG hemp deck.
Delta-8, delta-10, HHC, THC-O and other hemp-derived / converted cannabinoids, if in a product ≤0.3% delta-9 Not expressly banned by NC statute today; treated by UNC SOG (Dixon, Winter 2026, via secondary summary 14 Jan 2026) as remaining legal as a matter of state law This is the most litigated ambiguity in NC hemp law. The exclusion in §90-94(2) is for “tetrahydrocannabinols found in hemp or hemp products” — it does not list delta-8 separately. No NC appellate decision or AG opinion squarely holding delta-8 legal/illegal was located in Run 1 [UNAVAILABLE — see Notes]. HB 328 conference form would ban synthetic/converted cannabinoids as hemp — PROPOSED, not law.
Marijuana flower, edibles, vapes, concentrates, beverages (>0.3% delta-9, or synthetic THC in any quantity) Illegal — Schedule VI; possession/sale/manufacture under §90-95; concentrates/hashish thresholds above apply Labelling a marijuana product “hemp”, “THCA” or “CBD” does not change its legal status if it exceeds the delta-9 limit or is synthetic THC.
FDA-approved cannabis-derived drugs (e.g. Epidiolex, Marinol/Syndros, Cesamet as scheduled federally) Lawful only as prescription drugs under their own federal/state scheduling provisions, not as “medical cannabis” S.L. 2022-era Schedule VI amendment for FDA-approved drugs (SB 448, 2022, secondary Lexology summary) — pinpoint [VERIFY]; not a general medical programme.
Hemp extract for epilepsy (§90-94.1) Legal to possess/use/administer only if all §90-94.1(b) criteria are met (see §1.4) No lawful in-state sale channel is created by §90-94.1.

1.4 Medical — Epilepsy Alternative Treatment Act, and broader medical legislation

A. The Act as enacted.

  • Citation / history: Article 5G, Chapter 90, §§90-113.100–90-113.106, enacted by H1220, S.L. 2014-53 (“Hope 4 Haley and Friends”), 3 July 2014; §90-94.1 enacted by §3 of the same act, effective when DHHS rules under §4 were adopted (not 1 Oct 2014 as first drafted). Amended by H766, S.L. 2015-154 (2015), which (i) raised the THC ceiling from 0.3% to 0.9% and lowered the CBD floor from 10% to 5%, and (ii) allowed use without participating in a pilot study, and (iii) in its long title contemplated repeal in 2021 — a repeal that did not take effect for §90-94.1 as it stands today (the section remains in the current Code; the exact disposition of Art. 5G’s pilot-study provisions after 2021 is [VERIFY] — see Notes).
  • Purpose (§90-113.100): “to permit medical professionals to conduct limited-scope, evidence-based studies exploring the safety and efficacy of treating intractable epilepsy using hemp extract.”
  • Who qualifies — patient: “A person who has been diagnosed by a neurologist with intractable epilepsy” (§90-113.101, H1220 text). Intractable epilepsy = “A seizure disorder that, as determined by a neurologist, does not respond to three or more treatment options overseen by the neurologist.”
  • Who qualifies — neurologist: In H1220, an individual licensed under Ch. 90 Art. 1, board-certified in neurology, and affiliated with the neurology department at UNC-Chapel Hill, East Carolina University, Duke University, or Wake Forest University. H766 drafts broadened this to hospitals licensed in NC — current text [VERIFY]; do not advise a patient that any board-certified neurologist qualifies until the current §90-113.101(g) is opened.
  • Who qualifies — caregiver: “An individual who is a parent, legal guardian, or custodian of a person diagnosed with intractable epilepsy” (§90-113.101(a)). Only a caregiver (as defined) may hold the registration card and administer under §90-94.1(c); the patient alone is not the cardholder in the statutory scheme as enacted.
  • Agency: NC Department of Health and Human Services (NCDHHS / “Department”). DHHS must create “a secure, electronic, and online Intractable Epilepsy Alternative Treatment Pilot Study database registry for the registration of pilot studies, neurologists, caregivers, and patients” (§90-113.102(a)), accessible to law enforcement to verify caregiver registration, preventing duplicate active patient registration by multiple neurologists, containing at minimum: caregiver name/address + pilot-study name; patient name/address + pilot-study name; neurologist name/address/qualifying affiliation; pilot-study name/affiliation/affiliated neurologists/parameters. DHHS must also contact the county health department where the patient resides with specified information (§90-113.102(b) as drafted).
  • THC/CBD limits — hemp extract (§90-94.1(a), verbatim):

“As used in this section, ‘hemp extract’ means an extract from a cannabis plant, or a mixture or preparation containing cannabis plant material, that has all of the following characteristics: (1) Is composed of less than nine-tenths of one percent (0.9%) tetrahydrocannabinol by weight. (2) Is composed of at least five percent (5%) cannabidiol by weight. (3) Contains no other psychoactive substance.”

Note the measurement difference: hemp extract is by weight (not dry-weight delta-9), and its 0.9% ceiling is higher than the 0.3% hemp ceiling — a product can be lawful hemp extract for a registered caregiver yet be marijuana for everyone else. Do not conflate the two tests.

  • How registration / lawful possession works (§90-94.1(b)–(d), verbatim in substance):

“(b) Notwithstanding any other provision of this Chapter, an individual may possess or use hemp extract, and is not subject to the penalties described in this Chapter, if the individual satisfies all of the following criteria: (1) Possesses or uses the hemp extract only to treat intractable epilepsy, as defined in G.S. 90-113.101. (2) Possesses, in close proximity to the hemp extract, a certificate of analysis that indicates the hemp extract’s ingredients, including its percentages of tetrahydrocannabinol and cannabidiol by weight. (3) Is a caregiver, as defined in G.S. 90-113.101. (c) Notwithstanding any other provision of this Chapter, an individual who possesses hemp extract lawfully under this section may administer hemp extract to another person under the individual’s care and is not subject to the penalties described in this Chapter for administering the hemp extract to the person if the individual is the person’s caregiver, as defined in G.S. 90-113.101. (d) Any individual who possesses or uses hemp extract, as defined under this section, shall dispose of all residual oil from the extract at a secure collection box managed by a law enforcement agency. No criminal penalty shall attach for any violation of this subsection.”

In practice (secondary summaries, consistent with the statute): the caregiver applies to DHHS for a Caregiver Registration Card; the card + certificate of analysis kept in close proximity are the proof that possession is exempt. No possession quantity limit is stated in §90-94.1 — no ounce/gram cap was located [UNAVAILABLE — confirm no cap exists elsewhere in Art. 5G]. No in-state lawful purchase channel is created — the Act is a possession/use exemption, not a supply system; secondary sources uniformly note patients/caregivers must obtain extract outside NC. That gap is a finding, not an oversight in this report.

  • ⚠ Repeal conflict — read before relying on Art. 5G: Secondary summaries (NORML; EBCI sub-research, Accessed 2026-10-24) state Art. 5G was repealed effective 1 July 2021, while §90-94.1 itself remains in the current Code. H766's long title did contemplate “repealing the Epilepsy Alternative Treatment Act in 2021”. The current status of the DHHS registry / caregiver-card system described above is therefore [VERIFY] — it is possible the possession exemption in §90-94.1 survives while the pilot-study registry machinery does not, or that the registry continues in practice. Do not advise a patient that a DHHS card is currently issuable until the current §§90-113.100 et seq. library text is opened. This conflict is carried as Gap R1-01.
  • Registry statistics: No current DHHS-published count of registered caregivers/patients under Art. 5G was located in Run 1. Counts: [UNAVAILABLE] — see Part 5. Do not quote a registry size.
  • Conditions covered: Intractable epilepsy only. No other condition, no chronic pain, PTSD, cancer, etc., under state law.
  • Reciprocity: None. No NC statute recognises an out-of-state medical cannabis card, an EBCI medical card, or a visiting-patient status for state-law purposes. An out-of-state cardholder possessing marijuana in NC (outside tribal land) is subject to §90-95 in full. EBCI medical cards are tribal-law instruments for use on tribal land only (Run 2, Part 10).

B. Broader medical cannabis legislation — status (all PROPOSED / DEAD unless stated).

Bottom line for §1.4: As of 24 Oct 2026, a North Carolinian with cancer, PTSD, chronic pain or any condition other than intractable epilepsy has no state-law right to possess, buy or use marijuana, and no state registry to join. The epilepsy exemption is the whole of state medical law.

1.5 Hemp — limits, measurement, intoxicating products, age, retail, testing, labelling, enforcement, executive orders, federal response

THC limit and measurement (current law):

  • Limit: delta-9 THC ≤0.3% on a dry-weight basis (G.S. 90-87(13a)). For a finished product, the statute’s dry-weight language is applied to the hemp / hemp product as sold — secondary UNC SOG material notes there is no state rule prescribing a product-level dry-weight calculation for edibles/beverages, which is part of why enforcement is difficult. Do not invent a “per-serving” or “per-container” NC limit: none exists in current statute.
  • What is NOT measured in NC today: THCA (no 0.877 decarboxylation factor), delta-8, delta-10, total THC, or mg per container. A product with 20% THCA and 0.2% delta-9 is hemp under NC’s delta-9 test today and marijuana under a total-THC test tomorrow. That single fact drives the entire 11 Dec 2026 cliff.
  • Hemp vs hemp extract vs marijuana — three different tests, do not merge them: (1) Hemp: ≤0.3% delta-9, dry weight (§90-87). (2) Hemp extract (epilepsy): <0.9% THC by weight, ≥5% CBD by weight, no other psychoactive substance (§90-94.1). (3) Marijuana: everything else in genus Cannabis (§90-87(16)).

Delta-8 / delta-9 / THCA / other intoxicating hemp:

  • Delta-9 THC in hemp products: lawful if the product is hemp (≤0.3% delta-9 dry weight). Because 0.3% of a large edible/beverage can be many milligrams of delta-9 (e.g. 0.3% of a 100 g gummy pack ≈ 300 mg in arithmetic — illustrative only, not a statutory serving), NC today permits high-milligram delta-9 edibles/beverages that would exceed other states’ per-serving caps. No NC cap prevents this today.
  • Delta-8, delta-10, THCA, HHC, THC-O, etc.: No NC statute names them as banned or as expressly permitted. The §90-94(2) exclusion (“tetrahydrocannabinols found in hemp or hemp products”) is the textual basis for the “legal as a matter of state law” reading (UNC SOG Dixon, Winter 2026, via secondary summary 14 Jan 2026: “state law in this area has not changed and all the hemp products discussed above remain legal as a matter of state law”). Counter-risk: (a) synthetic / chemically converted cannabinoids may not be “found in hemp” in the ordinary sense — the federal and HB 328-conference positions treat them differently; (b) a product that is in fact >0.3% delta-9, or synthetic THC in any quantity for possession purposes (§90-95(d) hashish/synthetic tier), is marijuana regardless of label; (c) FDA food/drug law and general consumer-protection law still apply. No NC court or AG opinion resolving delta-8 was located [UNAVAILABLE].
  • THCA flower: lawful under the delta-9 test today; the subject of the federal change. See H11.
  • THC drinks and edibles: lawful as hemp products today if ≤0.3% delta-9; no state potency, serving, container, or age rule; no state requirement that a drink be sold only in a licensed premises. Retailers’ own 21+ policies are voluntary.
  • Kratom, xylazine: HB 328 also addresses kratom regulation and adding xylazine as Schedule III — PROPOSED, in HB 328 only, not current law. Do not treat kratom as a hemp product.

Minimum age (current law): NONE statewide for hemp products. No NC statute sets 18 or 21 for purchase or possession of hemp/CBD/intoxicating hemp products. Tobacco/alcohol age laws (18 for tobacco in NC statute as historically, 21 federally for tobacco; 21 for alcohol) do not apply to hemp by their terms. School-grounds bans for hemp consumables exist only in proposed bills (SB 265 / HB 328). Retailers commonly card at 21 voluntarily — that is policy, not law.

Retail licensing / registration (current law): NONE for hemp retail or hemp manufacturing at state level. See Part 4.1. A seller needs only generally applicable business registrations (Secretary of State entity, NCDOR sales-tax registration, local privilege/business registration where it still exists — see Part 3.4), not a hemp licence. No state hemp retailer list exists (Part 5).

Testing and labelling (current law): NONE state-mandated for hemp retail products. No NC statute or NCAC rule requires: COAs, QR codes, batch testing, ISO 17025 / DEA-registered lab testing for retail hemp, contaminant (pesticide/heavy-metal/microbial/solvent) limits, or specific label warnings for hemp consumables. Cultivation testing is USDA-programme testing (sampling agent + DEA-registered lab under the federal programme, with USDA enforcement delayed to 31 Dec 2026 per the fixed baseline — Run 2, Part 11). For retail, any COA a shop shows is voluntary / market practice. The NC Board of Pharmacy’s “Current Guidance to North Carolina Pharmacies on the Sale of Products Derived from Hemp” (secondary mirror via Docest) notes only that health claims are regulated like other product claims and that marketing a hemp product as a drug (diagnosis/cure/mitigation/treatment/prevention) without FDA approval is an unapproved-drug claim — that guidance is pharmacy guidance, not a hemp labelling statute, and its official Board page was not opened [VERIFY].

Enforcement by ALE or others (current law):

  • Alcohol Law Enforcement (ALE) Division, Dept. of Public Safety: ALE has no current statutory authority over hemp retail — its hemp role exists only in proposed Chapter 18D bills (licensing, inspections, civil penalties). ALE’s current authority is alcohol/tobacco/controlled-substance enforcement in its general remit; a hemp product that is in fact marijuana is a controlled-substance matter for ALE, SBI, local law enforcement and prosecutors on the same basis as any marijuana case. Attorney General Jeff Jackson has publicly supported hemp regulation (June 2025 support for a state bill banning some hemp products / 21+ for THC drinks and gummies, per NCDOJ release 27 Oct 2025 urging Congress to act on hemp-derived THC and children: https://ncdoj.gov/attorney-general-jeff-jackson-urges-congress-to-keep-hemp-derived-thc-products-away-from-kids/) — support is not enforcement authority, and no AG opinion creating a hemp ban was located.
  • NCDA&CS: No longer licenses or registers hemp producers/processors (see H3). Its remaining hemp-adjacent roles are general agriculture / food-safety functions, not hemp-retail enforcement.
  • NCDHHS / DHHS: Epilepsy registry only (Art. 5G); no hemp-retail regulator.
  • Local law enforcement / district attorneys: Marijuana enforcement (including hemp/marijuana distinction by lab test) is the practical enforcement channel today. The lab-test problem — field tests that cannot distinguish hemp from marijuana, and the need for quantitative delta-9 testing — is repeatedly noted in UNC SOG material and is a real enforcement constraint, but no NC statute creates a hemp-specific enforcement procedure.
  • No published ALE hemp-compliance dataset was located — see Part 5 [UNAVAILABLE].

Executive orders:

  • Exec. Order No. 16 (Gov. Josh Stein, 3 June 2025 per Council letter; 4 June 2025 in secondary reports [VERIFY date]) — creates the Advisory Council on Cannabis, charges it with studying a comprehensive statewide approach, interim recommendations and a final report due 31 Dec 2026. An executive order creating an advisory council does not change any statute, create any licence, ban any product, or set any age limit. No other NC executive order on hemp/cannabis was located in Run 1 [UNAVAILABLE — confirm via governor.nc.gov executive-order index in Run 2].
  • No NC executive order responding to PL 119-37 / PL 119-103, or setting a state position on the 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 federal dates, was located in Run 1. State response to the new federal definition: [UNAVAILABLE] as an official state position. The only state-level responses in the record are (a) the pending HB 328 conference form, which would adopt a total-THC + 0.4 mg standard in state law if enacted, and (b) the Advisory Council interim report’s recommendation of a total-THC standard — neither is law. NCDA&CS, NCDOR, ALE and the Governor’s office pages opened for this run contain no 2026 federal-hemp-definition guidance [UNAVAILABLE — re-check in Run 2 immediately before 11 Dec 2026].

1.6 Packaging, labelling, advertising, delivery, zoning and buffers — hemp products and smoke/vape shops

Current state law: essentially none hemp-specific. The table states the negative clearly so it is not mistaken for an omission:

Topic Current NC state rule for hemp / smoke-vape shops PROPOSED (not law)
Packaging (child-resistant, opaque exit package) None state-mandated for hemp. General consumer-product safety law applies, but no hemp child-resistant statute. HB 328 4th ed. §18D-101(a)(5): sale only in exit package / child-proof package; HB 607: child-resistant packaging (see §1.9)
Labelling (potency, ingredients, warnings, QR/COA) None state-mandated for hemp retail. FDA labelling / health-claim law applies generally. HB 328 4th ed. §18D-105 (compliance section referenced in §18D-101(a)(6)); HB 607: QR-linked COA from accredited lab
Advertising (health claims, youth appeal, cartoon shapes) No hemp-specific advertising statute. General deceptive-trade-practices law (Ch. 75) and FDA drug-claim law apply. Pharmacy guidance (above) warns against disease claims. SB 265 / HB 328 / HB 607: bans on cartoon-like shapes/characters appealing to children, sample distribution in public places, etc. — PROPOSED
Delivery / shipping / online sale No NC hemp-specific delivery ban, age-verification-at-delivery rule, or signature rule. Hemp may be shipped into/out of NC if it meets the federal/state hemp definition (USPS Pub. 52 hemp mailability is federal, Run 2 Part 10). Remote sellers have no NC hemp licence to obtain. HB 328 4th ed. §18D-101(a)(1): internet sellers must verify age + use delivery requiring signature of a person ≥21 — PROPOSED
Zoning / buffers (state) No state zoning or buffer rule for hemp/CBD/smoke-vape shops. Zoning is local (Ch. 160D) — see Part 3. No state 400-ft / 1,000-ft hemp-shop buffer exists (contrast: the 1,000-ft school/child-care elevation for marijuana manufacture in §90-95 secondary summaries is a criminal-sentencing elevation, not a shop-zoning buffer [VERIFY]). HB 328 / HB 607 contain no state shop-zoning buffer in the versions summarised; local zoning would remain local even if they passed [VERIFY conference text]
Smoke/vape shop licensing (state) No state smoke-shop or vape-shop licence for hemp. Tobacco retailer licensing is a separate regime (see Part 3.4 note on tobacco/ENDS reporting to NCDOR under the PACT Act notice — that notice concerns ENDS/tobacco reporting, not a hemp-shop licence). None located

Practical consequence: A hemp shop’s packaging, labelling, advertising and delivery practices today are governed by (i) generally applicable law (consumer protection, FDA, tobacco/ENDS law where a product is also an ENDS product, local zoning/licensing), and (ii) voluntary industry practice — not by a hemp code. Any compliance checklist that cites “NC hemp packaging rules” as current law is wrong.

1.7 Seed-to-sale / product tracking

  • State hemp/marijuana: NONE. NC has no Metrc or other seed-to-sale system, no state track-and-trace statute, and no state requirement to report hemp inventory, transfers or sales to a state system. USDA hemp growers report acreage to FSA and lots/testing under the federal programme (Run 2, Part 11), but that is federal cultivation reporting, not NC retail seed-to-sale.
  • Tribal (EBCI): The Great Smoky operation is described in tribal/enterprise sources as seed-to-sale tracked (cultivation → processing → testing → retail) under Cannabis Control Board oversight (Visit Smokies / One Feather summaries). The tribal tracking system’s name, vendor and data fields were not located in an official tribal source in Run 1 — [VERIFY in Run 2, Part 10/11]; do not name a vendor (e.g. Metrc) for EBCI without a tribal source.

1.8 Employment and drug testing protections

Bottom line: No cannabis employment protection in NC.

  • No statute protects a worker for marijuana use, for hemp-product use, for holding an EBCI medical card, or for holding an out-of-state medical card. Employers may maintain drug-free workplace policies and may refuse to hire or terminate for a confirmed positive THC test under their written policy (secondary employment-law summaries, consistent with the absence of any protective statute).
  • Lawful-products law, G.S. 95-28.2: Prohibits employers from refusing to hire / discharging because an employee uses a lawful product off-duty during non-working hours (tobacco is the classic example). Marijuana is not a “lawful product” for this purpose because it remains illegal under federal and NC state law — secondary Catapult HR summary: https://letscatapult.org/blog/north-carolinas-lawful-use-of-lawful-products-law/ . Hemp products that are lawful to buy do not thereby gain §95-28.2 protection in any located authority, and HB 607 as filed expressly preserved employer authority (secondary LegalClarity summary, noting HB 607 “does not limit an employer’s ability to maintain a drug-free workplace policy” — HB 607 is PROPOSED; the preservation clause is not law, but it confirms the current-law baseline that no protection exists).
  • Controlled Substance Examination Regulation Act (CSERA), G.S. 95-230 et seq.: Regulates how testing is done, not whether an employer may test for THC. Requirements in secondary summaries: approved laboratory (DHHS-certified / state-approved), screening positives confirmed by GC/MS or equally reliable method, confirmed samples retained ≥90 days for retest at the examinee’s expense, written notice of positive result and retest rights, Department of Labor enforcement with civil penalties for procedural violations (G.S. 95-234). CSERA text itself not opened line-by-line [VERIFY] — do not cite a CSERA subsection number beyond the chapter range without opening it.
  • Safety-sensitive / federal: DOT, FMCSA Clearinghouse and federal-contractor testing continue independently of NC law. Tribal cannabis employment on the Qualla Boundary is governed by tribal / enterprise policy, not by NC employment protections — and tribal employment does not create NC-law protection off tribal land.

1.9 Pending legislation — bill numbers, summaries, status on ncleg.gov (or LRS where ncleg tracker not opened)

Status key: ENACTED = session law; PENDING = still alive in the 2025–26 biennium (can still move in a reconvened session); DEAD = failed crossover / died at end of a prior session / no path in this biennium as tracked. Where status rests on a secondary tracker rather than an opened ncleg BillLookUp page, that is stated.

Bill (session) Short title / summary Status at 24 Oct 2026 What it would do if enacted (PROPOSED only)

(b) No NC General Assembly bill enacted or pending in 2025–26 that would ban, tax, license or otherwise regulate the EBCI programme was located in Run 1 — NC has no jurisdiction to license tribal activity on trust land in any event, and no such bill appears in the LRS hemp/medical lists checked. Any claim of a “NC bill to shut down Cherokee” in 2025–26 is [UNAVAILABLE — no bill number located]. (c) Congressional threats (Rep. A note on “crossover”: NC’s biennial session means a bill that fails crossover in the first year can still be revived in the second year only if it remains eligible under the crossover rule or is incorporated into another eligible bill (as HB 328’s conference process shows). “Dead” above therefore means “no live vehicle as tracked”, not “constitutionally impossible to revive” — the HB 328 conference history is the proof that a bill can return after a non-concurrence vote.

PART 2: TAXES

2.1 State sales tax — hemp/CBD/THC products

  • General rate: 4.75% — G.S. 105-164.4(a): “The general rate of tax is four and three-quarters percent (4.75%)” on each article of tangible personal property sold at retail not taxed under another subdivision (secondary Justia mirror of the statute; NCDOR current-rates page returned 404 on a sub-research open attempt — rate itself is uncontroversial and corroborated by Tax Foundation, NCDOR releases and every secondary guide, but the NCDOR table URL is [VERIFY]). Source: https://law.justia.com/codes/north-carolina/2022/chapter-105/article-5/section-105-164-4/ ; https://taxfoundation.org/research/all/state/north-carolina-local-tax-revenue/ (Accessed 2026-10-24).
  • Treatment of hemp/CBD/THC products: No hemp-specific NCDOR ruling, exemption or classification was located. By default, hemp/CBD/hemp-derived THC products sold at retail are tangible personal property taxed at the general + local rate. That is the secondary-guide consensus (Cannabis CPA Tax Guide, updated 1 July 2025: “normal state and local sales taxes apply to hemp-derived products”: https://cannabiscpa.tax/north-carolina-cannabis-tax-guide-cbd-only-2025-edition/) and the only position consistent with the absence of a hemp exemption in Chapter 105. Mark the “no special ruling” point [VERIFY] against an NCDOR private-letter-ruling search in Run 2 — absence from search is not proof no ruling exists.
  • Food vs dietary-supplement split (material, [VERIFY] for hemp): Qualifying food for home consumption is exempt from the 4.75% state rate and taxed at only the 2% local food rate (Chapter 105, Art. 39/40 food provisions). Dietary supplements are expressly excluded from “qualifying food” (17 NCAC 07B .2201, via secondary Justia mirror: https://regulations.justia.com/states/north-carolina/title-17/chapter-07/subchapter-b/section-2200/section-07b-2201/). Practical consequence: a CBD product marketed/labelled as a dietary supplement does not get the 2% food rate; it pays the full combined rate. A hemp food that genuinely qualifies as food (not a supplement, not prepared food, candy or a soft drink) would need a product-by-product analysis — no NCDOR hemp-food classification was located.
  • Prescription drugs are exempt from sales tax; non-prescription drugs pay 4.75% + local. No hemp product is a prescription drug except an FDA-approved drug dispensed as such (see Part 1.3).
  • No cannabis sales-tax exemption for medical use exists, because no state medical programme exists.

2.2 Local sales taxes

  • Structure (Chapter 105, Arts. 39, 40, 42, 43, 46): All 100 counties levy Arts. 39 (1%), 40 (0.50%) and 42 (0.50%) = 2.00% base local. Art. 43 (transit, 0.25% or 0.50%) is levied in a small number of counties; Art. 46 (0.25%) in 46 counties (2024 academic table: Afonso/Combs/Buerger, Plugging the Tax Leak, 2024: https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/0160323X231215057). Distribution is partly point-of-sale, partly per-capita, with school-capital earmarks (30% of Art. 40, 60% of Art. 42) — detail beyond the combined rate is not needed for a hemp retailer’s till, but matters for revenue analysis.
  • Combined range — UPDATED for July 2026: Pre-July 2026 combined rates were generally 6.75%–7.50% (4.75% + 2.00–2.75%). From 1 July 2026, Mecklenburg County (Charlotte) added 1% (voter-approved 4 Nov 2025, for roads/transit), taking its combined rate to 8.25% (NCDOR press release 24 June 2026, via search snippet — press-release URL [VERIFY] on open: https://www.ncdor.gov/news/press-releases/2026/06/24/mecklenburg-county-impose-additional-one-percent-sales-tax-beginning-july-1). Examples (secondary, Oct 2026): Wilmington / New Hanover 7.00% (4.75% + 2.25%): https://www.salestaxhandbook.com/north-carolina/rates/wilmington ; Wake 7.25%, Durham/Orange 7.50% in sub-research summaries [VERIFY against NCDOR county table]. Do not quote a single “NC combined rate” — always quote state + county + transit for the delivery county, and re-check Mecklenburg separately after July 2026.
  • Sourcing: Destination-based (sale sourced where the customer receives the product) in secondary guides; remote sellers with >$100,000 NC sales in the previous/current year must collect (economic nexus, secondary guide summary — statute pinpoint [VERIFY]).
