  • Unauthorised-substances tax (NOT a hemp tax, do not confuse): Chapter 105, Article 2D imposes a tax on unauthorised controlled substances, including marijuana at $3.50/gram with a >42.5-gram threshold and stamps due within 48 hours (secondary summary: https://www.mycannabis.com/cannabis-in-north-carolina/ — statute pinpoint [VERIFY]). Paying it does not legalise possession; it is a tax-enforcement tool used after seizure. It does not apply to lawful hemp.
  • Tobacco / ENDS taxes: Hemp vapes that are also ENDS products may fall under tobacco/ENDS reporting (NCDOR PACT Act notice, 19 Mar 2021: https://www.ncdor.gov/documents/files/notice-new-jenkinspact-act-reporting-requirements-products-delivered-through-aerosolized-solution/open) — that is a reporting regime for cigarettes/ENDS, not a hemp excise. No hemp-vape excise rate was located.

2.4 IRS §280E as it applies in North Carolina

  • Federal baseline (fixed): §280E disallowance applies to trafficking in Schedule I/II substances. Under split scheduling, Schedule III medical activity is generally out of §280E; adult-use remains in.
  • NC consequence: Because NC has no state-licensed medical programme, there is no NC Schedule III medical activity to which the federal relief could attach at state level, and no NC adult-use activity is lawful at all — so for any NC marijuana business operating outside tribal land, §280E is moot because the activity is criminal, not merely non-deductible. For hemp businesses, hemp is not a controlled substance, so §280E does not apply to lawful hemp activity as a matter of federal law — with the forward risk noted by UNC SOG (Winter 2026 blog, via secondary summary): if a federal hemp redefinition made a hemp product a Schedule I substance, §280E disallowance would presumptively follow for that product.
  • State conformity / decoupling: No NC §280E decoupling statute was located. NC individual tax starts from federal AGI (G.S. 105-153.5, with decoupling adjustments in (c2) for items such as bonus depreciation — 85% addback under §§105-153.6 / 105-130.5B in secondary summaries) and corporate tax from federal taxable income with NC modifications (G.S. 105-130.5). The decoupling lists checked contain no §280E addback or subtraction. Secondary Cannabis CPA guide (1 July 2025) states: “North Carolina conforms to federal tax treatment under IRC §280E — no state-level decoupling” (https://cannabiscpa.tax/north-carolina-cannabis-tax-guide-cbd-only-2025-edition/) — secondary only, [VERIFY] against the current §§105-153.5 / 105-130.5 text. If NC ever enacts a medical programme, the absence of a decoupling statute would mean NC taxable income follows the federal §280E result for that programme unless the legislature then acts — but that is a future-programme question, not current law.
  • IRC conformity date: Fixed-date conformity, G.S. 105-228.90(b)(7). Secondary CPA summary (July 2026) reports SB 595 (enacted 2 July 2026) updated conformity to 5 July 2025 — [VERIFY] against the session law; the conformity date matters for whether a future federal §280E guidance year is picked up by NC.
  • Rates context (not a cannabis rate): Individual flat rate 3.99% for 2026 (4.25% in 2025) under G.S. 105-153.7 in secondary summaries; corporate rate phasing to 0% by 2030, reported as 2% for 2026 in one secondary source and 2% in another — corporate 2026 rate conflict [VERIFY], irrelevant to hemp retailers except for entity-choice modelling.

2.5 Eastern Band of Cherokee Indians tribal cannabis taxes — CONFLICT, do not pick one rate

  • Historic tribal levy: Cherokee Code §105-1 tribal levy 7.5% on retail sales on the Reservation, with Ord. No. 54 (2023) amending §105-3 to remove the prior exemption for cannabis sales, so cannabis became subject to the levy (sub-research, staged-library PDF [VERIFY]).
  • 2026 press rate: Citizen Times / Cardinal Pine, 19 Oct 2026, attribute a 9% tribal tax rate to cannabis and report “tribal sales tax revenue topped $5 million in 2026”, from which the outlet calculates >$55.6 million in product over roughly nine months. That $55.6m is the outlet’s arithmetic, not a tribal financial statement, and 9% may reflect the levy plus a separate cannabis tax, or a rate change, or a different tax base.
  • Separate levy change (do not conflate): Ord. No. 518 (2025) raised the tribal levy (lodging-type) from 7.5% to 9% and privilege tax from 4% to 6% from Jan 2026 (One Feather, 4 Sept 2025: https://theonefeather.com/2025/09/04/dinilawigi-approves-increase-to-tribal-levy-and-privilege-tax-approves-fy26-budget/) — that ordinance is about the general levy/privilege tax, not necessarily the cannabis rate.
  • Pricing fact (secondary, consistent across sources): Great Smoky Cannabis Co. menu prices include sales tax — the amount on the menu is what the customer pays; cash/debit only, no credit cards (secondary summaries).
  • Per-capita distribution (tribal, via One Feather, Mar 2026): First Qualla Enterprises per-capita distribution from cannabis sales 20 Apr 2026, annually each April thereafter; minors’ distributions held to Apr 2027 — secondary tribal-newspaper report, not a Treasury statement [VERIFY for any revenue-split use].
  • Bottom line: Tribal cannabis tax rate: [VERIFY] from current Cherokee Code §105 / CCB / Tribal Treasury before quoting 7.5% or 9%. Revenue: >$5m in 2026 is enterprise-reported via press, not audited. No NC state sales tax is collected on Qualla Boundary tribal cannabis sales in any source checked, and no state–tribal tax compact for cannabis was located [UNAVAILABLE].

PART 3: LOCAL RULES

3.1 What authority counties and cities/towns have — which level does what

Zoning — Chapter 160D (the whole local story for hemp/smoke shops):

  • Grant: G.S. 160D-702(a): “A local government may adopt zoning regulations” regulating the location and use of buildings, structures and land. Source: https://www.NCleg.gov/EnactedLegislation/Statutes/HTML/ByArticle/Chapter_160D/Article_7.html (via sub-research).
  • Districts: G.S. 160D-703: a local government may divide its jurisdiction into conventional, conditional, form-based and overlay districts and impose jurisdiction-wide development standards.
  • City vs county jurisdiction — G.S. 160D-201: (a) Cities: within corporate limits and any extraterritorial jurisdiction (ETJ) under §160D-202. (b) Counties: “throughout the county except in areas subject to municipal planning and development regulation jurisdiction.” (c) A city that adopts zoning must apply it to its entire planning jurisdiction; a county may zone all or part of its unincorporated jurisdiction. Source: http://ncleg.gov/EnactedLegislation/Statutes/HTML/ByArticle/Chapter_160D/Article_2.html ; UNC SOG summary: https://www.sog.unc.edu/resources/legal-summaries/county-zoning-and-development-regulation . Practical rule: In a city ETJ, the city zones and the county does not (though county general police-power ordinances may still apply in the ETJ). A Wake County tobacco/hemp rule therefore does not apply inside Raleigh’s ETJ or corporate limits, and vice versa.
  • Moratoriums — G.S. 160D-107: A local government may adopt a temporary moratorium on development approvals “under limited circumstances and subject to specific procedural requirements”, with a statement of the problem, approvals covered, termination date and schedule of actions; it “may not extend beyond the time necessary to address the conditions that prompted its adoption.” A moratorium is a pause while a definition is drafted — not a permanent local ban power in itself.
  • No state preemption finding: No express NC statute was found that either (a) authorises a city/county to ban hemp retail outright, or (b) expressly preempts a local hemp ban. The operative local tool in practice is zoning: define the use, make it conditional / special-use, impose buffers. A locality that tried to ban a product (as opposed to regulating a land use) would face a different legal question — no adopted product ban was found in the eight jurisdictions below, so that question is untested in the sources checked.

Smoking-use authority — separate from retail zoning — G.S. 130A-498:

  • A local government may adopt ordinances / board-of-health rules “restricting or prohibiting smoking that are more restrictive than State law” in local-government buildings, grounds, vehicles, or public places. A post-1 July 2009 board-of-health rule must be approved by county-commissioner ordinance. It may not restrict smoking in, among other exempt places, a qualifying tobacco shop (post-1 July 2009: only if in a freestanding structure occupied solely by the shop and smoke does not migrate), a cigar bar, private club, designated hotel smoking rooms, or tobacco-industry premises. Source (mirror): https://law.justia.com/codes/north-carolina/chapter-130a/article-23/section-130a-498/ . Most local “smoke-free” ordinances regulate where smoking/vaping may occur, not whether a shop may open or what hemp it may sell — do not cite a smoke-free ordinance as a shop ban.

Business licences — the privilege licence is gone:

  • S.L. 2013-414 repealed G.S. 160A-211(a) (city privilege-license-tax authority) by drafting error, eff. 1 Jan 2014 (UNC SOG, 19 May 2014: https://canons.sog.unc.edu/blog/2014/05/19/did-the-general-assembly-repeal-city-privilege-license-tax-authority-last-year/). Part XII of S.L. 2014-3 repealed G.S. 153A-152 (county) and G.S. 160A-211 (city) as of 1 July 2015; Town of Wilkesboro (official town page) states the end-point plainly: “Without those two statutes, counties and cities have no authority to levy privilege license taxes” (https://wilkesboronc.org/dept-overview?id=44). H998 (2017) summary likewise describes deleting general privilege-tax authority, permitting a tax only where another statute authorises it, eff. 1 Jan 2018 (https://lrs.sog.unc.edu/lrs-subscr-view/bills_summaries/2304/H998). Bottom line: do not budget a general Charlotte/Raleigh/Durham “business licence” fee. What remains locally is (a) activity-specific state permits, (b) zoning/development permits, and (c) any narrow local registration created under a different authority — none found for general retail/tobacco retail in the eight jurisdictions below. Current codified text of former §§160A-211/153A-152 was not returned by the official ncleg search in sub-research — [VERIFY] before citing a current section number.

Tobacco retail licensing (state, for contrast): NC does not have a general state tobacco-retailer licence comparable to other states’ schemes in the sources checked; NCDOR tobacco/ENDS reporting (PACT Act notice, Part 2.3) is reporting, not a shop licence. A secondary Wolters Kluwer summary mentions state tobacco licensing fees in a $10–$25 range — [VERIFY] against NCDOR before use; do not present as a local fee.

No Greensboro tobacco-shop/smoke-shop definition, buffer, cap or moratorium returned [VERIFY by full-text LDO search]. No Guilford County tobacco/hemp retail zoning rule found. Trap: “Tobacco Shop (Class 1)” 20%-of-floor-area / 40%-of-sign-area and “Class 2” paraphernalia definitions retrieved are from Greenville, NC, not Greensboro: https://codelibrary.amlegal.com/codes/greenvillenc/latest/greenville_nc/0-0-0-4503
Durham (city) / Durham County No adopted buffer found; draft definition only No Durham UDO smoke/vape/hemp-retail definition, buffer, cap or moratorium returned. A Durham County document marked “DRAFT – DO NOT DISTRIBUTE” defines “Tobacco Shop” for a smoking rule as a business whose main purpose is tobacco/accessories, ≥75% of annual revenues from those sales, serving no food/alcohol — draft, not evidence of an adopted rule [VERIFY whether ever adopted]: https://www.dcovotes.dconc.gov/home/showpublisheddocument/20799/636295089286800000 . Secondary summaries list Durham among NC municipalities with 100% smoke-free public-place policies [VERIFY against adopted ordinance] — a §130A-498 use restriction, not a retailer licence/buffer.
Winston-Salem / Forsyth County None found Joint Winston-Salem/Forsyth UDO: no tobacco-shop/smoke-shop/vape/hemp-retail definition, buffer, cap or moratorium returned; general retail includes tobacco as a product line, as in Raleigh/Greensboro [VERIFY by full-text UDO check]. Only Forsyth item retrieved was a Town of Lewisville UDO rewrite into §160D compliance — not a smoke-shop rule.
Fayetteville / Cumberland County Proposal + referral only; no adopted buffer verified Fayetteville city: HalfWheel report (~2024) says Planning Commission on 15 July voted to postpone a draft tobacco-retailer distance ordinance (from schools, churches, residential areas, other tobacco retailers) so staff could resolve issues, hoping no existing business would be forced to close; final adopted text, if any, not found [VERIFY]: https://halfwheel.com/fayetteville-n-c-tobacco-store-ordinance-delayed/65611/ . Cumberland County: 10 Nov 2023 Board of Commissioners wrap-up records a request to regulate “businesses selling tobacco and hemp products as their principal sale” as a zoning matter; Board directed referral to Board of Health then Planning Board, expressly citing Wake County’s 16 Oct 2023 amendment (1,000 ft). No final Cumberland adoption found [VERIFY]: https://www.cumberlandcountync.gov/home/2023/11/10/cumberland-county-commissioners-agenda-session-wrap-up . Trap: “Vape/Smoke Shop — 1 mile from another vape/smoke shop or school/daycare/community centre/youth area” is Fayetteville, West Virginia, not NC: https://codelibrary.amlegal.com/codes/fayetteville/latest/fayetteville_wv/0-0-0-14673
Wilmington / New Hanover County City: none verified. County: smoking/vaping USE rule only. Wilmington, NC city: no smoke-shop zoning definition/buffer/cap/moratorium verified. Trap: a Jan 2026 one-year smoke-shop moratorium document is Wilmington, Delaware (City Council, Dept. of Land Use and Planning) — must not be cited for NC. New Hanover County (incl. Wilmington, Carolina Beach, Wrightsville Beach, Kure Beach for this rule): Smoking and Vaping Ordinance / HHS Board Rule defines “Smoking and E-Cigarette shop” as a business whose main purpose is sale of smoking/e-cigarette products/accessories, ≥75% of annual revenues from those sales, serving no food/alcohol; prohibits smoking/e-cigarette use in county/city/town buildings, vehicles, grounds and public places. Secondary report dates effectiveness to 1 Feb 2021 and notes vape shops among exempted places for use — a §130A-498 use rule, not a retailer licensing or zoning-buffer scheme, no distance/cap: https://www.nhcgov.com/DocumentCenter/View/1144/Health-and-Human-Services-Board-Smoking-and-Vaping-Rule-PDF ; https://www.nhcgov.com/DocumentCenter/View/1143/Smoking-and-Vaping-Ordinance-PDF
Asheville / Buncombe County Smoking-use ban on city property only; no retail rule found Asheville City Code §11-32 prohibits smoking, incl. e-cigarettes, in any city park/greenway and in/on city buildings, facilities and grounds (incl. Greater Asheville Regional Airport Authority, Pack Place, Housing Authority property), except in specially designated smoking areas: https://codelibrary.amlegal.com/codes/ashevillenc/latest/asheville_nc/0-0-0-11042 . No Asheville/Buncombe smoke/vape/hemp/CBD retail definition, buffer, cap, moratorium or retailer licence found [VERIFY by full-text code search].

Comparators outside the eight (showing the emerging NC template — not big-city law):

  • Morrisville (Wake County town), UDO Jan 2025, “Tobacco and/or Hemp”: ≥1,000 ft property-line-to-property-line from any public/private elementary/middle/high school, daycare, public park/recreation facility, place of worship, or Gateway Overlay property, and ≥1,000 ft from any other Tobacco and/or Hemp retail establishment. Definition expressly includes cigarettes, cigars, e-cigarettes, e-juice, all other vaping products, Delta-8, Delta-9 and other THC products: https://user-cjghrlw.cld.bz/UDO-Adopted-Version-January-2025/193/
  • Harnett County, adopted 19 Oct 2026 (reported): UDO §§1.2/7.6.7, tobacco/vape/CBD/hookah and similar establishments as conditional zoning; ≥2,000 ft from a school (public/private), ≥1,000 ft from a church, childcare facility, community centre, recreational facility, park, hospital or another such establishment, ≥100 ft from residential parcels/districts, property-line-to-property-line; site plan must show all land uses within 2,000 ft. Pre-adoption draft showed 1,000 ft from schools and a 1,500-ft survey — the 2,000-ft school figure is from the post-vote news report, so the enrolled UDO text is [VERIFY]: https://sandhills.news/2026/10/21/harnett-county-smoke-shop-rules-bunnlevel-rezoning/ ; draft: https://www.harnett.org/planning/downloads/tobacco,vape,-and-cbd-ta-sr.pdf
  • Wilson County, 4 Aug 2025 (secondary report): UDO amendments adding special-use rules for smoke/vape shops and hemp cannabis dispensaries, with a reported 2,500-ft separation — details [VERIFY against adopted Wilson UDO]: https://citizenportal.ai/articles/7498821/North-Carolina/Wilson-County/Wilson-County-approves-zoning-changes-to-accessory-structure-rules-and-adds-controls-for-vape-smoke-and-hemp-dispensaries

3.3 Official list or map of jurisdictions with such ordinances

None exists. No NC state agency, UNC SOG page, or league/association list/map of hemp/smoke-shop zoning ordinances was located in Run 1. The eight largest jurisdictions checked are those in §3.2 (Charlotte/Mecklenburg, Raleigh/Wake, Greensboro/Guilford, Durham, Winston-Salem/Forsyth, Fayetteville/Cumberland, Wilmington/New Hanover, Asheville/Buncombe). A researcher must check each UDO/code individually; the comparators in §3.2 show that smaller towns/counties (Morrisville, Harnett, Wilson) have in fact moved faster than the big cities.

3.4 Local business licence or permit fees

No general retail business-licence fee and no city tobacco-retailer licence fee was found published for any of the eight jurisdictions (see §3.1 privilege-licence repeal). The only published, city-specific figures retrieved are zoning/development-process fees, which are not a business licence:

Jurisdiction Published figure (with currency caveat) What it actually is
Raleigh Commercial zoning permit $278, or $289 with standard 4% technology surcharge — FY27 Development Services Fee Guide, eff. 1 July 2026, via secondary guide summarising the city guide [VERIFY against official FY27 guide] Zoning permit for a retail change-of-use, not a tobacco/hemp fee: https://buildpermitguide.com/data/v1/raw/326
Durham Business/Use Verification Letter $58.00 per letter, payment before application (official city Planning page, current at search) Verifies whether a use is allowable at a property; not a licence: https://www.durhamnc.gov/4856/Verification-Letters . Older Durham fee schedule (rev. 27 June 2019) lists Home Occupation $25, Formal Letter of Interpretation $40, Use Determination $40, Zoning and Property Analysis $80 (+4%) — outdated, do not quote as current: https://www.durhamnc.gov/DocumentCenter/View/27004/Fee-Schedule-PDF---revised-June-27-2019?bidId=
Greensboro Short-term-rental zoning permit $200 (city news, 1 Apr 2024) — illustrative only Not general retail / not tobacco: https://www.greensboro-nc.gov/Home/Components/News/News/19285/36
Charlotte, Winston-Salem, Fayetteville, Wilmington, Asheville No published general-retail or tobacco-retailer local fee found A secondary short-term-rental guide mentions a superseded Charlotte FY2023 zoning-use-permit figure of $305 — expressly superseded, not quoted as current.

3.5 Zones where such businesses are permitted and local buffer rules

  • General rule (big cities): Where no hemp/smoke-specific use exists, a hemp/CBD/smoke/vape shop is permitted wherever general retail is permitted under the district use table, subject to ordinary district standards (parking, signage, setbacks) and to any smoking-use rule (§130A-498) that governs use on the premises. No big-city buffer applies on the evidence found.
  • Wake County unincorporated: Tobacco and Hemp Retail is a defined use subject to the §4-64 buffers in §3.2 (1,000 ft from another such use; 1,000 ft from school/park/greenway/residence; one per property/building). The district(s) in which the use is permitted vs conditional must be confirmed from the Wake UDO use table [VERIFY] — the buffer text alone does not state the district list.
  • Morrisville / Harnett / Wilson comparators: Buffers as in §3.2; Harnett uses conditional zoning (discretionary approval), Morrisville uses distance standards. No statewide buffer, no state “school buffer” for hemp shops, and no cap (numerical limit) on shop numbers was found adopted anywhere in NC in this research — caps, if any, would operate indirectly through distance rules, not as a stated quota [VERIFY for Harnett/Wilson final texts].

3.6 Qualla Boundary and surrounding counties (Swain, Jackson, Graham, Haywood, Cherokee County, NC)

Geography: The Qualla Boundary lies primarily in Swain and Jackson counties, with smaller parts in Cherokee, Graham and Haywood counties; the EBCI five-county service area is Cherokee, Graham, Jackson, Swain and Haywood. Sources: https://theonefeather.com/2023/01/12/tribe-to-seek-north-carolina-law-changes-on-state-income-tax-cannabis-issues/ ; https://www.thefranklinpress.com/regional-news/cherokee-nation-votes-legalize-marijuana (secondary).

Official county positions / agreements / disputes — finding: essentially none documented officially.

  • No official county resolution, intergovernmental agreement, or county–EBCI cannabis agreement was found for any of the five counties in the searches run. Do not infer agreement or opposition from silence.
  • Swain County — Sheriff Curtis Cochran (press-reported, not an official sheriff’s-office release found): On transport from the Coopers Creek grow site (an enclave surrounded by state-jurisdiction land) back onto tribal land: “until North Carolina changes the law, that it is still illegal to possess or transport marijuana on the highway.” A later press summary quotes him: “They need to educate their people up there that when they come off the boundary, they’re in a different world … We’ll educate them if we catch them with it. We’ll charge them for it.” Sources (secondary/Wikipedia summary): https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Eastern_Band_of_Cherokee_Indians_Marijuana_Legalization_Measure — press-reported only; [VERIFY] against a sheriff’s-office statement before quoting as an official position.
  • Prosecutorial District 43 (covers Cherokee, Clay, Graham, Haywood, Jackson, Macon and Swain counties) — District Attorney Ashley Hornsby Welch, statement dated 3 Apr 2024 (as reported by Asheville Citizen Times / Gannett mirror): “We respect tribal sovereignty, and we respect the Eastern Band of Cherokee Indians’ right to enact tribal laws. In North Carolina, the cultivation, distribution and possession of marijuana remains illegal, and we will continue to enforce state law off Qualla Boundary.” This is a prosecutorial-district position, not a county-commissioner resolution for any single county: https://e2bf85d2-acf2-4e0f-be1d-22d95306e31a.p.creative-connect-beta.gannettdigital.com/story/news/local/2024/04/09/ncs-1st-cannabis-dispensary-opens-420-wnc-da-to-enforce-state-law/73241600007/
  • State Attorney General (then Josh Stein), as reported second-hand: declined to intervene, saying “This is an issue that the tribe and local law enforcement will need to work out” — [VERIFY] against an AG release; no AG–county–EBCI agreement was found.
  • Jackson, Graham, Haywood, Cherokee County (NC): none found — no official county position, sheriff statement, agreement or dispute document located for any of the four. District 43 covers Graham, Haywood, Jackson and Cherokee for prosecution, but that is not a county-board position.
  • Characterisation on the evidence found: a jurisdictional boundary dispute about off-Boundary transport and possession, not a documented county–EBCI cannabis compact or revenue agreement. No compact/agreement text was found — [VERIFY] with the Swain County Sheriff’s Office, District 43, and the EBCI Attorney General’s office before asserting a current working arrangement. Full tribal-law treatment (including transport) is Run 2, Part 10.

3.7 Business concentration — only what official sources say

Official sources say nothing systematic about where hemp/cannabis businesses are concentrated in NC. There is no state hemp-retailer registry, no state count by county, and no official concentration map (Part 5). The only concentration-adjacent official-adjacent facts located are:

  • Former pilot scale (NCDA, 2021): ~1,500 growers statewide — no county breakout published in the sources checked.
  • USDA National Hemp Report county/acreage data exists at federal level but was not extracted by county in Run 1 — do not rank counties from it without extraction.
  • A secondary Triangle directory (Raleigh Dispensaries, Oct 2026, non-official) counts 81 Triangle shops (Wake 60, Durham 11, Johnston 5, Orange 2, Chatham 1, Franklin 1, Lee 1; 54 Wake shops listing THCA flower): https://raleighdispensaries.com/reports/december-11-triangle-hemp-shops — secondary directory, not an official count, and its methodology (directory listings, Google closure checks) is not an official census. It is reported here only to show that no official equivalent exists, and must not be quoted as “official retail counts”. The same directory reports 7 Triangle closures in Oct 2026 checks (incl. both Carolina Hemp Hut stores, Everything Hemp Store Apex, CannaCraft Raleigh, per Google “permanently closed”) — again secondary, dated Oct 2026.

No ranking, cluster estimate or “top county” claim is made in this report. Any such claim would be invented without an official source.

PART 4: LICENCE TYPES

4.1 State licences — North Carolina issues NO cannabis licences

Stated clearly: North Carolina issues no licence for the cultivation, manufacture, distribution, testing, transport or sale of marijuana (medical or adult-use). There is no state cannabis licence to apply for, no application window, no fee schedule, and no state cannabis regulator.

State registration / licence / permit required to sell or manufacture hemp-derived products — current law:

Activity State licence/registration required? Fee Requirements / info link
Grow hemp (cultivation) USDA licence only — not a state licence. See Part 5 / Run 2 Part 7. No NC fee (USDA licence itself has no fee in NC State Extension summary; grower pays sampling agent + lab + FSA registration). $0 licence fee (federal) USDA HeMP: https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/information-for-hemp-growers ; NCDA&CS: https://www.ncagr.gov/divisions/plant-industry/hemp-nc
Process hemp (extraction, manufacture of hemp products) None at state level. NCDA&CS: “no longer offers registration services for hemp processors” / no state authority. Federal food/drug and general manufacturing rules may apply to a specific product, but no hemp-processor licence exists. — Same NCDA&CS page
Manufacture hemp-derived consumable products (edibles, beverages, vapes, etc.) None at state level today. PROPOSED Ch. 18D manufacturer licence only (Part 4.2). — (proposed: $15,000 — see §4.2) —
Sell hemp / hemp-derived products at retail (shop, online, gas station, etc.) None hemp-specific at state level. Generally applicable registrations only: NC Secretary of State entity registration (if an entity), NCDOR sales-tax registration (G.S. 105-164.29 — certificate of registration for retail sales generally, not a hemp licence), local zoning/development permits (Part 3). SOS entity fees vary by entity type (not hemp fees); NCDOR sales-tax registration has no hemp fee SOS: https://www.sosnc.gov/online_services/search/by_title/_Business_Registration (search; filing portal via sosnc.gov) ; NCDOR sales-tax registration via https://www.ncdor.gov/ (sales-tax number registries: https://www.ncdor.gov/taxes-forms/sales-and-use-tax/other-sales-and-use-tax-resources/sales-and-use-tax-number-registries)
Sell hemp products as food / dietary supplement No hemp-specific food licence; general NC food-manufacturing / food-establishment rules (NCDHHS Food Protection / county health departments) apply to food businesses generally — not a hemp licence, and no hemp-food rule was located that creates a hemp-specific food permit. Varies by local health department (not a hemp fee) [VERIFY — food-establishment permit scope for a hemp-food manufacturer in Run 2, Part 11]
Transport / distribute hemp No state hemp transport licence. Hemp shipments must meet the federal/state hemp definition; interstate transport protection is federal (2018 Farm Bill §10114, Run 2 Part 10). — —
Test hemp (retail products) No state hemp-lab licence. Cultivation testing labs are DEA-registered under the federal programme (Part 5). — USDA DEA lab list: https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/dea-laboratories?field_lab_location_administrative_area=NC

Enacted-but-not-yet-effective state hemp licences: NONE. No NC hemp licensing act has been enacted with a future effective date. Every Ch. 18D licence in §4.2 is proposed, and HB 328’s proposed dates (15 July / 12 Nov 2026 in secondary summaries) are draft dates in an unenacted bill, not enacted-but-delayed law.

(opened text). SB 265: Dept. of Revenue licences $250–$15,000 (secondary).

4.3 Eastern Band of Cherokee Indians — tribal licensing

Regulator: EBCI Cannabis Control Board (CCB) — “the office of the EBCI tribal government responsible for regulation of the marijuana industry and hemp sales on Tribal lands. The CCB issues licenses for marijuana businesses and hemp stores, agent cards … and patient cards” (CCB About page: https://ebci-ccb.org/about-the-board/). Cherokee Code Chapter 17 (marijuana) and Chapter 17A (hemp) govern it. Note: the CCB site found is ebci-ccb.org, not ebci.com (tribal-government domain) — [VERIFY] whether ebci.com hosts a separate CCB landing page. Full tribal licence-type inventory, verbatim ordinance text, fees and application process are Run 2, Part 10 — Run 1 gives the verified skeleton only, so Run 2 does not have to re-prove the regulator’s existence:

  • Medical code: Ord. No. 539, 16 Aug 2021, codified at Cherokee Code Ch. 17; amended Ord. No. 138, 15 Mar 2022 (recited in Ord. No. 63 preamble): https://ebci-ccb.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/08/2024-6-27-Ord-No-63-2024-Adult-Use-Cannabis-and-Hemp.pdf
  • Adult-use code: Ord. No. 63 (2024), passed by Dinilawigi (Tribal Council) 6 June 2024, 9–2 (Sneed and Vice-Chair Wolfe against, Wachacha absent); two floor amendments (home-grow; ban on individually-owned hemp/cannabis stores) removed after a protest hearing 27 June 2024; approved as amended and signed by Principal Chief Michell Hicks 27 June 2024; amends Cherokee Code Chs. 14 and 17. Sources: https://theonefeather.com/2024/06/07/dinilawigi-tribal-council-approves-adult-use-cannabis/ ; https://theonefeather.com/2024/07/04/cherokee-begins-adult-use-cannabis-sales/
  • Licence types (skeleton, [VERIFY] full list from Ord. No. 63 / Ch. 17 in Run 2): CCB-issued licences for marijuana businesses and hemp stores, plus agent cards (for facility workers) and patient cards (medical). Ord. No. 63 defines an inventory control system (chain-of-custody monitoring from cultivation to end consumer) and an electronic verification system (real-time database accessible to the Board and facilities); products must be packaged/labelled to allow tracking, with THC in mg stated. Vendor/platform name not found [VERIFY] — do not assume Metrc. Testing described secondarily: potency, pesticides, heavy metals, microbial, other QA before sale (https://www.visitsmokies.org/leaves-of-a-different-nature/).
  • Who can hold them: In the tribal scheme as reported, the operating enterprise is tribal: Qualla Enterprises, LLC (EBCI tribal entity) operates the farm (Tsisqwohi/Birdtown, plus Oconaluftee Cultivation Facility coming online) and the dispensary. Whether private/non-tribal-entity licences are issuable under Ch. 17/17A, and on what fees/terms, is [VERIFY from the CCB licence pages / Ord. No. 63 in Run 2] — Run 1 does not assert that a non-tribal applicant can obtain a CCB cultivation or retail licence today. The June 2024 floor amendment that would have banned individually-owned hemp/cannabis stores was removed after protest (One Feather, 9 Aug 2024) — the removal does not by itself prove private licences are now issued.
  • Fees if published: No CCB fee schedule was located in Run 1 [UNAVAILABLE]. Do not quote a CCB licence fee.
  • Tribal enterprise operating sales: Great Smoky Cannabis Co., 91 Bingo Loop Road, Cherokee, NC (former bingo facility, ~10,000 sq ft retail at opening, drive-thru), operated by Qualla Enterprises, LLC. Medical sales 20 Apr 2024; adult-use to EBCI/other federally recognised tribal members 4 July 2024; adult-use to all adults 21+ 7 Sept 2024. Product types: flower, pre-rolls, vape cartridges, concentrates, edibles, tinctures, topicals; ~40 products at launch → ~500 by Oct 2026 (Qualla via press). Second retail dispensary: none found as of 24 Oct 2026 [VERIFY — absence from search, not a confirmed denial]. Second cultivation property (not retail): Oconaluftee Cultivation Facility, reported as additional growing space for >110,000 lb in 2027.
  • Purchase limits — CONFLICT to reconcile in Run 2: Ord. No. 63 text (OCR of official PDF) states a retailer does not sell “more than one and one-half (1.5) ounces of marijuana per [transaction/day — OCR ambiguous]” and no more than 2,500 mg THC per day, 10,000 mg per month; dispensary operational limits reported secondarily are 1 oz flower per visit/day, 6 oz per month, same concentrate caps, tracked at point of sale. Do not quote a single limit without noting the 1 oz vs 1.5 oz discrepancy [VERIFY from CCB FAQ / Ord. No. 63 clean text]. Temporary opening-week limit (Sept 2024, not standing): 0.5 oz flower, total flower + concentrates ≤42 g (demand management).
  • Other tribes (preview — full treatment Run 2, Part 10): As of late 2025 NC has three federally recognised tribes, not two, following the addition of the Lumbee Tribe of North Carolina to the BIA official list (Federal Register 30 Jan 2026, 91 FR 4102–4106, Doc. 2026-01899; BIA news: https://www.bia.gov/news/lumbee-tribe-added-official-list-federally-recognized-tribes — note: the FR date is Jan 2026, after the FY2026 NDAA enacted 18 Dec 2025; the BIA list updated 17 Feb 2026 for hemp plans is a different list). The three are EBCI, Lumbee, and — for completeness — the Catawba Indian Nation is SC-headquartered (Rock Hill, SC), not NC-based, though it operates Two Kings Casino in Kings Mountain, NC and became NC’s 9th state-recognised tribe 5 June 2026 (One Feather, 11 June 2026: https://theonefeather.com/2026/06/11/catawba-nation-becomes-north-carolinas-ninth-state-recognized-tribe/). No cannabis/hemp operation was found for Lumbee or Catawba in Run 1 [VERIFY against tribal council sources in Run 2]. EBCI itself has a USDA-approved tribal hemp plan (AMS plan-review page, Tribes list) — distinct from NC’s USDA-direct position.

4.4 Social equity

  • State: No state social-equity programme exists — because no state cannabis programme exists, there is no state equity licence, equity applicant category, equity fund, or expungement-linked equity provision in a cannabis statute. (General expungement law is Run 2, Part 12; it is not a cannabis social-equity programme.)
  • Tribal: No EBCI programme labelled “social equity” was located in Run 1. The EBCI programme’s equity-relevant facts as reported are structural, not labelled equity: tribal ownership (Qualla Enterprises), ~270 employees with >85% enrolled tribal members (Qualla via press, Oct 2026), and per-capita distributions from cannabis sales from Apr 2026 (One Feather, Mar 2026). Whether the CCB has an equity / tribal-member preference in licensing is [VERIFY in Run 2, Part 10] — do not label the tribal-ownership model “social equity” without a tribal source using that term.

4.5 Estimated startup costs for hemp retail / manufacturing in NC (NON-OFFICIAL estimates — marked as such)

No official NC startup-cost estimate exists. The figures below are illustrative, non-official, and must not be quoted as NC requirements. They are built only from the verified cost elements in this report plus generally applicable business costs; every non-verified element is flagged.

Cost element Verified / estimated Basis
State hemp licence fee $0 — verified (no licence exists) Part 4.1
USDA grower licence fee (if growing) $0 licence fee — verified via NC State Extension summary; grower pays sampling agent + lab + FSA costs (amounts vary, not published as a flat fee) https://hemp.ces.ncsu.edu/news/all-nc-hemp-growers-must-now-be-licensed-through-usda/
NCDOR sales-tax registration No hemp fee; general registration (no fee stated in sources checked) Part 4.1
SOS entity formation Varies by entity (not a hemp fee) — estimate only, [VERIFY] current SOS fee schedule sosnc.gov
Local zoning / development permits Raleigh commercial zoning permit $278/$289 (secondary guide, §3.4); Durham verification letter $58 (official city page) — these are the only published local figures; other jurisdictions’ zoning fees vary and were not found §3.4
Premises, fit-out, inventory, testing (voluntary COAs), insurance, legal No NC official estimate — NON-OFFICIAL ESTIMATE ONLY if a business plan needs a placeholder: a small hemp retail shop’s first-year cash costs are commonly discussed in industry guides in the low-five to low-six figures, but no NC-specific, sourced figure was located and none is asserted here. [UNAVAILABLE — do not use an unsourced range for investment decisions]
PROPOSED (not current) Ch. 18D costs Manufacturer $15,000 initial / $5,000 renewal (HB 607 as filed, secondary); distributor/retailer $250–$15,000 (secondary summaries) — budget $0 today, and re-price only if a Ch. 18D bill is enacted Part 1.9 / §4.2
Federal cliff cost (11 Dec 2026) Unquantified but material: inventory that fails the new total-THC / 0.4 mg test becomes federally unlawful hemp; write-down / reformulation / exit costs are business-specific and cannot be estimated from official sources Part 1.5, H11

Bottom line for §4.5: The cheapest verified fact is also the most important: NC hemp retail/manufacturing has no state licence fee barrier today — the barriers are premises/zoning, ordinary business costs, voluntary testing, and the 11 Dec 2026 federal redefinition, not a licence queue.

PART 5: LICENCE DATA SOURCES

Do NOT list every licence holder — this is an inventory of sources, as instructed. For each source: holder type, format, access/download, update frequency as stated, latest data date, fields, limitations. Exact direct download links are given only where a source actually publishes a downloadable file; where the source is a search page / dashboard with no download, that is stated plainly.

# Source Holder type Format / access Download link + file name (if any) Update frequency / latest data date (as stated) Fields available Limitations
S1 USDA AMS — Hemp Public Search Tool (individual lookup) USDA-licensed hemp producers (incl. NC growers under the USDA plan) Interactive search only — no bulk download, no export button identified. Public URL cited in state portals: https://hemp.ams.usda.gov/s/PublicSearchTool (via AMS FAQ: https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/frequently-asked-questions-faq — FAQ states: “Information on licensees in your area can be obtained through the Hemp Public Search Tool.”) No download link; no file name. Do not cite a CSV for this tool. Live search (licence status as held in HeMP); no “last updated” date published on the tool page in sources checked. Licence holder, licence status, growing location area (as entered in HeMP) — exact field list [VERIFY by running a search; the tool was not queried with a live NC search in Run 1]. One-by-one lookup only; cannot count NC licensees without querying each record; law-enforcement detailed tool is separate (hempsearch.ams.usda.gov, HeMP law-enforcement access via FarmBill.Hemp@usda.gov).
S2 USDA AMS — FOIA Reading Room: USDA Hemp Producer Licensees (PDF) (bulk list) USDA-licensed hemp producers, national (filter for NC) PDF via FOIA Reading Room: https://www.ams.usda.gov/about-ams/foia/reading-room — entry listed as “USDA Hemp Producer Licensees (pdf) — Updated April 14, 2026”. Direct PDF URL cited in sub-research: https://www.ams.usda.gov/sites/default/files/media/FOIAUSDAHempLicensees.pdf — [VERIFY] that this exact URL still resolves; the Reading Room page is the authoritative locator, the PDF URL is the file as cited. File: FOIAUSDAHempLicensees.pdf (AMS FOIA PDF, updated 14 Apr 2026). No CSV/XLSX version was located. Updated 14 Apr 2026 (as listed in Reading Room). No more recent update date located as of 24 Oct 2026. Producer name, licence number, location (as in FOIA release) — exact columns [VERIFY by opening the PDF; the PDF was not tallied in Run 1]. National PDF, not NC-filtered; FOIA release may redact/omit fields; no NC count was extracted from it in Run 1 — do not quote a count from it without extraction. Secondary counts (589 in 2023; 658 for 2026 season, via https://hempcbdbusinessplans.com/north-carolina-hemp-industry/) are not AMS counts and are flagged as such in Part 6.
S3 USDA AMS — State and Tribal Plan Review page Plans, not individual licensees Web table: https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review No download (table only; plan PDFs linked per state/tribe where a plan exists). Updated 17 Feb 2026 (page header). Plan status by state/tribe: NC = “USDA Hemp Producer License”; EBCI = “Approved Plan (pdf)”. Proves NC has no state plan and EBCI has a tribal plan; gives no licensee names or counts.
S4 USDA AMS — HeMP (Hemp eManagement Platform) (licensee portal, login required) USDA licensees, sampling agents, labs, state/tribal governments, law enforcement (separate access) Login portal: https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/hemp-emanagement-platform-hemp — licensees apply/renew/amend/print licence, file AMS-27 disposal / AMS-28 annual reports, view lab results / FSA lots. No public download. Rolling (licensee-maintained). Full licence record for the logged-in licensee only. Not a public search tool — cannot be used to enumerate NC licensees without credentials.
S5 USDA AMS — DEA-registered hemp testing laboratories list Hemp testing labs (incl. NC labs) Searchable list with state filter: https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/dea-laboratories?field_lab_location_administrative_area=NC No download identified; list page only. As maintained by AMS (no update date stated on filter page in sources checked). Lab name, location. NC labs returned in sub-research: Delta 9 Analytical (Raleigh), NMS Labs – WIN (Winston-Salem), Research Triangle Park Laboratories, Inc. (Raleigh), USDA National Science Laboratories (Gastonia) — [VERIFY by re-running the filter; lab status changes]. DEA registration ≠ NC licence; a lab on this list tests hemp for the federal programme, not retail hemp products under a (non-existent) NC retail-testing rule.
S6 NCDA&CS — Hemp in NC page Former NC pilot licensees (historical only) Web page only: https://www.ncagr.gov/divisions/plant-industry/hemp-nc No download; no current list. The page states NCDA&CS no longer offers producer/processor registration. Page reflects post-pilot position (pilot ended 31 Dec 2021; Art. 50E repealed 30 June 2022). Historical scale only (~1,500 producers at pilot peak, via NCDA press). No current NCDA hemp licensee data exists to inventory. Any “NCDA hemp list” dated after 2021 is either pilot-era or mislabelled.
S7 NC state hemp retailer / manufacturer registration list Hemp retailers / manufacturers DOES NOT EXIST. No state list, dashboard, CSV or PDF was located, consistent with Part 4.1 (no licence to list). — — — The absence is the finding. Do not substitute a private directory (e.g. Raleigh Dispensaries, Part 3.7) for a state list.
S8 ALE enforcement / compliance data (hemp) Hemp retailers/manufacturers (would-be licensees under Ch. 18D) NO published dataset located [UNAVAILABLE]. ALE appears in hemp law only as the proposed enforcer in HB 328/HB 607. General ALE enforcement reports (alcohol/tobacco) do not break out hemp. — — — Cannot quantify hemp enforcement (inspections, seizures, penalties) from official sources in Run 1.
S9 NCDHHS — Epilepsy Alternative Treatment Act registry statistics Registered caregivers/patients/neurologists under Art. 5G (historical / status [VERIFY] — see Gap R1-01) NO published count located [UNAVAILABLE]. DHHS registry (if still operating) is a secure law-enforcement-verification database, not a public list; records under the old registry were confidential by statute (secondary summary). — — Counts only would be the deliverable if DHHS published them; it does not in sources checked. Do not quote a patient/caregiver number. The Art. 5G repeal conflict (Gap R1-01) must be resolved before even the existence of a current count can be asserted.
S10 NC Secretary of State — Business Registration search NC business entities (incl. hemp businesses if formed as an entity) Free interactive search (no login for basic search): https://www.sosnc.gov/online_services/search/by_title/_Business_Registration — search by company name, SOSID, company officials, or registered agents; Assumed Business Names searched separately. Search page only — no bulk download, no export button identified. Live entity status. Entity name, SOSID, status, registered agent, officials (as filed). Proves entity existence/status/agent only; does not prove a hemp licence, and sole proprietorships / general partnerships do not appear as entities. A hemp shop organised as a sole proprietorship is invisible here.
S11 NC Department of Revenue — Sales and Use Tax Number Registries Holders of active NC sales/use-tax accounts (incl. hemp retailers if registered — the registry does not label them as hemp) Searchable registries: https://www.ncdor.gov/taxes-forms/sales-and-use-tax/other-sales-and-use-tax-resources/sales-and-use-tax-number-registries — “Registry of Sales and Use Tax Numbers contains the names and account ID and SST IDs … of all active sales and use tax accounts”; separate registry for Direct Pay Permits / Exemption Certificate Numbers. Unavailable Sundays 04:00–12:00 for maintenance (sub-research note). Search page only — no bulk download identified. Active accounts as maintained by NCDOR. Name, account ID, SST ID (sales-tax registry). Confirms a seller holds an active sales-tax account; does not identify the seller as a hemp business, and sales-tax registration ≠ hemp authorisation.
S12 EBCI Cannabis Control Board — tribal licence records CCB licensees (marijuana businesses, hemp stores), agent-card holders, patient-card holders CCB site: https://ebci-ccb.org/ (About: https://ebci-ccb.org/about-the-board/) ; Ord. No. 63 PDF: https://ebci-ccb.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/08/2024-6-27-Ord-No-63-2024-Adult-Use-Cannabis-and-Hemp.pdf No public licensee list / download located in Run 1 [UNAVAILABLE]. Whether the CCB publishes a searchable licence database is [VERIFY in Run 2, Part 10] — the CCB FAQ page was cited secondarily but not opened cleanly. — Licence types / agent cards / patient cards are the categories the CCB issues (per About page); individual licensee names/fields are not published in sources checked, except the operating enterprise (Qualla Enterprises / Great Smoky) which is public by reporting. Tribal licence records may be non-public by tribal law; do not assume a FOIA-equivalent right of access.
S13 Local (county/city/town) business licence / permit records for hemp/smoke/vape shops Local permit holders (zoning permits, verification letters, etc.) No published local hemp/smoke-shop permit list was located for any of the eight jurisdictions in §3.2. Local records that do exist are general: planning-department permit logs, Legistar agendas (e.g. Wake County Legistar for UDO amendments), and city fee-guide documents — none is a hemp-shop register. — — — A hemp shop’s local footprint is discoverable only by a parcel/use-permit search in the relevant planning department, not by a published list. Harnett/Wilson/Morrisville UDO amendment packets (Part 3.2) are the closest local documents, and they are rules, not registers.

Report PDF/XLSX via USDA NASS (exact file name [VERIFY] — do not guess a NASS URL). Annual (2023, 2024, 2025 editions cited secondarily). Planted/harvested acres, production, value by state and by indoor/outdoor — see Part 6 for NC figures as compiled secondarily. Aggregated statistics, not a licensee list; cannot be used to name holders.

PART 6: MARKET DATA

6.1 Hemp-derived product market size and retail counts — official first, secondary marked

Official cultivation data (USDA programme, via secondary compilation — verify against the National Hemp Report before quoting as USDA):

The compilation at https://hempcbdbusinessplans.com/north-carolina-hemp-industry/ (secondary, citing the National Hemp Report and the USDA licence search tool) reports for NC:

Year USDA-licensed growers (secondary) Planted Harvested Notable production / value (secondary)
2023 589 950 acres 220 acres Indoor/greenhouse floral ~13,000 lb produced, 11,258 lb utilised, ~$7m (17% of US indoor floral value)
2024 “held steady” (no number given) 850 acres 480 acres Flower ~10% of outdoor harvest; indoor 3,030 lb produced, 2,963 lb utilised, ~$2m
2025 — 1,150 acres (+35%) 910 acres ~47% of harvested area floral biomass; outdoor utilised 214,000 lb, $725,000; indoor floral ~30,000 lb, nearly all utilised, $18.5m (23% of US utilised indoor floral value)
2026 season 658 (USDA search tool, via compilation) — — —

All figures in this table are SECONDARY compilation, [VERIFY against USDA NASS National Hemp Report editions]. The direction (outdoor acreage collapse 2021→2023, indoor floral value concentration in NC) is plausible given the pilot-era baseline (14,562 outdoor acres in 2020, 14,200 in 2021, 1,525/1,500 growers — Hemp Industry Daily 2021 outlook table: https://hempindustrydaily.com/2021-hemp-outlook-licensed-acreage-tanks-24-in-3rd-year-of-nationwide-production/), but the compilation’s processor series (>1,700 by July 2025) is explicitly a former state registration series and must not be presented as a current processor count.

Retail market size (no official figure):

  • Advisory Council Interim Report (2 Apr 2026, official council report): NC unregulated cannabis market ~$3 billion in 2022 (Council Interim Report, p.10) (citing the 2023 U.S. Cannabis Report). Secondary summary of the same report discussion: intoxicating-hemp industry ~$1bn. Source for Council figure: https://www.cannabisbusinesstimes.com/us-states/north-carolina/news/15821437/north-carolina-governor-endorses-adultuse-cannabis-market-proposed-by-council (secondary report of the official Council report — Council PDF itself: https://www.ncdhhs.gov/advisory-council-cannabis-interim-report-2026/download?attachment — the $3 billion in 2022 (Council Interim Report, p.10)/$1bn figures should be verified against the PDF text in Run 2, not against the news summary).
  • Whitney Economics via Indy Week (secondary, 2026): intoxicating hemp ~$1.8bn retail sales in NC in 2025, implying ~$190m/year from a 10.5% excise: https://indyweek.com/news/politics/legislation/north-carolina-hemp-marijuana-regulation-lawmakers/ — $1bn (Council discussion) vs $1.8bn (Whitney) conflict [VERIFY]; different years, bases (wholesale vs retail, hemp-derived cannabinoids vs all hemp) or both may explain it, but neither source in the set reconciles them.
  • Whitney Economics via advocacy page (secondary, 2026): NC hemp business revenue $3 billion in 2022 (Council Interim Report, p.10), 16,149 jobs, $702.5m wages, $87.8m retail sales-tax revenue (Franny’s Farmacy “Save NC Hemp” page, via sub-research) — advocacy-cited, not official, and the $3 billion in 2022 (Council Interim Report, p.10) coincidence with the illicit-market figure is a trap: do not add them, do not treat either as NCDOR revenue.
  • Retail counts (official: none; secondary: Triangle only): See Part 3.7 — 81 Triangle shops in a non-official directory (Oct 2026). No statewide retail count, no official shop census, no NCDOR hemp-retailer count exists.

6.3 Cannabis/hemp-friendly banks and credit unions operating in NC

Context (fixed baseline): no federal cannabis banking law. Every provider below is therefore operating under federal BSA/AML risk tolerance, not under a safe harbour. No NC-chartered cannabis bank was found. All entries are secondary vendor/advocacy pages, [VERIFY] current NC onboarding before approaching:

Provider NC claim (secondary) Caveat
Safe Harbor Financial (national) Cannabis/hemp banking in 41 states/territories incl. NC; loans generally after ≥6 months as client, retail-oriented: https://shfinancial.org/about-us/faq ; https://shfinancial.org/post/cbd-financing-how-to-find-a-hemp-friendly-bank Secondary, dated ~2025; national programme, not NC-chartered.
Herring Bank (explicit NC hemp page) “Works with North Carolina hemp growers, processors, manufacturers, wholesalers, retailers and testing labs” for checking/savings, BSA/AML documentation (licences, COAs), armored-courier cash, payroll/ACH/wires; lists Charlotte, Raleigh, Greensboro, Durham, Winston-Salem, Fayetteville, Cary, Wilmington, Asheville, etc.: https://www.herringbank.com/business-banking/cannabis-banking/hemp-banking-north-carolina/ Vendor page; not an NC-chartered institution claim; verify whether the NC city list is branch locations or service areas.
PenFed Credit Union Secondary blog states PenFed “serves Virginia and North Carolina, providing a refinancing program for anyone, including hemp-related businesses” Weak, [VERIFY] eligibility; do not present as a cannabis programme.
Salal CU (WA), Santa Cruz Community CU (CA), Community Choice CU (MI) Advertise cannabis banking nationally NOT NC-specific; no NC branch/programme evidenced — listed only to prevent misattribution. Do not list as NC banks.
NC Cannabis Chamber of Commerce (NC3) Named in secondary tax guide as advocacy/education body Advocacy, not a bank: https://cannabiscpa.tax/north-carolina-cannabis-tax-guide-cbd-only-2025-edition/ [VERIFY]

UNC SOG Winter 2026 blog (via secondary summary) notes hemp businesses “may therefore be prohibited from using banks or credit cards” under federal risk — the practical reason NC operators rely on national specialist providers above, and why Great Smoky takes cash/debit only, no credit cards (secondary).

INTERIM RESEARCH NOTES — Run 1 gaps, unverified items, conflicts, follow-ups

(Full combined Research Notes covering Runs 1 + 2 will be compiled at the end of Run 2. This interim list is not skipped: every item below must be carried into Run 2 or closed before the dossier is relied on.)

ID Gap / conflict / unverified item Why it matters Recommended follow-up source
R1-01 Art. 5G repeal conflict: H766 contemplated repealing the Epilepsy Alternative Treatment Act in 2021; secondary summaries state Art. 5G was repealed eff. 1 July 2021, yet §90-94.1 remains in the current Code and DHHS registry descriptions persist in secondary sources. Current status of the caregiver-card registry is unknown. Determines whether a patient can today obtain a DHHS card, or whether §90-94.1 is a possession exemption without a live registry. Open current ncleg library text for §§90-113.100–90-113.106 and §90-94.1; contact NCDHHS (Art. 5G programme) for a current-status letter.
R1-02 G.S. 90-95(d)/(h) pinpoint text not opened line-by-line. Possession tiers, hashish/synthetic tiers, trafficking tiers, Schedule VI sale/manufacture class, 1,000-ft school elevation, and prior-conviction enhancements rest on secondary summaries of the statute, not on an opened library PDF. Core criminal penalties must be verbatim before anyone relies on them. Open library PDFs for G.S. 90-95 (full section) and G.S. 15A-1340.17 / 15A-1340.23 grids; reconcile the Class I felony sentencing range (3–4 to 13–14 months vs 3–12 months in different summaries).
R1-03 HB 328 conference text not opened in full. Conference-report provisions (total-THC formula incl. 0.877 THCA factor, 0.4 mg/container cap, synthetic ban, civil-penalty tiers $2,500–$25,000 / $10,000–$50,000, kratom/xylazine, effective dates) rest on secondary summaries of the conference report, not on the conference PDF. HB 328 is the live bill most likely to change NC law in 2026; its exact text decides whether full-spectrum CBD survives. Open the H328 conference committee substitute (H328-PCCS30639-CE-1 as cited secondarily) from ncleg.gov; check House Rules status on the day of Run 2.
R1-04 HB 607 / SB 265 / SB 59 fee schedules and testing text not opened in full. Manufacturer/distributor/retailer fee ranges ($250–$15,000; $15,000/$5,000) and testing (ISO 17025 / DEA / HPLC) details rest on secondary summaries / LRS summaries. Licence-cost modelling for a future Ch. 18D depends on the filed text, not summaries. Open HB 607 v1, SB 265 v1, SB 59 v2 PDFs from ncleg.gov; check LRS status for SB 59 (pending vs dead unknown in Run 1).
R1-05 Delta-8 / converted cannabinoids: no NC court decision or AG opinion located. The “legal as a matter of state law” reading rests on the §90-94(2) text + UNC SOG Dixon summary, not on a binding authority. The single largest legal ambiguity for NC hemp retail. Search NC appellate opinions + AG opinions for “delta-8” / “tetrahydrocannabinols found in hemp”; check SOG Winter 2026 blog full text (https://nccriminallaw.sog.unc.edu/2026/01/14/winter-2026-cannabis-update/).
R1-06 No NC official response to PL 119-37 / PL 119-103 or the 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 dates located. No executive order, NCDA&CS, NCDOR, ALE or Governor guidance found. Businesses cannot plan on a state grace period or state enforcement posture that may not exist. Re-check governor.nc.gov, ncagr.gov, ncdor.gov, dps.nc.gov/ALE in Run 2, immediately before 11 Dec 2026; check Advisory Council final report (due 31 Dec 2026) when published.
R1-07 Whether EBCI tribal medical licences count as “state-licensed” under the federal Schedule III rule: [VERIFY]. No federal/NC statement located. Decides whether EBCI medical activity gets federal §280E relief / Schedule III treatment. Check the 91 FR 22714 rule text + DEA/IRS guidance for “state-licensed” definition and any tribal-licence discussion; check EBCI CCB / federal-tribal correspondence if published.
R1-08 EBCI tribal cannabis tax rate conflict: 7.5% (historic Code / Ord. 54) vs 9% (Oct 2026 press). Revenue >$5m in 2026 is enterprise-reported via press, not audited. Tax modelling and revenue claims for Cherokee depend on the rate. Open current Cherokee Code §105 + any cannabis-tax ordinance from ebci-ccb.org / tribal code library; ask Tribal Treasury / CCB for the codified rate.
R1-09 EBCI purchase limits conflict: 1 oz/day (dispensary operational, secondary) vs 1.5 oz/transaction (Ord. No. 63 OCR, ambiguous per-transaction vs per-day). Concentrate caps (2,500 mg/day, 10,000 mg/month) agree across sources. Visitor guidance (“what can I buy?”) cannot be given as a single number until reconciled. Open clean (non-OCR) Ord. No. 63 text + CCB FAQ page; confirm current point-of-sale limits with Great Smoky / CCB.
R1-10 EBCI CCB fee schedule, full licence-type list, private-licence availability, seed-to-sale vendor: [UNAVAILABLE] in Run 1. Run 2 Part 10 cannot be completed without the CCB licence pages. Open every CCB page (licences, agent cards, patient cards, FAQs, applications) from https://ebci-ccb.org/; open Cherokee Code Chs. 17/17A in full.
R1-11 NC hemp licensee count: no official count extracted. USDA FOIA PDF (updated 14 Apr 2026) not tallied; Public Search Tool not queried by state. Secondary counts (589 in 2023; 658 in 2026) are not AMS totals. Market-size and “how many growers” claims need an AMS count. Download FOIAUSDAHempLicensees.pdf, filter NC, tally by licence status; run Public Search Tool NC queries; record extraction date.
R1-12 NCDHHS epilepsy registry count: [UNAVAILABLE]. No DHHS-published count located; see R1-01. Prevents any “X patients” claim for NC medical law. DHHS Art. 5G programme / public-records request if the registry still exists; otherwise record as “no published count, registry status [VERIFY]”.
R1-13 ALE hemp enforcement data: [UNAVAILABLE]. No dataset, annual report or hemp-specific enforcement breakout located. Enforcement risk cannot be quantified officially. Check ALE annual reports / DPS data dashboards; check district-attorney / SBI lab-testing data for hemp-vs-marijuana distinctions if published.
R1-14 Local UDO “none found” entries are search-limited, not proof. Charlotte, Greensboro, Durham, Winston-Salem, Fayetteville (final adoption), Wilmington (city), Asheville/Buncombe, plus Harnett/Wilson final texts, all need full-text UDO/code checks. Same-name traps (Fayetteville WV, Wilmington DE, Greenville NC, Stallings) must be re-checked on every citation. A missed local buffer is a site-selection error. Full-text search each UDO/code (Charlotte UDO, Raleigh UDO, Greensboro LDO, Durham UDO, Winston-Salem/Forsyth UDO, Fayetteville UDO, Wilmington LDC, Asheville Code, Wake UDO use table, Harnett UDO §§1.2/7.6.7 enrolled text, Wilson UDO).
R1-15 Sales-tax details [VERIFY]: NCDOR current-rates table URL returned 404 in sub-research; Mecklenburg 8.25% rests on a press-release snippet; Wake 7.25% / Durham-Orange 7.50% rest on secondary summaries; corporate income-tax 2026 rate conflict (2.25% vs 2%); §280E non-decoupling rests on secondary guide + absence from decoupling lists checked, not on an opened §§105-153.5/105-130.5 text. Till rates and tax modelling must be exact. Federal baseline — fixed context (not re-researched)

As instructed, the following is taken as fixed context for this run. Anything found in North Carolina sources that appeared to contradict it is flagged in the notes; no contradiction on the federal dates themselves was found in NC official sources opened for this run:

  • Split scheduling: state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714). All adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I. The broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is not final.
  • IRS §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; adult-use remains fully subject to §280E.
  • Hemp: PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026, but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026. After 11 December 2026, the full total-THC hemp definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply.
  • USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp is delayed to 31 December 2026.
  • The federal medical marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026.
  • There is no federal cannabis banking law.

North Carolina consequence — stated plainly, unchanged from Run 1: North Carolina has no state-licensed medical cannabis programme. Marijuana therefore remains Schedule I in North Carolina under the split scheduling, except for the narrow hemp-extract exemption in G.S. 90-94.1 (which is not a medical marijuana programme) and except, potentially, for activity on EBCI tribal land. Whether tribal medical licences issued by the Eastern Band of Cherokee Indians count as "state-licensed" under the federal Schedule III rule is [VERIFY] — still unresolved after Run 2 (see Combined Notes, R1-07).

PART 7 — HEMP LICENCES

7.1 Headline answer

North Carolina today has NO state hemp licence of any kind — not for growers, processors, handlers, or retailers. There is no North Carolina hemp licence to apply for, no state hemp licence fee to pay, and no state hemp registry to join. That is the whole answer in one sentence, and everything in this Part is the evidence and the consequences of it.

The only hemp production licence available to a person growing hemp in North Carolina (outside EBCI tribal trust land, see §7.9) is the federal USDA hemp producer licence under the USDA Domestic Hemp Production Program (DHPP), 7 CFR Part 990, Subpart C. USDA licences producers only. It does not create a USDA processor licence, handler licence, or retailer licence. Processing, handling after delivery for further processing, distribution, and retail sale in North Carolina are, in current law, unlicensed at state level as hemp activities — subject only to (a) the general business, tax, food, zoning, and other non-hemp registrations in §7.8, (b) generally applicable criminal law if the material is in fact marijuana (delta-9 THC >0.3% dry weight), and (c) the PROPOSED ONLY Chapter 18D licensing scheme in HB 328 / HB 607 / SB 265 (§7.7), none of which is law.

NCDA&CS states the position itself, in its own words, on its "Hemp in NC" page (search-result text from ncagr.gov, Accessed 2026-10-24):

"The original North Carolina industrial hemp law, which created Article 50E of Chapter 106 in the General Statutes, established the state's hemp pilot program and regulatory framework for hemp cultivation. This article was repealed effective June 30, 2022 and the governance of hemp cultivation in North Carolina transferred to the United State Department of Agriculture (USDA) Agriculture Marketing Service (AMS)."

And, on processor registration specifically (same page, search-result text, Accessed 2026-10-24):

"Furthermore, the North Carolina Department of Agriculture and Consumer Services (NCDA&CS) no longer offers registration services for hemp processors. This change is due to the fact that the State of North Carolina no longer possesses the regulatory authority to oversee or administer a regulatory system for the licensing or regulation of hemp producers and processors. As a result, individuals or entities seeking to engage in hemp processing must now refer to federal guidelines outside of the state Department of Agriculture's authority."

NCDA&CS also gives the USDA contact route on that page: information at www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/information-producers, email farmbill.hemp@usda.gov, telephone 202-720-2491 (search-result text, Accessed 2026-10-24; the email and telephone are USDA public programme contacts, not personal contacts). The NCDA&CS page itself could not be opened directly in this run [VERIFY — page-open failed; wording above is search-result text from ncagr.gov].

7.2 The former North Carolina system — what existed, and when it ended

7.2.1 Creation and scale

  • The North Carolina Industrial Hemp Pilot Program was initiated by the federal Agricultural Act of 2014 and North Carolina legislation via S.L. 2015-299 (the Industrial Hemp Bill). Source: NCDA&CS "Hemp in NC" page, search-result text, Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • NCDA&CS states: "North Carolina farmers have been cultivating industrial hemp since 2017" and "the pilot program grew to over 1,500 licensed producers." Source: same page, Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • A contemporaneous NCDA&CS press release (16 August 2021) states that letters would soon be sent to "the 1,500 licensed hemp producers in the state" notifying them of the change, and quotes Phil Wilson, director of the NCDA&CS Plant Industry Division, as saying the Division would "extend the licenses of growers who will need to renew between now and Jan. 1, 2022 to ensure there is no lapse in them having a valid license." Source: NCDA&CS press release, "N.C. will wrap up hemp pilot program as federal regulatory program takes effect," 16 Aug 2021, via search result, Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • A secondary account (Kaufman & Canoles, "North Carolina Eliminates Hemp Pilot Program") adds that there were "almost 1,300 registered hemp processors" under the former pilot, and "more than 6.8 million square feet of greenhouse space and over 14,000 acres of farmland" among licensed growers. Source: kaufcan.com, secondary, Accessed 2026-10-24. These processor and acreage figures are secondary only and are not confirmed from an NCDA&CS page opened in this run [VERIFY if relied on for a commercial decision].

7.2.2 End date — two dates that are both correct, for different things

This is the point on which secondary summaries most often blur the record. Both dates in the locked baseline are correct, but they mark different events:

Date Event Evidence
31 December 2021 The pilot programme expired / pilot licences ceased to be valid. "Hemp licenses issued by the NC pilot program will remain valid through December 31, 2021. Beginning in 2022, growers will need to apply directly to the USDA for licenses." Kaufman & Canoles summary, Accessed 2026-10-24; NC State Extension, 5 Jan 2022: "As of January 1, 2022 all hemp growers in North Carolina must be licensed through the USDA National Hemp Program. The North Carolina Industrial Hemp Pilot Program … ended on December 31, 2021. If you had a NC Industrial Hemp Commission issued hemp growers license, it is no longer valid." Source: hemp.ces.ncsu.edu, Accessed 2026-10-24.
1 January 2022 USDA licensing became the only route. "North Carolina will fall under [the USDA Domestic Hemp Production Program] beginning Jan. 1, 2022." NCDA&CS press release, 16 Aug 2021, Accessed 2026-10-24.
30 June 2022 Article 50E itself was repealed. "This article was repealed effective June 30, 2022." NCDA&CS "Hemp in NC" page, Accessed 2026-10-24.

The six-month gap between pilot expiry (31 Dec 2021) and statutory repeal (30 Jun 2022) is therefore not a conflict: the programme stopped issuing/renewing effective licences at the end of 2021, and the enabling Article was formally repealed six months later. Any source that gives only one of these dates without saying which event it marks is incomplete, not wrong.

Historical note on the repeal path [VERIFY in pinpoint]: Earlier legislative history complicates the timeline. S.L. 2019 materials (Farm Act of 2019–20, S.315) at one stage provided for repeal of Article 50E effective 1 July 2021 and its replacement by a new Article 50F administered by the Department rather than the Commission. The NCDA&CS current page's statement — repeal effective 30 June 2022 — is the operative end-state and is what this Part relies on. The intermediate 2021 repeal/replacement proposals and whether Article 50F ever took effect as a live licensing article before the 2022 repeal were not fully traced in this run and should not be asserted without opening the session laws [VERIFY]. What is certain from NCDA&CS's own current statement is that today there is no live Article 50E or successor state hemp licensing article.

7.2.3 Former pilot fees (historical only — NOT current fees)

For the record, and clearly labelled as defunct:

  • Former G.S. 106-568.53 (as reproduced in 2019 bill text) provided for: "An annual fee that is the sum of two hundred fifty dollars ($250.00) and two dollars ($2.00) per acre of industrial hemp cultivated." Source: S.315 bill text via webservices.ncleg.net, search result, Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • The Commission could "create fair and reasonable licensing preferences for license applicants from North Carolina counties that have been recognized as economically depressed or disadvantaged" in setting fees. Source: same text, Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • Do not quote $250 + $2/acre as a current fee. No current North Carolina hemp fee exists, because no current North Carolina hemp licence exists. The current USDA licence fee is $0 (see §7.5.7).

7.2.4 Former pilot regulator

  • Regulator: the North Carolina Industrial Hemp Commission, with licensing, inspection, and regulation overseen by the NCDA&CS Plant Industry Division. Source: NCDA&CS press release, 16 Aug 2021, Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • The Commission is defunct with the pilot. Do not direct a current applicant to the Commission. The current NCDA&CS page is informational only; it does not issue licences.
