7.4 What "no processor/handler/retailer licence" does and does not mean

This distinction matters commercially and is frequently misstated:

  1. It does NOT mean processing or retail sale is prohibited. Hemp (≤0.3% delta-9 THC dry weight) is excluded from the North Carolina definition of marijuana (G.S. 90-87, as amended by S.L. 2022-32) and from Schedule VI tetrahydrocannabinols (G.S. 90-94). Lawful hemp may therefore be possessed, processed, transported, and sold without a hemp-specific state licence, subject to generally applicable law.
  2. It does NOT mean the activity is unregulated. A processor or retailer is still subject to: general business formation/registration (§7.8), sales-tax registration and collection (§7.8), local zoning and any local business permit (§7.8), food-safety law if the product is a food (§7.8), FDA/FTC constraints on drug/disease claims (federal, outside this Part's scope), and criminal law if any lot exceeds the hemp threshold.
  3. It does NOT mean a USDA producer licence authorises processing by someone else. The USDA licence authorises the named licensee to produce hemp at the listed locations. A non-licensee who buys compliant hemp biomass and extracts it is not "licensed" by USDA for that extraction — but neither does North Carolina currently require them to be. The gap is a regulatory gap, not a prohibition, in current law. PROPOSED Chapter 18D would close it for consumable products (§7.7).
  4. Transport: No NC hemp transport licence, manifest, or checkpoint system was located in current law. The practical compliance document for transport remains the producer licence, the lot's passing test result, and FSA lot registration — not a state transport permit [VERIFY — no NC transport rule located; do not invent one].

7.5 The USDA Domestic Hemp Production Program in detail (7 CFR Part 990)

7.5.1 Legal basis and why North Carolina falls under the USDA plan

  • Statute: Agriculture Improvement Act of 2018 (2018 Farm Bill), 7 U.S.C. §§1639o–1639s, directing USDA to establish the DHPP.
  • Regulation: 7 CFR Part 990, final rule 86 FR 5680 (19 January 2021). Search-result text confirms the Part 990 framework and the Subpart C USDA plan.
  • 7 CFR 990.20(a) (verbatim, eCFR via search result, Accessed 2026-10-24):

"General hemp production requirements. The production of hemp in a State or territory of an Indian Tribe where there is no USDA approved State or Tribal plan must be conducted in accordance with this subpart, provided that the production of hemp is not prohibited by the State or territory of an Indian Tribe where production will occur."

  • North Carolina has no USDA-approved State plan. AMS's "List of USDA-approved Hemp Plans," updated 17 February 2026, lists North Carolina as "USDA Hemp Producer License" — i.e. USDA issues the licence directly — while neighbouring South Carolina and Virginia are listed with approved State plans. Source: AMS plan-review page, search-result text, Accessed 2026-10-24. AMS plan-review page: https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review — page-open failed in this run [VERIFY]; the listing itself is confirmed by search-result text from that page.
  • North Carolina does not prohibit hemp production. The §990.20(a) proviso is therefore satisfied.

7.5.2 Definitions that control the licence (7 CFR 990.1)

Verbatim or near-verbatim from 7 CFR 990.1 (Cornell LII / eCFR search-result text, Accessed 2026-10-24):

  • "Hemp" — "The plant species Cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant, including the seeds thereof and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of not more than 0.3 percent on a dry weight basis."
  • "Key participants" — "A sole proprietor, a partner in partnership, or a person with executive managerial control in a corporation. A person with executive managerial control includes persons such as a chief executive officer, chief operating officer, and chief financial officer. This definition does not include non-executive managers such as farm, field, or shift managers." (Tribal-government leadership acting in that capacity is also excluded, except when exercising executive managerial control over hemp production.)
  • "Lot" — "A contiguous area in a field, greenhouse, or indoor growing structure containing the same variety or strain of cannabis throughout the area." The term also maps to FSA "farm," "tract," "field," and "subfield" as used in 7 CFR 718.2.
  • "Handle" — "To harvest or store hemp plants or hemp plant parts prior to the delivery of such plants or plant parts for further processing." "Handle" also includes disposal of cannabis plants that are not hemp for chemical analysis and disposal purposes.
  • "Acceptable hemp THC level" — the level defined by reference to the 0.3% threshold and the measurement of uncertainty in §990.25 (see §7.6.3).

Practical consequence: Only key participants need FBI criminal-history reports (see §7.5.4). A farm manager, field manager, or shift manager is not a key participant and does not trigger a report by that role alone — but if that person also holds executive managerial control (e.g. is also a partner or officer), they are a key participant in that capacity.

7.5.3 Who must be licensed — no small-grower or personal-use exception

7 CFR 990.21(a)(1) (verbatim, Cornell LII, Accessed 2026-10-24):

"Requirements and license application. Any person producing or intending to produce hemp must have a valid license prior to producing hemp. A valid license means the license is unexpired, unsuspended, and unrevoked."

There is no de minimis, backyard, personal-use, or single-plant exception in the USDA plan. A person growing one hemp plant in North Carolina without a USDA licence is producing without a licence. NC State Extension states the same consequence in plain terms (5 Jan 2022, Accessed 2026-10-24): "You should not have any hemp growing, in the field, greenhouse, or grow-room, at this time unless you have a USDA hemp production license."

7.5.4 Application — what must be filed (7 CFR 990.21(a))

  • When: "Applicants may submit an application for a license at any time." — §990.21(a)(2).
  • Contact information (§990.21(a)(3)(i)): Full name, residential address, telephone number, and email address. For a business entity: full business name, principal business location address, full name and title of the key participants, title, email address (if available), and employer identification number (EIN).
  • Land information: The application must also cover the production locations. Under §990.23 and the HeMP/FSA system, this means street address and, to the extent practicable, geospatial location for each lot, greenhouse, building, or site, plus acreage / indoor square footage and the licence number (see §7.5.6). The search-result text for §990.21 itself emphasises contact + criminal-history information; the land-description requirement is imposed through §§990.3/990.23 and the application form in HeMP. Applicants should expect to supply legal descriptions / geospatial coordinates for every field, greenhouse, and indoor facility — do not file an application that lists only a mailing address [VERIFY — exact HeMP field list not opened in this run].
  • Criminal history report (§990.21(a)(3)(ii)) (verbatim in substance, Cornell LII, Accessed 2026-10-24):

"A current criminal history report for an individual, or if the applicant is a business entity, all key participants, dated within 60 days of the application submission date. A license application will not be considered complete without all required criminal history reports."

  • In practice this is the FBI Identity History Summary (fingerprint-based). NC State Extension (5 Jan 2022, Accessed 2026-10-24) confirms: "the need for a FBI Identity History Summary (criminal background check) and fingerprinting." The report must be dated within 60 days of submission — an older report does not complete the application.
  • Incomplete applications: "Applications missing required information shall be returned to the applicant as incomplete. The applicant may resubmit a completed application." — §990.21(a)(5).
  • Submission route: Via HeMP. AMS states (HeMP page, search-result text, Accessed 2026-10-24): "As a USDA licensed producer, you will use HeMP to submit and manage your license and reporting online, replacing the need to download forms and submit them via email, mail, or fax. Through HeMP you will: Apply for a hemp license under USDA; Renew, amend, and print your hemp license; Submit disposal/remediation reports (AMS-27) and annual reports (AMS-28); View hemp testing lab results and registered FSA lots."

7.5.5 The 10-year felony bar (7 CFR 990.20(b) and 990.22(a)(2))

7 CFR 990.20(b) (verbatim, eCFR search-result text, Accessed 2026-10-24):

"Convicted felon ban. A person with a State or Federal felony conviction relating to a controlled substance is subject to a 10-year ineligibility restriction on participating in and producing hemp under the USDA plan from the date of the conviction. An exception applies to a person who was lawfully growing hemp under section 7606 of the Agricultural Act of 2014 (7 U.S.C. 5940) before December 20, 2018, and whose conviction also occurred before that date."

7 CFR 990.22(a)(2) restates the approval condition: a licence shall not be issued unless "the criminal history report(s) submitted with the license application confirms that all key participants to be covered by the license have not been convicted of a felony, under State or Federal law, relating to a controlled substance within the past ten (10) years unless the exception in § 990.20(b) applies."

Key points:

  • The bar runs from the date of conviction, not the offence date and not the end of sentence (on the face of the regulation).
  • It applies to every key participant, not only the named applicant. One barred key participant bars the entity unless that person is removed from a key-participant role.
  • The only exception is the narrow 2014-pilot grandfather: lawfully growing under the 2014 Farm Bill pilot before 20 December 2018 AND convicted before that same date. Both limbs must be satisfied.
  • Falsification: §990.20(c): "Any person who materially falsifies any information contained in an application for a license under the USDA plan shall be ineligible to participate in the USDA plan." Material falsification is therefore a standalone ineligibility, not merely a denial ground.
  • Stand-ins: §990.22(a)(6): no licence if "the applicant is applying for a license as a stand-in for someone whose license has been suspended, revoked, or is otherwise ineligible to participate."

7.5.6 Licence approval, term, transfer, renewal, and modification (7 CFR 990.22, 990.21(b)–(c))

Approval conditions (§990.22(a), Cornell LII, Accessed 2026-10-24) — a licence shall not be issued unless all of the following hold:

  1. The application is complete and accurate;
  2. Criminal-history reports confirm no disqualifying felony within 10 years (subject to the §990.20(b) exception);
  3. The applicant, if previously or currently licensed, submitted all reports required by Part 990;
  4. No materially false statements or misrepresentations in this or any prior application;
  5. The applicant's licence is not currently suspended (if currently licensed);
  6. The applicant is not a stand-in for a suspended/revoked/ineligible person;
  7. The production State/Tribe does not have a USDA-approved plan and is not awaiting a USDA decision on a submitted plan (for North Carolina, this condition is met because NC has no plan and is under the USDA plan);
  8. The production State/Tribe does not prohibit hemp production.

Term (§990.21(a)(6) / §990.22(b)(2)) (verbatim):

"USDA-issued hemp producer licenses shall be valid until December 31 of the year three years after the year in which license was issued."

Worked example (arithmetic, not a separate rule): a licence issued at any point in calendar year 2026 is valid until 31 December 2029. A licence issued in 2024 is valid until 31 December 2027. Do not describe USDA licences as simply "three years from issuance" without the 31-December anchor — the anchor is what the regulation says.

Non-transferability (§990.22(b)(3)) (verbatim):

"Licenses may not be sold, assigned, transferred, pledged, or otherwise disposed of, alienated or encumbered."

A sale of a hemp business does not transfer the USDA licence. The buyer must obtain their own licence before producing. Any Part 8 business sale that implies a licence transfers with the business is misstated to that extent (see §8.5).

Denial and appeal: USDA must give written notification of approval or denial; a denial notification must explain the reason; the applicant may appeal under Subpart D of Part 990. §990.22(b)(4).

Renewal (§990.21(b)): "USDA hemp producer licenses must be renewed prior to license expiration. Licenses are not automatically renewed. Applications for renewal shall be subject to the same terms, information collection requirements, and approval criteria as provided in this subpart for initial applications unless there has been an amendment to the regulations in this part or the law since approval of the initial or last application." In practice, a fresh criminal-history report within 60 days will be required on renewal, because renewal is subject to the same information-collection requirements — applicants should not assume a prior report carries over [VERIFY — renewal form field list not opened in this run].

Modification (§990.21(c)): "A license modification is required if there is any change to the information submitted in the application including, but not limited to, sale of a business, the production of hemp in a new location, or a change in the key participants under a license." A new growing location, a new key participant, or a sale of the business therefore requires a modification before the changed production occurs, not after.

Multiple licences (§990.22(c)): "If the applicant is producing in more than one State or territory of an Indian Tribe, the applicant may have more than one license to grow hemp. If the applicant has operations in a location covered under a State or Tribal plan, that operation must be licensed under the State or Tribal plan, not the USDA plan." For North Carolina this matters at the EBCI boundary: production on EBCI tribal trust land under the EBCI approved plan must be licensed under that plan, not under the USDA plan (see §7.9).

7.5.7 Fees — the $0 licence and the real costs

  • USDA licence fee: $0. NC State Extension (5 Jan 2022, Accessed 2026-10-24) states: "There is, however, no fee for the license itself." The Greenhouse Product News summary of the same transition states the same. No USDA licence-application fee, annual fee, or per-acre fee was located in Part 990.
  • Costs the grower does pay (not licence fees):
  • FBI Identity History Summary / fingerprinting — market cost, typically tens of dollars per key participant; no USDA-published fee was located in this run [VERIFY — do not quote a single FBI fee figure as current].
  • USDA-certified sampling agent — selected and paid by the grower (NC State Extension, 5 Jan 2022, Accessed 2026-10-24: "growers will have to select and pay for a USDA-certified sampling agent to collect their compliance samples").
  • Laboratory testing — selected and paid by the grower ("Growers will also select the lab for those samples to be sent to and pay for the lab testing" — same source).
  • Contrast with the former NC fee and the PROPOSED Chapter 18D fees: The former $250 + $2/acre pilot fee (§7.2.3) and the proposed $250–$25,000 Chapter 18D fees (§7.7) must never be presented as current USDA fees. The USDA licence is free; the compliance costs are sampling and testing.

7.5.8 FSA acreage reporting (7 CFR 990.23)

7 CFR 990.23 (eCFR search-result text, Accessed 2026-10-24):

"All USDA licensees shall report hemp crop acreage to FSA within 30 days of hemp been [sic — 'being'] planted and shall provide, at a minimum, the following information: (a) Street address and, to the extent practicable, geospatial location of the lot, greenhouse, building, or site where hemp will be produced. All locations where hemp is produced must be reported to FSA. (b) Acreage dedicated to the production of hemp, or greenhouse or indoor square footage dedicated to the production of hemp. (c) The hemp license number."

Practical steps (from the AMS-28 form instructions and NC State Extension, Accessed 2026-10-24):

  • Register the growing location with the local Farm Service Agency (FSA) office to obtain farm / tract / field / subfield numbers. AMS-28 instructions: "Each licensed producer must register the growing location with FSA to receive these numbers."
  • Report within 30 days of planting — not at harvest, not at year-end.
  • Report every location. An unreported greenhouse or field is an unreported production location even if the licence itself is valid.
  • The FSA report form is FSA-578 (Report of Acreage), referenced in the federal information-collection notice (see §7.5.10).

7.5.9 Research production

7 CFR 990.21(d) (search-result text, Accessed 2026-10-24):

  • Producers producing hemp for research must still obtain a USDA licence.
  • Hemp produced for research that does not enter the stream of commerce is not subject to the sampling requirements in §§990.24 and 990.26, provided the producer adopts and carries out a USDA-approved alternative sampling method that has the potential to ensure, at a 95% confidence level, that the Cannabis sativa L. subject to the method will not test above the acceptable hemp THC level.
  • Research licensees must still ensure disposal of all non-compliant plants under §990.27, and comply with reporting in §990.71 including disposal reporting.
  • Only research institutions registered with DEA to handle marijuana may keep hemp that tests over 0.3% until the end of the study.

There is no separate North Carolina research licence. A university or private researcher in North Carolina uses the same USDA licence route, with the research alternative-sampling provision above.

7.5.10 Ongoing reporting — AMS-27, AMS-28, and audits

Report / obligation Who files Deadline / trigger Contents (minimum) Source (Accessed 2026-10-24)
FSA acreage report (FSA-578) Every USDA licensee Within 30 days of planting Street address + geospatial location (to extent practicable) for each lot/greenhouse/building/site; acreage or indoor sq ft; licence number 7 CFR 990.23; federal information-collection notice, Jan 2026 (regulations.justia.com), Accessed 2026-10-24
Disposal / remediation report (AMS-27) USDA licensee disposing of or remediating non-compliant plants No later than 30 days after the disposal/remediation date listed on the form (per the information-collection notice) Producer name/address, licence number, location information including geospatial location or other land descriptor for the production area subject to disposal, information on the agent handling disposal, date disposal was completed Federal information-collection notice, Jan 2026, Accessed 2026-10-24; AMS HeMP page confirms AMS-27 is filed via HeMP

Audits / record retention (7 CFR 990.28) USDA licensee (subject to audit) Audit verifications may be performed once every 3 years unless otherwise determined by USDA; audit may be remote or in person and may cover current and previous crop years, including site visits to farms, fields, greenhouses, storage facilities, or other affiliated locations Records and documentation relating to hemp production; licensees must maintain records for a minimum of 3 years (secondary summary of §990.28 — pinpoint [VERIFY]) 7 CFR 990.28 via govinfo/eCFR search-result text, Accessed 2026-10-24; Holland & Knight summary for the 3-year retention figure

Do not confuse AMS-27/AMS-28 with State/Tribal forms: AMS-23/24 (monthly State/Tribal reports) and AMS-25 (annual State/Tribal report) are filed by State/Tribal governments, not by North Carolina producers. A North Carolina USDA licensee files AMS-27 and AMS-28 (and FSA-578), not AMS-23/24/25.

7.6 Sampling, testing, harvest, and disposal — the compliance chain that IS the licence in practice

A USDA licence is not a permission to grow and then test at leisure. The licence is conditional on a strict pre-harvest chain. Failure at any link can make the lot unsaleable even if the licence itself remains valid.

7.6.1 Pre-harvest sampling (7 CFR 990.24)

7 CFR 990.24 (verbatim, Cornell LII, Accessed 2026-10-24). USDA licensees must:

"(a) No more than 30 days prior to the anticipated harvest of cannabis plants, have a sampling agent collect samples from the cannabis plant for total delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration level testing."

"(b) Have samples collected from the flowering tops of the plant by cutting the top five to eight inches from the 'main stem' (that includes the leaves and flowers), 'terminal bud' (that occurs at the end of a stem), 'or 'central cola' (cut stem that could develop into a bud) of the flowering top of the plant. Sampling guidelines and training requirements for sampling agents are available from USDA. The method used for sampling must be sufficient at a confidence level of 95 percent that no more than one percent (1%) of the plants in the lot would exceed the acceptable hemp THC level. The method used for sampling must ensure that a representative sample is collected that represents a homogeneous composition of the lot."

"(c) Have an authorized representative of the USDA licensee present at the growing site during a scheduled sample collection, if possible."

"(d) Ensure that sampling agents are provided with complete and unrestricted access during business hours to all hemp and other cannabis plants, (whether growing or harvested), all hemp production and storage areas, all land, buildings, and other structures used for the cultivation, handling, and storage of all hemp and other cannabis plants, and all locations listed in the producer license."

"(e) Not harvest the cannabis crop prior to samples being taken."

"(f) Use post-harvest samples only for remediated biomass."

Additional rules from §990.3(a)(2) (for State/Tribal plans, but stating the USDA principle): "Producers may not collect samples from their own growing facilities." The grower therefore cannot self-sample for compliance. The sampling agent must be USDA-certified/trained: AMS HeMP page (search-result text, Accessed 2026-10-24): "As a Sampling Agent, you will use HeMP to receive your Sampling Agent certificate and ID number and report Sampling Events to USDA."

Timing trap: The sample must be taken no more than 30 days before anticipated harvest, AND the crop must then be harvested no later than 30 days after sample collection (§7.6.2). The two 30-day windows are different windows. Missing the second window requires a second pre-harvest sample.

7.6.2 Harvest deadline and lot integrity (7 CFR 990.26)

7 CFR 990.26 (verbatim, eCFR/Cornell search-result text, Accessed 2026-10-24):

"(a) The producer shall harvest the crop no later than thirty (30) days after the date of sample collection." "(b) If the producer fails to complete harvest within thirty (30) days of sample collection, a second pre-harvest sample of the lot shall be required to be submitted for testing." "(c) Harvested lots of hemp plants shall not be commingled with other harvested lots or other material." "(d) Lots that meet the acceptable hemp THC level may enter the stream of commerce." "(e) Lots that do not meet the acceptable hemp THC level are subject to § 990.27." "(f) Any producer may request additional pre-harvest testing if it is believed that the original total delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration level test results were in error. Additional testing may be conducted by the laboratory that conducted the initial test, or another laboratory."

7.6.3 Testing standard — total delta-9 THC, measurement of uncertainty, and the DEA-lab delay

  • Test analyte: Total delta-9 THC concentration, determined by post-decarboxylation methods or by applying the conversion formula. The formula in Part 990 is: Total THC = delta-9 THC + (THCA × 0.877). The 0.877 factor accounts for mass lost when THCA decarboxylates to THC.
  • Acceptable level and measurement of uncertainty (§990.25): A sample test result exceeding the acceptable hemp THC level is conclusive evidence the lot is non-compliant — subject to the measurement of uncertainty. Under the final-rule framework, a result is treated as compliant if 0.3% falls within the reported measurement-of-uncertainty range (typically a 95% confidence range). The exact §990.25 text was not fully reproduced in the search results in this run; the measurement-of-uncertainty principle is stated in secondary summaries of §990.25 (Arnold & Porter, Holland & Knight) and should be confirmed against the current eCFR text before a commercial reliance [VERIFY — §990.25 pinpoint].
  • DEA-registered laboratory requirement and its delay — read carefully:
  • The printed §990.25 text states: "After December 31, 2022, USDA licensees may only use laboratories registered with the DEA to conduct testing under this section." Source: eCFR search-result text, Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • But USDA has repeatedly delayed enforcement of that requirement. The fixed federal baseline for this run states the delay runs to 31 December 2026. A secondary summary (LegalClarity, Accessed 2026-10-24) states the same: "The USDA has delayed enforcement of the requirement that all hemp testing occur at DEA-registered laboratories until December 31, 2026, meaning non-DEA labs can still perform compliance testing in the interim."
  • Therefore: Do not tell a North Carolina grower in October 2026 that only a DEA-registered lab may test their crop, and do not tell them the DEA requirement does not exist. The correct statement is: the CFR text requires DEA registration after 31 Dec 2022, but USDA enforcement of that requirement is delayed to 31 Dec 2026. What happens on 1 January 2027 if the delay is not extended again is [VERIFY — no post-2026 enforcement statement located in this run].
  • Federal forward risk (not current NC law): After the P.L. 119-37 / P.L. 119-103 dates (11 Dec 2026), the federal hemp definition itself changes to total THC with a 0.4 mg per-container cap for finished products (fixed baseline). That is a federal product-definition change, not a change to the §990.25 testing method for the crop, but it will affect whether a compliant crop's finished product remains "hemp" federally. Do not conflate the two.

7.6.4 Non-compliant lots — disposal and remediation (7 CFR 990.27)

7 CFR 990.27 (verbatim in substance, eCFR search-result text, Accessed 2026-10-24):

"(a) Cannabis plants exceeding the acceptable hemp THC level constitute marijuana, a schedule I controlled substance under the Controlled Substances Act (CSA), 21 U.S.C. 801 et seq., and producers must either use a DEA-registered reverse distributor or law enforcement to dispose of non-compliant plants or ensure the disposal of such cannabis plant on site at the farm or hemp production facility." "(b) Producers must notify USDA of their intent to dispose of or remediate non-conforming plants and verify disposal or remediation by submitting required documentation." "(c) If a producer elects to perform remediation activities, an additional sampling and testing of the post-remediated crop must occur to determine THC concentration levels."

In practice, USDA's on-farm disposal flexibilities (plowing under, composting into green manure for use on the same land, tilling, disking, burial, burning) and remediation by (i) disposing of flower material and salvaging the remainder, or (ii) blending the entire plant into biomass, are described in USDA guidance and secondary summaries of the final rule (Cannabusiness Law summary of the final rule, Accessed 2026-10-24). The regulation itself requires notification, verification documentation (AMS-27), and post-remediation re-testing. A remediated lot may enter commerce only if the post-remediation test passes.

Negligent-violation threshold (§990.28 / final-rule summaries): The final rule raised the negligence threshold from 0.5% to 1.0%: a producer using reasonable efforts to grow hemp who produces plants exceeding the acceptable level but not exceeding 1.0% THC on a dry-weight basis does not commit a negligent violation (though the lot must still be disposed of or remediated). Above 1.0%, or with a culpable mental state greater than negligence, the violation is treated more seriously, including possible licence revocation and referral. The 1.0% figure is from a secondary summary of the final rule (Cannabusiness Law, Accessed 2026-10-24) and the pinpoint §990.28 text should be confirmed before legal reliance [VERIFY — §990.28 negligence pinpoint].

7.6.5 What the USDA licence does NOT cover

  • It does not authorise the production of marijuana (>0.3% delta-9 THC dry weight, and after 11 Dec 2026 federally, material failing the new total-THC / per-container test for finished products).
  • It does not authorise processing, distribution, or retail sale as a licensed activity (see §7.4).
  • It does not override local zoning, building, fire, or environmental law.
  • It does not confer any North Carolina cannabis licence, and it does not make EBCI tribal cannabis licences "state-licensed" (that question remains [VERIFY] under the locked baseline).

7.7 PROPOSED ONLY — Chapter 18D licences in HB 328, HB 607, and SB 265

All of this section is PROPOSED ONLY. None of it is law. No Chapter 18D licence can be applied for today, and no Chapter 18D fee is payable today.

7.7.2 HB 328 as passed by the Senate / conference version — PROPOSED Chapter 18D (latest version)

The figures below are from the House Bill 328 Fourth Edition / committee-substitute text (H328-PCS40590-CEf-19 and H328v4, ncleg.gov / webservices.ncleg.gov, Accessed 2026-10-24) and Legislative Reporting Service summaries. Other proposed HB 328 provisions (PROPOSED ONLY, latest version):

  • Definitions (§18D-100): ALE Division, batch, distributor, exit package (opaque, child-resistant per 16 CFR 1700.15(b)(1) / 1700.20), hemp (by reference to G.S. 90-87), hemp-derived cannabinoid (including delta-9, THCA, CBD, CBDA, CBN, CBG, CBC, CBL, CBV, THCV, CBDV, CBT, delta-7, delta-8, delta-10, etc.), hemp-derived consumable product, hemp product, independent testing laboratory, ingestion, inhalation, licence, licensee, manufacture, manufacturer, producer, prohibited hemp-derived consumable product, retail dealer, serving.
  • Age 21: sale to a person under 21 prohibited; retail dealer must demand proof of age if there are reasonable grounds to believe the purchaser is under 30; internet sellers must verify age and use delivery requiring the signature of a person at least 21.
  • Prohibited product (conference/Senate version, as described in secondary analysis, Accessed 2026-10-24): a finished product intended for ingestion or inhalation containing more than 0.4 mg total THC per retail container (innermost package) or any synthetic/chemically converted cannabinoid would be a prohibited product, and would become a Schedule VI controlled substance under NC law. Proposed effective date for those provisions in the Senate version: 12 November 2026 (secondary analysis, Cannabusiness Law, Accessed 2026-10-24) — but this date is inoperative unless HB 328 is enacted, and it must not be quoted as a current NC effective date. The federal 11 Dec 2026 date (P.L. 119-103 §2019) is a separate federal timeline.
  • Criminal/civil penalties (bill text, Accessed 2026-10-24): violation of the manufacturing/distribution-without-licence, >0.3% delta-9, or sample-distribution provisions is a Class A1 misdemeanor; violation of the prohibited-product provision is a Class G felony; ALE may also suspend (up to 3 years), revoke, condition operating hours, or impose civil penalties ($1,000 first / $5,000 second within 3 years / $7,500 third within 3 years of the first).
  • CBD drafting issue [VERIFY — secondary claim, not confirmed from bill text opened in this run]: One secondary analysis (Cannabusiness Law, Accessed 2026-10-24) argues the Senate-passed text as written "expressly prohibits CBD, and any other non-delta 9 THC molecules, in hemp-derived consumable products" through its prohibited-product definition, probably by oversight. This claim was not verified against the full conference-report text in this run and must not be presented as settled law or settled bill meaning. It is flagged here so Run 2's audit pass can check the conference text pinpoint.

7.7.3 HB 607 as filed — PROPOSED Chapter 18D (different fees from HB 328)

HB 607 as filed (31 March 2025, v1) is a different bill with different fees. Do not substitute HB 328's fees for HB 607's or vice versa:

Distributor $2,500 (or $750 if income <$100,000, per Ward & Smith) $750 Same sources.
Retail dealer $250 per location, capped at $5,000 for entities with >25 locations/websites (per Ward & Smith) — LRS summary states "$250 per location for retail dealer licensees with 25 locations or less or who do not offer internet delivery" Same as initial fee Same sources.
Multi-business applicant (HB 607 as filed) Single licence only (LRS summary: "a person or entity engaged in more than one of the businesses listed is required to obtain only a single license") Fee = highest applicable LRS H.607 summary, Accessed 2026-10-24. This directly conflicts with the HB 328 latest-version rule (separate licence per business) — the conflict is between two different bills, not within one bill, and must be preserved as such.

HB 607 as filed also proposed: ALE enforcement; 10 mg THC per serving / 100 mg per package edible caps; child-resistant packaging; accredited-lab Certificate of Analysis with QR-linked batch testing; age 21; and a proposed effective date of 1 July 2026 that has passed unenacted. It retains the 0.3% delta-9 threshold at state level as filed (secondary summary, Accessed 2026-10-24) — it does not adopt a total-THC standard. Testing detail in HB 607 is described as exceptionally detailed (100 specific substances with thresholds, per Ward & Smith, Accessed 2026-10-24) [VERIFY — substance list not opened in this run].

7.7.4 SB 265 as filed — PROPOSED (DEAD)

SB 265 (filed 11 March 2025, referred to Senate Rules 12 March 2025) would have created a similar Chapter 18D scheme but with Department of Revenue licensing (not ALE licensing) and ALE enforcement, fees in the same $250–$15,000 range as HB 607 as filed (manufacturer $15,000 / $1,000 low-income tier; distributor $2,500 / $750; retailer $250 per location, $5,000 cap), age 21 with signature delivery, potency limits (75 mg for non-liquid servings in one secondary summary — [VERIFY] against SB 265 text), child-resistant packaging, and a ban on use on school grounds. Source: BillTrack50 + Ward & Smith summaries, Accessed 2026-10-24. SB 265 is dead and must not be cited as a pending licensing route. Its only value in this Part is to explain why some secondary sources quote Department of Revenue licensing — that was SB 265's model, not HB 328's or HB 607's.

7.7.5 What Chapter 18D would NOT do (even if enacted)

  • It would not create a North Carolina hemp grower licence. Chapter 18D as proposed covers manufacturers, distributors, and retail dealers of hemp-derived consumable products. Cultivation would remain USDA-licensed. Do not describe Chapter 18D as a "hemp farming licence."
  • It would not create a state medical or adult-use cannabis licence. It regulates hemp-derived consumables only.
  • It would not by itself resolve the federal total-THC / 0.4 mg question, which is governed by P.L. 119-37 / P.L. 119-103 (federal baseline above).

7.8 General North Carolina registrations that DO apply — and are NOT hemp licences

These are included because applicants routinely confuse them with hemp licences. Each is a general registration that applies to many businesses, not a hemp authorisation, and none of them authorises a person to grow hemp without a USDA licence.

Registration Who needs it Fee / notes Why it is NOT a hemp licence Source (Accessed 2026-10-24)
Secretary of State entity formation / assumed-name registration LLCs, corporations, partnerships forming or doing business in NC; sole proprietors using an assumed name (county Register of Deeds) State filing fees apply (amounts vary by entity type — not stated here because no current fee schedule was opened in this run [VERIFY]) It creates the legal entity; it does not authorise any hemp activity. NCDOR registration itself asks for the Secretary of State business ID "if applicable," confirming the two are separate steps. 17 NCAC 07B .0104 (NCDOR registration rule) via Justia, Accessed 2026-10-24, listing SOS business ID among information needed for a Certificate of Registration.
NCDOR Certificate of Registration (sales/use tax) Any person engaging in business as a retailer, wholesale merchant, or facilitator liable for sales/use tax; also persons required to pay use tax on purchases No fee to obtain a Certificate of Registration (17 NCAC 07B .0104(a)(1)). Apply via ncdor.gov online business registration, Streamlined Sales Tax Registration System, or by calling 1-877-252-3052 for a paper form (Form NC-BR). Information needed includes ownership/SOS ID, FEIN or SSN, legal business name, trade names, addresses, telephone, and responsible-person demographics. It authorises collection/remittance of sales tax. It does not authorise hemp production, processing, or retail sale as hemp. A hemp retailer with no Certificate is tax-non-compliant, not "unlicensed for hemp" — and a hemp retailer with a Certificate is tax-registered, not "hemp-licensed." 17 NCAC 07B .0104 via Justia, Accessed 2026-10-24; secondary NCDOR guides confirm free online registration and ~10-business-day mailed certificate (secondary, Accessed 2026-10-24).
Local zoning / land-use approval + any local business permit Any business operating at a physical location, especially cultivation/processing/retail Varies by city/county; no statewide fee schedule. No general state business-licence fee exists (the former state privilege-licence tax was repealed effective 1 July 2015 per Run 1 baseline). Zoning confirms the use fits the location; it does not authorise the hemp activity itself. A USDA-licensed grower on land where agriculture is prohibited by local zoning still has a zoning problem. Conversely, zoning approval for "agriculture" or "industrial" does not substitute for a USDA licence. Run 1 baseline (Ch. 160D zoning; privilege-licence repeal); secondary NC business-licensing guides, Accessed 2026-10-24, confirming no general state licence.
Food establishment / food manufacturing permits (if applicable) A hemp business that manufactures, processes, packs, or sells food (including hemp-infused foods, beverages, gummies as foods) Varies by county health department / NCDA&CS Food & Drug Protection Division jurisdiction; no hemp-specific food fee located in this run [VERIFY — food-permit jurisdiction and fee schedule not opened] A food permit authorises the food operation (sanitation, labelling as food, facility standards). It is not a hemp licence and does not test THC, authorise a hemp claim, or override FDA positions on CBD in food. A hemp food business needs both the food compliance and (for its own crop) a USDA licence — neither substitutes for the other. General principle; pinpoint food-permit authority for hemp foods [VERIFY].
Federal EIN, FSA farm registration USDA licensees (EIN for entities; FSA farm/tract/field numbers for every growing location) EIN free from IRS; FSA registration via local FSA office (see §7.5.8) Administrative identifiers, not licences. 7 CFR 990.21(a)(3)(i) (EIN); 7 CFR 990.23 + AMS-28 instructions (FSA numbers), Accessed 2026-10-24.

Explicit non-licences to avoid mislabelling: A Secretary of State filing, an NCDOR Certificate of Registration, a county food permit, a local privilege/business permit (where a locality still requires one), and a USDA producer licence held by a supplier are all sometimes described in marketing as a "hemp licence." None of them, alone or together (except the USDA producer licence for the producer's own crop), is a North Carolina hemp licence, because no such licence exists.

7.9 EBCI tribal hemp — cross-reference only (full tribal detail is another agent's part)

This section is deliberately brief and does not contradict Run 1's EBCI cannabis findings (referendum, Ord. No. 63, Great Smoky Cannabis Co. dates, purchase-limit and tax conflicts — all preserved in the locked baselines above).

  • EBCI has a USDA-approved tribal hemp plan. AMS's "List of USDA-approved Hemp Plans" (updated 17 February 2026) lists "Eastern Band of Cherokee Indians — Approved Plan (pdf)" among tribal plans, while North Carolina as a State is listed as "USDA Hemp Producer License." Source: AMS plan-review page, search-result text, Accessed 2026-10-24. The EBCI plan PDF itself was not opened in this run [VERIFY — plan terms, fees, and current administrator not confirmed from the plan text].
  • Consequence: Under 7 CFR 990.22(c), production on land covered by the EBCI approved plan must be licensed under that plan, not under the USDA plan. A USDA licence does not authorise production on EBCI tribal trust land, and an EBCI hemp permit does not authorise production off tribal trust land.
  • Historical EBCI hemp permit (2020, dated — current terms [VERIFY]): The Cherokee One Feather (3 August 2020, Accessed 2026-10-24) reported that Tribal Council unanimously passed Resolution No. 215 (2020) on 9 July 2020 authorising the Cannabis Commission to submit the EBCI Hemp Regulatory Plan to USDA for final federal review and approval, and that "An EBCI Hemp Permit would only be valid on tribal trust land." The plan as described then required an EBCI tribal member seeking a permit to demonstrate "Evidence of income from a farming operation from the two previous years" (Schedule F / Form 1120S / Form 1120 / Form 1065 variants). These 2020 plan-description details must not be quoted as current EBCI hemp-permit requirements without confirming the approved plan PDF and any subsequent EBCI Cannabis Control Board rules [VERIFY]. No current EBCI hemp-permit fee, term, or application link was located in this run.
  • Do not conflate EBCI hemp and EBCI cannabis: The EBCI hemp plan (USDA-approved, for hemp ≤0.3% delta-9) and the EBCI cannabis programme (Tribal Council ordinances, Cannabis Control Board, Great Smoky Cannabis Co. medical/adult-use sales) are separate legal regimes under separate authorities. A hemp permit is not a cannabis licence, and a cannabis licence is not a hemp permit.
  • No EBCI-area property is counted in Part 8 unless it is genuinely listed/marketed for cannabis/hemp (see §8.4 — none was found).

7.10 Registries — where a North Carolina hemp licensee can (and cannot) be looked up

7.10.1 USDA Hemp Public Search Tool (search-only)

  • AMS FAQ (search-result text, Accessed 2026-10-24): "How can I obtain a list of USDA licensed hemp producers? Information on licensees in your area can be obtained through the Hemp Public Search Tool. Records regarding the U.S. Domestic Hemp Production Program that have been previously released under the Freedom of Information Act are available at the AMS FOIA webpage. You can also submit a formal FOIA request."
  • The public tool URL given in a secondary official-adjacent source (Kansas Dept. of Agriculture portal, Accessed 2026-10-24) is https://hemp.ams.usda.gov/s/PublicSearchTool. The AMS HeMP page distinguishes this public tool from the law-enforcement search tool at hempsearch.ams.usda.gov, which law-enforcement members access by requesting HeMP access via FarmBill.Hemp@usda.gov and which shows detailed maps of growing locations. The public tool is search-only: it returns individual licensee records for a search; it does not provide a downloadable statewide list or a count. That search-only design is itself the reason Run 1 gap R1-11 (licensee count) cannot be resolved from the tool alone (see §7.11).
  • The public tool was not queried live in this run (no live-browser access in this subagent run). No count derived from a live tool query is claimed here.

7.10.2 USDA FOIA PDF (updated 14 April 2026 per Run 1) — tally attempt

  • Attempt made: Search was used to locate the AMS FOIA reading-room release and the 14 April 2026 PDF. The FOIA release was not opened or tallied in this run: no search result returned the PDF's contents or a North Carolina count, and direct page-opening failed (see Method Note). Therefore no FOIA-derived North Carolina licensee count is reported in this Part.
  • What was found instead (secondary, NOT an official tally): A hemp-industry business-plan site (hempcbdbusinessplans.com, "North Carolina Hemp Industry," Accessed 2026-10-24, publication date not stated on the page excerpt) claims, citing the USDA National Hemp Report and the USDA licence search tool:
  • 2023: 589 USDA-licensed growers in North Carolina; 950 acres planted, 220 harvested; indoor floral hemp ~13,000 lb produced, 11,258 lb utilised, value "close to $7 million."
  • 2024: licensed growers "held steady"; 850 acres planted, 480 harvested; indoor floral ~3,030 lb produced, 2,963 lb utilised, value "nearly $2 million."
  • 2025: planted acreage up 35% to 1,150 acres, 910 harvested; ~47% floral biomass; 214,000 lb utilised valued at $725,000; indoor floral "nearly 30,000 pounds … valued at $18.5 million, accounting for 23% of the value of total US utilized indoor floral hemp production."
  • 2026 growing season: "658 licensed hemp growers in North Carolina according to the USDA license search tool."
  • Treatment of the 658 figure: It is secondary, undated, and not verified against the USDA tool or FOIA PDF in this run. It is recorded here only as a lead for the audit pass, and must not be quoted as the official North Carolina licensee count. The difference between 589 (2023, National Hemp Report) and 658 (2026, search-tool claim) is plausible as growth, but plausibility is not verification. The official count remains STILL OPEN (see §7.11, R1-11).
  • Earlier pilot-era scale (for contrast, not a current count): Over 1,500 licensed producers under the pilot (NCDA&CS, §7.2.1). The drop from 1,500+ (pilot, 2021) to ~589–658 (USDA, 2023–2026, secondary) reflects both the end of the pilot and industry contraction/consolidation — but the two figures come from different regimes and different counting methods and must not be presented as a like-for-like trend without that caveat.

7.11 Run 1 gaps R1-11 and R1-23 — resolution status

R1-11 — "NC hemp licensee count" — STILL OPEN

  • What was attempted: (1) AMS plan-review page (NC = USDA plan, confirmed); (2) AMS FAQ (Public Search Tool + FOIA route, confirmed); (3) search for the USDA FOIA PDF updated 14 April 2026 and for a tally; (4) search for National Hemp Report figures.
  • What was found: No official, tallied North Carolina count from the FOIA PDF or from a live Public Search Tool query. Only a secondary, undated claim of 658 growers for the 2026 season (see §7.10.2), plus secondary National Hemp Report figures for 2023–2025.
  • Verdict: STILL OPEN. The gap is not resolved by a secondary 658 figure. Resolution requires either (a) opening and tallying the AMS FOIA PDF (with its stated update date and the counting method — licences vs licensees vs locations — stated), or (b) a live Public Search Tool query with the query parameters and date recorded, or (c) the USDA National Hemp Report table for North Carolina with its publication date. Until one of those is done, any North Carolina hemp licensee count must carry [VERIFY] and must not be presented as an official total.
  • Partial progress (not resolution): The counting problem is now precisely defined: the tool is search-only, the FOIA PDF is the only bulk source identified, and the secondary range is 589 (2023) to 658 (2026, unverified). That narrows the audit task but does not close it.

R1-23 — "Former 02 NCAC 62 / current NCAC hemp chapter" — RESOLVED IN SUBSTANCE; pinpoint instrument STILL OPEN

  • Former chapter — RESOLVED (existence, agency, and content areas confirmed):
  • Chapter 02 NCAC 62 was the Industrial Hemp Commission's rule chapter. OAH temporary-rule documents (search-result text, Accessed 2026-10-24) show, e.g., 02 NCAC 62 .0107 Application for Licenses, with history note citing G.S. 106-568.50–58 and 106-568.53(3), effective 24 February 2017 (temporary rule, adopted 13 February 2017, proposed 28 December 2016, published 5 January 2017, hearing 12 January 2017). The reason given was S.L. 2016-93, which gave rulemaking authority to the newly created Industrial Hemp Commission.
  • 02 NCAC 62 .0103 is identified in a secondary hemp-law guide (Chow420, Accessed 2026-10-24) as the prior rule requiring sampling prior to harvest and analysis by qualified labs, citing the NC Register Vol. 31, Issue 18 (files.nc.gov). The .0103 pinpoint is secondary and [VERIFY] against the Register text if the audit pass needs the verbatim rule.
  • Content areas of the former chapter therefore included: licence applications (.0107), sampling and testing procedures (including decarboxylation testing method in the Article 50F successor drafts — see §7.2.2 note), supervision during growth/harvest, seed/plant verification, and fees (see §7.2.3).
  • Current chapter — RESOLVED (there is none):
  • There is no current North Carolina Administrative Code hemp chapter that licenses hemp in North Carolina today, because there is no state hemp licensing article for it to implement. NCDA&CS's statement that the State "no longer possesses the regulatory authority to oversee or administer a regulatory system for the licensing or regulation of hemp producers and processors" (§7.1) is dispositive: a current 02 NCAC 62 licensing chapter cannot coexist with that statement.
  • Any current reference to "02 NCAC 62" in a guide, listing, or application should be treated as a historical reference to the pilot-era rules, not a live rule.
  • Pinpoint STILL OPEN: The exact repeal instrument and effective date for 02 NCAC 62 itself (as distinct from the Article 50E repeal effective 30 June 2022) was not located in this run. Whether the chapter was repealed by the same session law, by a separate OAH repeal action, or left as a dead chapter on the books was not confirmed. The audit pass should check the OAH NCAC database for "02 NCAC 62" status (current / repealed / reserved) and record the repeal citation if one exists. Until then, state the position as: former chapter identified; current chapter: none; formal repeal citation for the chapter itself [VERIFY].

Other gaps carried from Run 1 that touch Part 7 (for the audit pass, not resolved here)

  • Whether EBCI tribal medical licences count as "state-licensed" federally — [VERIFY] (locked baseline; untouched by Part 7, which concerns hemp, not medical cannabis).
  • HB 328 conference-report CBD drafting issue (§7.7.2) — [VERIFY] against conference text.
  • HB 607 income-tier fee detail (§7.7.3) — [VERIFY] against HB 607 v1 text.
  • §990.25 measurement-of-uncertainty pinpoint and §990.28 negligence pinpoint — [VERIFY] against current eCFR text.
  • Current EBCI hemp-plan terms, fees, and administrator — [VERIFY] (plan PDF not opened).
  • Food-permit jurisdiction/fee for hemp foods — [VERIFY] (§7.8).

PART 8 — CANNABIS-ZONED PROPERTIES

8.1 Headline answer — one verified hemp-marketed property, no verified cannabis-zoned property

After genuine searching across 420Property, LoopNet, Crexi, BizBuySell, CBXRE, local-broker listings, and general web search (sources and queries detailed in §8.6), the result is:

  • Verified hemp-marketed properties in North Carolina: 1 — the Old Fort farm at 1891 Silvers Welch Rd (item P-1 below), marketed as a former licensed hemp farm with light-deprivation greenhouses and processing warehouses. Its price and current status are conflicted (see P-1), so it is carried as [VERIFY] for status/price even though its hemp marketing is verified from multiple listing sources.
  • Verified cannabis-zoned properties in North Carolina: 0. No listing was found that states a North Carolina property is zoned for cannabis (medical or adult-use) or that carries a cannabis-specific zoning designation. This is unsurprising and legally coherent: North Carolina has no state medical/adult-use licensing programme whose zoning a listing could reference, and the only lawful cannabis retail in the State is on EBCI tribal trust land, where tribal land is not marketed through ordinary commercial listing platforms (see §8.4).
  • Verified hemp/CBD retail properties (storefronts marketed for hemp/CBD retail): 0 as real-property listings. What exists instead are business sales (a CBD shop business, a hemp distributor business, an online hemp retailer business) that do not include real estate or do not disclose a property — those are listed separately in §8.5 and are not counted as properties.
  • General industrial / farm / retail listings are NOT counted. Dozens of ordinary farms, warehouses, and storefronts exist for sale in North Carolina. None of them is counted here unless the listing itself markets it for hemp/cannabis or states a cannabis/hemp zoning or prior hemp use. Presenting a generic greenhouse farm as "cannabis-zoned" would be fabrication, and this Part does not do it.

The single verified item and the negative result together are the honest finding. A researcher who needs a cannabis-zoned site in North Carolina today is, on current evidence, looking either at (a) a former hemp farm whose new owner would need a USDA licence for hemp (not a cannabis licence, which does not exist), (b) EBCI tribal land (not publicly listed — see §8.4), or (c) a speculative purchase in anticipation of a future state programme that has not been enacted.

P-1 detail — what is verified, what is not

Verified from multiple listing sources (Accessed 2026-10-24):

  • Address and size: 1891 Silvers Welch Rd, Old Fort, NC 28762; 43.37 acres (CBX, RateMyAgent, Likewise, SurvivalRealty all agree on 43–43.37 acres).
  • Two state-of-the-art, fully automated light-deprivation greenhouses, equipped with 200 Amp / 3-phase electrical service, raised beds, irrigation, grow lighting, pump-driven water tanks, dehumidifiers, and remote control, with year-round growing capacity (CBX / Likewise text).
  • Processing infrastructure: metal processing warehouse with concrete floors and drains; clear-span steel warehouse with temperature-controlled drying/processing rooms and LED lighting; propagating hoop house; tool barn; pole barn (Likewise text).
  • Hemp history: "Previously used as a licensed hemp farm" (Likewise text; CBX index text: "HISTORIC HEMP PRODUCTION"). One Likewise excerpt is truncated as "(2017 – 202)" — the end year is cut off in the search excerpt and must not be guessed; the plausible reading is 2017–2021 (pilot era) or 2017–2022, but the truncated year is [VERIFY].
  • Historic production claims (marketing claims, NOT verified yields): CBX index text states "Greenhouses: 800 – 1000 lbs per harvest, 4 harvests per year. Outdoors – current owner grew outside 2019 and 2020 and yielded 1,000 pounds from the working fields. Owner estimates a total of 6,400 – 8,000 pounds per year (greenhouses and outdoors)." These are seller/owner estimates reproduced from the listing, not audited production records, and must be labelled as such if quoted.
  • Location context: "Gateway to the Blue Ridge Mountains," ~15 minutes to Black Mountain, ~30 minutes to Asheville; scenic terrain overlooking Hickory Nut Mountain; Crooked Creek frontage with streams "deep enough for irrigation"; southwest portion along Crooked Creek in the 100-year flood plain (Likewise text). Flood-plain status is a material diligence item — a buyer must confirm flood-plain mapping and insurability, and must not assume greenhouses in a flood plain are insurable or permittable for a new use without checking.
  • Land management: "continually cover cropped to build soil health"; current owner practices "regenerative organic, bio-dynamic, no till farming" (Likewise text — seller claims, not certifications verified in this run [VERIFY]).

NOT verified / must not be added:

  • Zoning: No listing excerpt states a zoning district (e.g. agricultural, industrial, commercial) for P-1, and none states a cannabis/hemp zoning overlay. RateMyAgent classifies it as "Rural." McDowell County GIS / zoning verification for this parcel was not performed in this run (no live GIS access) and is a [VERIFY] diligence step for any buyer. Do not describe P-1 as "cannabis-zoned."
  • Current price and active status: See the price conflict in the table. The $2,640,000 CBX price is indexed as "3 years ago" (i.e. circa 2023); the $1,599,900 SurvivalRealty price is dated 14 October 2026. Whether the SurvivalRealty entry is the same offering repriced, a different broker's re-listing, or a stale syndication cannot be determined from search excerpts. Do not quote a single current asking price for P-1 without opening the live listings.
  • Licence transfer: The former "Hemp Seed Dealer license, Hemp Cultivation license, and Nursery license" mentioned in the CBX index excerpt are pilot-era licences. Under §7.5.6, USDA licences are non-transferable, and pilot licences expired 31 December 2021. No licence transfers with P-1. A buyer who wants to grow hemp at P-1 needs their own USDA licence and a §990.21(c) modification / new application covering this location, plus FSA registration for the parcel.
  • Cannabis use: P-1 is a hemp farm, not a cannabis dispensary or cultivation site. Nothing in the listings suggests it is licensed or zoned for marijuana, and North Carolina has no licence that could authorise marijuana cultivation there. Marketing P-1 as a "cannabis property" without the "former hemp" qualifier would be misleading.

Why P-1 counts (and generic farms do not): P-1 is counted because the listings themselves market it by its hemp history and hemp-capable infrastructure (light-deprivation greenhouses, processing/drying warehouses). That is the Part 8 test: the listing markets it for hemp. A generic farm with greenhouses but no hemp marketing would not count, even if it could physically grow hemp.

8.4 EBCI-area properties — none found, and why that is expected

  • Search: Queries for Cherokee / Swain County / EBCI-area hemp or cannabis properties returned no property listing. The only EBCI-area commercial material returned was editorial about Great Smoky Cannabis Co.'s retail operation in Cherokee (Cardinal & Pine, Accessed 2026-10-24 — describing the dispensary's interior and the statewide intoxicating-hemp debate, not a property for sale).
  • Why no listing is expected: Lawful cannabis retail in North Carolina exists only on EBCI tribal trust land, under tribal law. Tribal trust land is not fee-simple land marketed on LoopNet, Crexi, or 420Property, and a tribal cannabis licence is not a real-property interest that can be listed for sale. A search for "EBCI cannabis property for sale" that returned a fee-simple listing would itself be suspect.
  • What would count (for future runs): A genuinely listed property within the Cherokee area that is marketed for hemp (e.g. a former hemp farm on fee land near Cherokee, with a stated address and hemp marketing) would count under the hemp test, even if it is not on tribal trust land. No such listing was found. A tribal-trust parcel offered by the Tribe itself through an official EBCI channel would need to be verified through official EBCI sources before counting — none was located.
  • Do not infer from the absence of EBCI listings that EBCI has no cannabis real estate. Great Smoky Cannabis Co. operates from tribal premises; those premises are simply not publicly listed for sale, and this Part does not invent a listing for them.

Rule applied: If a listing does not disclose a North Carolina address and a real-property interest (fee, leasehold of a specific unit offered for sale/lease as property, or land), it is a business sale, not a property, and is excluded from the property count even if the business sells hemp/CBD.

8.7 Zoning — what "cannabis-zoned" would mean in North Carolina, and why no such zoning was found

  • There is no statewide cannabis zoning category in North Carolina, because there is no state cannabis licensing programme to zone for. Local zoning in North Carolina operates under Chapter 160D (Run 1 baseline). A city or county could, in theory, zone for agriculture, industrial, or commercial uses that a hemp operation could occupy — but that is general zoning, not cannabis zoning, and this Part does not re-label it.
  • Hemp-specific local zoning: Run 1 found only one adopted hemp-specific zoning rule among the eight largest localities checked — Wake County unincorporated 1,000-ft buffers (16 Oct 2023, amended 18 Nov 2024). No Wake County property listing marketed under that buffer rule was found in this run, and the buffer rule itself is a restriction (where hemp cannot go), not a cannabis-zoned district a listing could claim.
  • EBCI zoning: Tribal trust land is governed by tribal law, not Chapter 160D. No EBCI zoning map or cannabis-zoning district for a publicly listed parcel was located.
  • Practical zoning diligence for P-1 (or any future hemp site): (1) Confirm the parcel's current zoning district with McDowell County (for P-1) or the relevant city/county; (2) confirm agriculture / greenhouse / processing / warehousing is a permitted or special use in that district; (3) confirm any Wake-County-style hemp buffer does not apply in that jurisdiction (it does not apply in McDowell County on current evidence, but the jurisdiction must be checked, not assumed); (4) confirm flood-plain, well/septic, electrical-service, and road-access constraints for greenhouse/processing use; (5) confirm that any proposed retail use is permitted in the district (retail is a different use from agriculture/processing). None of these steps was completed for P-1 in this run (no GIS access) — all are [VERIFY] diligence, not findings.

PART 9 — REGULATORS AND HISTORY

9.1 The regulatory map: there is no single North Carolina cannabis regulator

North Carolina’s most important regulatory fact is negative: because there is no state medical or adult-use programme and no state cannabis licence, there is no North Carolina cannabis commission, cannabis control board, or equivalent state licensing agency. Regulation is fragmented across agriculture (hemp cultivation, now federal), public health (the former epilepsy-extract registry and the Governor’s advisory council), revenue (sales and unauthorised-substances tax), and criminal enforcement (ALE, SBI, sheriffs and district attorneys). The only licensing regulator for marijuana in the state’s geography is tribal: the EBCI Cannabis Control Board, whose jurisdiction is tribal land only.

9.1.1 A note on vendor tables that misstate North Carolina law

Several commercial “is it legal in NC?” tables found in this run state a “21+” age requirement for North Carolina hemp/Delta-9 products, or cite “SB 352” as authority. CONFLICT with the locked baseline: North Carolina currently has no state age minimum for hemp products, and SB 352 is not a Run 1 locked authority for such a rule. Those tables are retailer policies or errors, not law, and must not be quoted as NC law. Examples: https://www.atlrx.com/blogs/delta-9/is-delta-9-legal-in-nc/ ; https://www.atlrx.com/blogs/delta-8/is-delta-8-legal-in-north-carolina/ . The only age-21 rules that are law in this geography are (a) EBCI tribal law on tribal land (21+), and (b) pending bills (SB 328 / HB 328 Ch. 18D) that would impose 21+ if enacted — neither is law today.

9.2 Key dates timeline — with the Art. 5G repeal conflict addressed

Table 9B — Timeline (all dates as stated in sources; session-law pinpoints marked [VERIFY] where the enrolled text was not re-opened in this run)

Date Event Legal effect / why it matters Source — Accessed 2026-10-24
1977 Decriminalisation of small amounts — in the limited NC sense. Possession of ≤0.5 oz marijuana reduced to a Class 3 misdemeanour with suspended imprisonment and a maximum $200 fine. NC “decriminalisation” is not civil-infraction decriminalisation: it remains a criminal conviction on the record. Current penalty ladder (Run 1 / secondary summaries, consistent): ≤0.5 oz Class 3 misdemeanour (≤$200); >0.5–1.5 oz Class 1 misdemeanour; >1.5 oz Class I felony; trafficking >10 lb under §90-95(h). Session-law pinpoint for the 1977 act itself [VERIFY] — the 1977 date and the current §90-95(d)(4) suspended-sentence language are confirmed in secondary/legislative summaries, but the original 1977 session law was not re-opened in this run. Connecticut OGA comparative table quoting §90-95(d): http://www.cga.ct.gov/2010/rpt/2010-R-0204.htm ; https://journal.cannabislawreport.com/zuber-lawler-the-state-of-cannabis-in-north-carolina/ ; current §90-95 text: http://www.ncleg.net/enactedlegislation/statutes/pdf/bysection/chapter_90/gs_90-95.pdf
2014 (enacted as S.L. 2014-53); 2015 (HB 766 amendments) Epilepsy Alternative Treatment Act, Art. 5G, Ch. 90. Pilot-study model: registered neurologists, NCDHHS database, hemp extract for intractable epilepsy. HB 766 (2015) encouraged UNC-Chapel Hill, East Carolina, Duke and Wake Forest research and, critically, enacted the repeal below. Created the only state medical-cannabis-adjacent exemption that survives in any form (see next rows). https://www.ncleg.gov/Sessions/2015/Bills/House/PDF/H766v5.pdf
1 July 2021 Art. 5G REPEALED — Run 1 gap R1-01, PARTIALLY RESOLVED. HB 766 §8.5(a)–(b), verbatim: “Article 5G of Chapter 90 of the General Statutes is repealed.” / “This section becomes effective July 1, 2021.” The registry, pilot-study and neurologist-registration machinery is gone. What survives is the separate exemption in §90-94.1 (see below), which still refers to repealed definitions — that dangling reference is the real conflict, and it is not a drafting error to “fix” by assuming the exemption died with Art. 5G. https://www.ncleg.gov/Sessions/2015/Bills/House/PDF/H766v5.pdf ; corroborated: https://www.mycannabis.com/cannabis-in-north-carolina/ (“Article 5G’s registry and definition provisions were repealed effective July 1, 2021, although G.S. 90-94.1 still refers to them”)
Current (post-2021) §90-94.1 hemp-extract exemption survives — narrow, no supply chain. Verbatim operative definition (via NC Board of Pharmacy guidance quoting §90-94.1(a)): hemp extract is “an extract from a cannabis plant … that … [i]s composed of less than nine-tenths of one percent (0.9%) tetrahydrocannabinol by weight,” “[i]s composed of at least five percent (5%) cannabidiol by weight,” and “contains no other psychoactive substance.” Possession/use is exempt only to treat intractable epilepsy, with a certificate of analysis in close proximity (per §90-94.1(b) as summarised in secondary sources; full current §90-94.1 text [VERIFY] before court reliance). This 0.9% / 5% extract is different from 0.3% hemp. It is not a general medical programme, has no state dispensaries, no home grow, and no in-state lawful supply chain stated in the statute summaries found. Patients/caregivers were historically told to register with NCDHHS — that registration route no longer exists as an Art. 5G registry. R1-01 outcome: RESOLVED as to the repeal fact and date; STILL OPEN as to whether §90-94.1 is operable today without its repealed definitions, and whether NCDHHS gives any current guidance — no current NCDHHS guidance page was located. https://docest.com/doc/470140/current-guidance-to-north-carolina-pharmacies-on-the-sale-of-products-derived-from-hemp ; https://www.lawserver.com/law/state/north-carolina/nc-laws/north_carolina_laws_90-94-1 ; https://norml.org/laws/north-carolina-cbd-marijuana-law/
2015 (SB 313); autumn 2016; spring 2017 Industrial hemp pilot created (SB 313, 2015); Commission seated autumn 2016; temporary rules Feb 2017; first planting spring 2017; first harvest 2017. Modified 2016 by HB 992. Federal authority: 2014 Farm Bill §7606 pilot. NC’s pilot was temporary by design — the reason it could simply expire. https://www.ncagr.gov/news/press-releases/2021/08/16/nc-will-wrap-hemp-pilot-program-federal-regulatory-program-takes-effect ; https://www.mdpi.com/2077-0472/13/4/887
20 Dec 2018 2018 Farm Bill (Pub. L. 115-334) deschedules hemp federally (≤0.3% delta-9) and adds §10114 interstate-transport protection (see Part 10, §10.3). Federal floor for NC hemp commerce; does not create a NC retail regime. https://harris-sliwoski.com/cannalawblog/transporting-hemp-across-state-lines-is-still-risky/
31 Dec 2021 NC hemp pilot ENDS. NCDA&CS: pilot programme ended 31 December 2021; letters sent to licensed producers in 2021. Hard cut-over, not a phase-out: NC licences ceased to be valid even if unexpired (Extension notice). https://hemp.ces.ncsu.edu/news/all-nc-hemp-growers-must-now-be-licensed-through-usda/ ; https://www.ncagr.gov/divisions/plant-industry/hemp-nc
1 Jan 2022 – present USDA-only hemp growers in NC. Federal plan licence via AMS HeMP; FSA acreage reporting; FBI background check + fingerprinting; grower-paid sampling agent and lab; no USDA licence fee. NCDA&CS processor registration discontinued. Locked baseline confirmed again in Run 2; USDA list updated 17 Feb 2026 still shows NC as USDA-licence state. https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review ; https://hemp.ces.ncsu.edu/news/all-nc-hemp-growers-must-now-be-licensed-through-usda/
30 June 2022 (S.L. 2022-32, SB 455) Hemp permanently excluded from NC Controlled Substances Act. Current definition, §90-87(13a), verbatim: “‘Hemp’ means the plant Cannabis sativa (L.) and any part of that plant, including the seeds thereof and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of not more than three-tenths of one percent (0.3%) on a dry weight basis.” §90-87(13b) defines “hemp products” as all products made from hemp. Marijuana definition in §90-87(16) excludes hemp/hemp products. Art. 50E (former pilot article) repealed same date (locked baseline). This is the permanent state-law basis for hemp commerce in NC. It uses delta-9 only, not total THC — the gap the federal PL 119-37/119-103 total-THC change will expose from 11 Dec 2026 (federal baseline), with no NC conforming amendment located. https://codes.findlaw.com/nc/chapter-90-medicine-and-allied-occupations/nc-gen-st-sect-90-87.html ; https://www.sog.unc.edu/sites/default/files/course_materials/Dixon_Cannabis%20Update%20Fall%202025%20%28judges%20version%29.pdf

9.2.1 Decriminalisation — what “1977” does and does not prove

Three cautions. First, the 1977 reform did not make possession lawful, did not create a civil fine, and did not expunge anything: a ≤0.5 oz first possession is still a Class 3 misdemeanour conviction, albeit with a suspended sentence and a $200 maximum fine. Second, the often-quoted “three or fewer misdemeanour convictions” qualifier in encyclopaedic summaries (possession ≤0.5 oz decriminalised “for individuals with three or fewer misdemeanour convictions”) was not verified against §90-95 text in this run — [VERIFY] before repeating it as a statutory qualifier. Third, local “lowest enforcement priority” / citation-instead-of-arrest proposals (e.g., Winston-Salem Fair Justice & Public Safety Ordinance campaign reported August 2026) are advocacy proposals, not adopted ordinances on the evidence found; the Yes! Weekly report expressly notes its announcement “does not establish that the city adopted them”: https://www.yesweekly.com/cannabis/marijuana-poll-finds-bipartisan-support-as-triad-debates-cannabis-policy/article_42fe3b48-c530-4be9-80ab-78a5178a3a51.html

9.3 Ballot / referendum history

9.3.1 North Carolina has NO citizen initiative — confirmed

Finding: CONFIRMED. North Carolina provides no statewide citizen-initiated ballot measure (no initiative, no popular referendum, no citizen veto). Ballotpedia lists North Carolina among the 24 states that “do not provide for statewide citizen-initiated ballot measures”: https://ballotpedia.org/States_with_initiative_or_referendum

The League of Women Voters of North Carolina states it identically, and the statement also defines the only referendum that does exist:

“Currently, North Carolina does not have initiative and the only form of referendum — voter approval or rejection of a legislative enactment — now in place applies to constitutional amendments. The state legislature must place its proposed constitutional amendments on the ballot for voter approval.”

Source: https://my.lwv.org/north-carolina-state/citizen-initiative

Constitutional anchor (verbatim). NC Constitution, Article II, §1:

“The legislative power of the State shall be vested in the General Assembly, which shall consist of a Senate and a House of Representatives.”

Source: https://house.ncleg.gov/Laws/Constitution/article2

There is no initiative clause in Article II to quote, because none exists — the absence is structural: legislative power is vested exclusively in the General Assembly, and the Constitution’s amendment procedure (Article XIII — pinpoint [VERIFY] against the enrolled Constitution text before citing a section number in court) requires legislative proposal followed by voter approval. That is why every cannabis reform in §9.2, however popular in polls (§9.3.3), must pass the General Assembly, and why Senate-passed bills can die in the House without any voter recourse.

Practical consequence, stated plainly: a North Carolina voter cannot put medical or adult-use legalisation on the ballot by petition. The only ballot route is persuading a legislative supermajority to refer a constitutional amendment — a route no cannabis amendment has taken. Local initiative exists in some NC municipalities for local matters, but no local initiative can legalise marijuana against Ch. 90, and no such cannabis local initiative was found.

No statewide cannabis referendum has ever been held in North Carolina. No cannabis question has appeared on a statewide NC ballot. The only cannabis referendum in the state’s geography is tribal (EBCI, §9.3.2).

9.3.2 EBCI tribal referendum — official results (tribal reporting where official tribal text is unavailable, labelled as such)

Question (verbatim, as reported by the Cherokee One Feather, the Tribe’s official newspaper — tribal reporting, not the enrolled tribal resolution text):

“Do you support legalizing the possession and use of cannabis for persons who are at least twenty-one (21) years old and require the EBCI Tribal Council to develop legislation to regulate the market?”

Result: PASSED 2,464 (yes) to 1,057 (no), 7 September 2023 General Election. Turnout denominator and certified canvass [VERIFY] — the enrolled referendum resolution / certified results document was not opened in this run; the 2,464–1,057 figures are consistent across the One Feather, the Citizen Times, and Run 1, and are carried forward as the locked baseline.

Sources: https://theonefeather.com/2024/06/07/dinilawigi-tribal-council-approves-adult-use-cannabis/ ; https://theonefeather.com/2024/07/01/dinilawigi-tribal-council-reverses-decision-on-hemp-stores-adult-use-cannabis-ratified/?utm_source=rss&utm_medium=rss&utm_campaign=dinilawigi-tribal-council-reverses-decision-on-hemp-stores-adult-use-cannabis-ratified

Legal effect: the referendum was advisory/directive in form (“require the Tribal Council to develop legislation”) — it did not itself legalise adult use. Legalisation required Ord. No. 63 (2024) (see Part 10). The one-year anniversary symmetry is exact: adult-use sales to all adults opened 7 September 2024, one year to the day after the referendum.

Other tribal votes noted: Lumbee Tribal Council passed a gaming ordinance 14–2 with a referendum next (reported 5 days before access date) — gaming, not cannabis, included only to prevent confusion: https://rg.org/news/gambling-industry/lumbee-council-passes-gaming-ordinance-sending-casino-plan-to-voters . No Lumbee cannabis referendum was found. Catawba state-recognition was by Commission vote (5 June 2026), not a referendum (see §10.2).

Reading the two 2026 polls together — and why they differ. Both find majority adult-use support and ~3/4 medical support. They differ materially on Republican adult-use support: SurveyUSA 52% vs Elon 33% — a 19-point gap that methodology alone (different field dates: late August vs early October; different samples: adults vs adults/registered/likely voters; different question wording, not published in summaries) may or may not explain. Do not average them. The honest summary is: adult-use support is 55–63% depending on pollster; medical support is 74–75% in both; Republican adult-use support is contested (33–52%). The SurveyUSA–Elon gap on Republicans is itself a gap for Run 2 follow-up: obtain both questionnaires and topline PDFs.

Polling vs lawmaking gap. The central Part 9 political finding is unchanged from Run 1: supermajority public support coexists with House inaction. The structural reasons are (a) no initiative (§9.3.1), (b) Senate–House asymmetry (Senate has passed medical bills twice; House leadership has declined floor votes), and (c) the Advisory Council’s 2027 timetable. Governor Stein’s April 2026 call for broad reform, following the Council interim report, has not produced an enacted bill as of 24 October 2026.

PART 10 — TRIBAL AND INTERSTATE

10.1 Eastern Band of Cherokee Indians (EBCI) — in full

Sovereignty in one paragraph. The EBCI is a federally recognised tribe with its own elections, laws, government and institutions, exercising sovereignty over the Qualla Boundary (primarily Swain and Jackson Counties, with smaller parts in Cherokee, Graham and Haywood Counties) and other trust lands. Tribal law applies on tribal land only. The EBCI Cannabis Control Board (CCB) states this expressly in its FAQs: “The Tribe’s laws on medical cannabis only apply on Tribal land. This means the laws apply on the Qualla Boundary and the Tribe’s other trust lands.” Source: https://ebci-ccb.org/faqs/ . The CCB’s own “About” page (updated to cover adult use) states: “The Cannabis Control Board (CCB) is the office of the EBCI tribal government responsible for regulation of the marijuana industry and hemp sales on Tribal lands. The CCB issues licenses for marijuana businesses and hemp stores, agent cards for the individuals managing and working in marijuana businesses, and patient cards for individuals with certain health conditions. Cherokee Code Chapters 17 and 17A govern the marijuana and hemp industries, including the authority and responsibilities of the CCB.” Source: https://ebci-ccb.org/about-the-board/
