10.1.2 Verbatim operative text — Cherokee Code Ch. 17 (medical/adult-use) and Ch. 14 prohibited conduct

Method note on verbatim text. The CCB’s Ord. No. 63 PDF is a scanned/OCR document. The extracts below are taken from its search-indexed text and from One Feather’s transcription of §14-95.12. OCR garbling (e.g., “cannabis facility” rendered with broken characters) is preserved in meaning but normalised in spelling; any passage where OCR made the operative number uncertain is marked [VERIFY against enrolled PDF] rather than silently cleaned.

A. Retail sale limits — §17 (as amended by Ord. No. 63, from the CCB PDF text). The retail facility duties provision states, in substance and in the PDF’s words (normalised):

“Each cannabis retail facility [dispensary] shall ensure all of the following: (1) That marijuana or marijuana products are not sold to any person under 21 years old as proven by valid, government-issued identification. (2) [That] the weight, concentration, and content of THC in all marijuana and marijuana products that the retail [facility] sells is clearly and accurately stated on the product sold. (3) That the [facility] does not sell to a person, in any one transaction, more than one and one-half (1.5) ounces of marijuana [per day — OCR ambiguous: ‘per day’ appears in the indexed fragment immediately following ‘1.5 ounces’; see §10.1.6 on the limit conflict]. (4) That the retail facility does not sell to a person marijuana products containing more than 2,500 milligrams of THC per day, not to exceed 10,000 milligrams of THC per month. (5) That the legal limits on the possession of marijuana [are] posted clearly and conspicuously within the retail [facility / dispensary area]. (6) That only persons who are at least 21 years of age [or hold a patient card — fragment incomplete in index] are allowed to enter the premises of the [retail facility].”

Source: https://ebci-ccb.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/08/2024-6-27-Ord-No-63-2024-Adult-Use-Cannabis-and-Hemp.pdf

B. Prohibited conduct — §14-95.12 (One Feather transcription, 7 June 2024). Verbatim as reported:

“It shall be unlawful for any person, business, or other entity to: — Possess marijuana when the person is less than 21 years old; — Provide, give, or transfer in any way, marijuana to any person less than 21 years old; — Cultivate marijuana without a valid license, valid agent card, or a valid medical cannabis patient card issued by the EBCI Cannabis Control Board; — Operate any marijuana facility without a valid license issued by the EBCI Cannabis Control Board”

Source: https://theonefeather.com/2024/06/07/dinilawigi-tribal-council-approves-adult-use-cannabis/

Further §14-95.12 provisions reported secondarily (2026 summaries, [VERIFY] against enrolled Ch. 14): public consumption prohibited; no possession/consumption within 100 feet of a school, daycare, church, hospital, tribal government building, public park, playground, community center, or public swimming pool; violation of public-consumption / 100-foot rules is an infraction punishable by a fine of up to $250 plus court costs; giving/transferring to under-21s is a criminal offence with a mandatory minimum $1,000 fine on first offence, escalating to $5,000 minimum and up to six months’ jail on a third offence; consuming while driving on the Boundary is a criminal offence punishable by up to six months’ jail and up to $5,000 fine. Source (secondary, 2026): https://legalclarity.org/is-marijuana-legal-in-cherokee-north-carolina/ — use as a lead, not as enrolled text.

C. Home grow — zero. As clarified by the EBCI Attorney General on the record 27 June 2024 (One Feather): after Res. No. 239 removed the floor amendments, “the ordinance now says that nobody can grow marijuana in their home. So, we went from original, 10 plants. The floor amendment dropped us to four plants. What’s been passed at this point is zero plants for home grow.” Source: https://theonefeather.com/2024/07/01/dinilawigi-tribal-council-reverses-decision-on-hemp-stores-adult-use-cannabis-ratified/?utm_source=rss&utm_medium=rss&utm_campaign=dinilawigi-tribal-council-reverses-decision-on-hemp-stores-adult-use-cannabis-ratified — This is a deliberate contrast with every state adult-use programme in the region: EBCI adult use is retail-only, no home cultivation, even for patients (patient-card cultivation language in §14-95.12’s “valid … patient card” clause is a cultivation-authorisation clause whose current scope [VERIFY] — it does not appear to authorise home grow after Ord. No. 63 as clarified above).

D. Ch. 17A hemp — legislative findings (§17A-1, from CCB PDF indexed text, normalised). In substance: (a) the hemp industry has potential benefit to public health and general welfare of enrolled members, including through job creation and economic development; (b) growth/success of the hemp industry depends on public confidence and trust that people who could benefit from hemp/hemp products can obtain them conveniently and that hemp stores do not unduly impact quality of life of nearby residents/community; (c) hemp store licences are issued responsibly; (d) the hemp industry is free from criminal/corrupt practice; (e) public confidence/trust can only be maintained by strict regulation. Ch. 17A further provides hemp-store licensing (§17A-4), employee cleanliness/health rules (17A CAR 4.08, from the CCB administrative-regulations PDF indexed in this run), and related retail rules. Full Ch. 17A text [VERIFY against enrolled Code] — the PDF index gives fragments, not a clean enrolled chapter.

E. Medical programme — qualifying conditions and patient cards. The CCB FAQ (current) states: CCB “is now accepting applications from North Carolina residents. You must show proof you are a current resident residing in North Carolina.” Age: “Applicants must be at least 21 years old.” The FAQ’s conditions list was truncated in the search index and was not retrieved in full — the current qualifying-conditions list is therefore [VERIFY] against https://ebci-ccb.org/faqs/ . Historically (2021 launch reporting), the Board approved patients who could provide health records showing a condition medical cannabis is proven to help (cancer, chronic pain cited), and the Board extended reciprocity to out-of-state medical cards and other tribal medical cards for medical sales. Whether that reciprocity survives into the adult-use era is moot for most buyers (adult-use needs ID only) but matters for medical-card benefits — [VERIFY].

10.1.3 Who may buy — medical and adult-use, in sequence

Phase Dates Who may buy Proof required Source — Accessed 2026-10-24
Medical only 20 Apr 2024 – 3 July 2024 Adults 21+ holding an EBCI CCB medical cannabis patient card; plus reciprocity for out-of-state medical cards / other tribal medical cards (per Great Smoky press release as reported). NC residency required for a CCB card (current FAQ). Patient card + ID; health records showing qualifying condition (2021–2024 launch reporting). https://theonefeather.com/2026/03/06/growing-and-growing-per-capita-distribution-approved-from-cannabis-sales/ ; https://beardbrospharms.com/cherokee-tribe-in-north-carolina-clarifies-who-can-purchase-from-their-new-medical-retail/ ; https://ebci-ccb.org/faqs/
Adult-use, tribal members + members of other federally recognised tribes 4 July 2024 – 6 Sept 2024 EBCI enrolled members and members of other federally recognised tribes, 21+. Tribal enrolment / tribal ID + age ID (per launch reporting). https://theonefeather.com/2026/03/06/growing-and-growing-per-capita-distribution-approved-from-cannabis-sales/ ; https://theonefeather.com/2026/08/25/cannabis-adult-use-sales-are-two-years-in-and-continuing-to-thrive/
Adult-use, all adults 7 Sept 2024 – present All adults 21+, regardless of tribal membership, NC residency, or state of residence. No NC residency requirement, no patient card, no medical condition. First in line 7 Sept 2024 was reported as a customer from Greenville, South Carolina — illustrating the cross-border draw. Valid government-issued photo ID proving 21+. https://legalclarity.org/can-i-buy-weed-in-cherokee-north-carolina/ ; https://theonefeather.com/2026/08/25/cannabis-adult-use-sales-are-two-years-in-and-continuing-to-thrive/ ; https://themarijuanaherald.com/2024/08/north-carolina-tribe-to-open-marijuana-superstore-to-everyone-21-and-older-in-less-than-a-month/
Medical cards today Present Still issued (NC residents 21+, $100/$50 first issuance — see §10.1.5). Medical vs adult-use purchase benefits (price, tax, limits) not stated on any CCB page retrieved — [VERIFY]. In practice, adult-use access has made the medical card optional for most NC buyers, except any medical-specific limits/tax treatment the CCB may still apply. — https://ebci-ccb.org/faqs/

Out-of-state visitors — the trap in plain language. A visitor from South Carolina, Georgia, Tennessee or Virginia may lawfully buy and possess on the Boundary if 21+, but the lawfulness ends at the Boundary. The visitor’s home state and North Carolina both prohibit the same product off tribal land (see §10.3 neighbour table). No EBCI card, receipt, or tribal purchase creates a defence in NC state court or in the visitor’s home state. Keep the receipt: it proves tribal purchase if questioned on tribal land; it is not a defence off it.

10.1.4 Possession limits on and off the Qualla Boundary

On tribal land (tribal law): the operative retail caps are the §17 caps in §10.1.2A (1.5 oz per transaction; 2,500 mg THC/day; 10,000 mg THC/month for products). Possession limits as distinct from purchase caps were not cleanly retrieved from enrolled Ch. 17 — [VERIFY]. The 100-foot buffer and public-consumption ban (§10.1.2B) apply on top of any possession amount.

Off tribal land (NC state law — the conflict): the moment product crosses the Boundary, NC Ch. 90 applies in full: ≤0.5 oz Class 3 misdemeanour (≤$200, suspended sentence); >0.5–1.5 oz Class 1 misdemeanour; >1.5 oz Class I felony; >10 lb trafficking (§90-95(h)). There is no tribal-purchase exception, no reciprocity, and no “transport” defence in §90-95 as summarised in Run 1. A lawful 1.5 oz tribal purchase therefore becomes, off the Boundary, a Class 1 misdemeanour at the top of the misdemeanour range (and any amount over 1.5 oz a felony) — the purchase that is lawful at the dispensary counter is unlawful on the drive home. This is not a theoretical conflict: secondary reporting consistently warns that “those caught with it outside of tribal land on North Carolina grounds still face a misdemeanour charge for possessing even a single gram and a felony charge for possessing over 1.5 ounces”: https://themarijuanaherald.com/2024/08/north-carolina-tribe-to-open-marijuana-superstore-to-everyone-21-and-older-in-less-than-a-month/

Driving: NC impaired-driving law (§20-138.1) is impairment-based with no per-se THC limit (Run 1 / secondary summary); EBCI law separately criminalises consuming while driving on the Boundary (up to 6 months / $5,000, secondary — [VERIFY] against Ch. 14). Do not conflate them: a driver can violate tribal law on the Boundary and state law off it on the same trip.

10.1.5 Cultivation, processing, retail, testing — operations

Operator (not regulator). Qualla Enterprises, LLC, an EBCI tribal entity, operates both the dispensary (Great Smoky Cannabis Co., in a former bingo hall near Harrah’s Cherokee Casino Resort, ~10,000 sq ft retail, drive-thru service, $50 million renovation reported at launch) and the farm. The CCB regulates; it does not grow or sell. Source: https://themarijuanaherald.com/2024/08/north-carolina-tribe-to-open-marijuana-superstore-to-everyone-21-and-older-in-less-than-a-month/ ; https://legalclarity.org/is-marijuana-legal-in-cherokee-north-carolina/

Cultivation sites.

  • Tsisqwohi (Birdtown) farm, Cherokee, NC — the original and principal farm. 15-acre parcel just outside Birdtown (Citizen Times, Oct 2026): outdoor hoop houses + indoor grow rooms with controlled temperature, automated irrigation, rigorous sanitation (including sterilisation of newly delivered glass jars before use). Capacity reported (Oct 2026, secondary): facilities can hold up to 150,000 plants, production nearing 80,000 pounds in 2026, expected to exceed 110,000 pounds in 2027. Plant counts are operational reporting, not a tribal-code cap — no plant-count cap was found in Ch. 17 text retrieved. Sources: https://www.citizen-times.com/story/news/local/2026/10/19/cherokee-cannabis-industry-nc-legalization/92175880007/ ; https://cardinalpine.com/news/cannabis/great-smoky-cannabis-company-nc-legalization/ ; https://www.mmjdaily.com/article/9884589/cherokee-cannabis-operation-gives-nc-marijuana-council-a-working-regulatory-model/
  • Oconaluftee Cultivation Facility (second property) — coming online for the 2027 expansion (Cardinal Pine, Oct 2026). No acreage, plant count, or opening date beyond “as additional growing space … come online” was retrieved — [VERIFY].
  • No other cultivation site (no private cultivation facility open to the public) was identified. The farm supplies the single dispensary; there is no wholesale market off the Boundary.

Scale (Oct 2026 reporting, secondary — treat as enterprise reporting, not audited tribal financials): >3.5 million products sold since opening; approaching 1 million customer visits; >750,000 orders in the first two years (One Feather, Aug 2026) from customers across NC, SC, GA and TN; ~270 employees (Oct 2026; >250 in Aug 2026), >85% enrolled tribal members (83% in Aug 2026 reporting — small variance by date), >90% of jobs supporting tribal families including relatives; production cost per gram of flower down 50% year-on-year; workforce expected 300–500 eventually, against ~16,500 enrolled members. Sources: https://www.citizen-times.com/story/news/local/2026/10/19/cherokee-cannabis-industry-nc-legalization/92175880007/ ; https://theonefeather.com/2026/08/25/cannabis-adult-use-sales-are-two-years-in-and-continuing-to-thrive/

Testing. The enterprise describes a seed-to-sale system tracking cultivation → processing → laboratory testing → retail, with extensive R&D testing before final product testing, and a stated policy that failed product is discarded (“if we do fail, that product is gone”). Depending on product, testing may evaluate potency, pesticides, heavy metals, microbial contaminants and other quality measures; products must pass applicable quality-assurance testing before reaching the sales floor. No CCB testing regulation (analytes, action limits, lab accreditation, remediation vs destruction rule) was retrieved from an enrolled CCB regulation in this run — [VERIFY] against CCB Title 17 administrative regulations. Do not import another state’s testing panel (e.g., DC/VT panels from other runs) into EBCI. Sources: https://www.visitsmokies.org/leaves-of-a-different-nature/ ; https://www.citizen-times.com/story/news/local/2026/10/19/cherokee-cannabis-industry-nc-legalization/92175880007/

Seed-to-sale vendor — Run 1 gap R1-10 (part): STILL OPEN. Visit Smokies confirms a seed-to-sale system exists; no vendor name (Metrc, BioTrack, or other) was found in any EBCI/CCB source retrieved. A targeted search for “EBCI Metrc” returned only non-EBCI Metrc material. Do not assume Metrc. The vendor, if any, and whether tracking is CCB-mandated by regulation or enterprise-operated, remain open.

Products and payment (secondary, 2026): flower, pre-rolls, vaporizers, concentrates, edibles, tinctures, topicals; flower ~$40–$56 per eighth, pre-rolls from ~$8, edibles ~$20–$34 in one secondary guide; cash or debit only, no credit cards (federal banking reason). Prices include tax (see §10.1.6). Source: https://legalclarity.org/can-i-buy-weed-in-cherokee-north-carolina/ ; https://legalclarity.org/is-marijuana-legal-in-cherokee-north-carolina/

10.1.6 Tax and fees — resolving R1-08, R1-09, R1-10

R1-08 — Tribal tax 7.5% vs 9%: PARTIALLY RESOLVED — 9% is the current cannabis rate on the best 2026 evidence; 7.5% was the general tribal levy, not the current cannabis rate

  • 7.5% origin: Cherokee Code §105-1 imposes “a levy of seven and onehalf percent on all retail sales of goods or merchandise” (Ord. No. 54 (2023) indexed text). Ord. No. 539/593 originally added Ch. 17 cannabis sales to the §105-3 exemption from that levy; Ord. No. 54 (2023) then amended §105-3 “to remove the Tribal levy exemption for sales of medical marijuana / medical cannabis made under C.C. Chapter 17.” In other words, medical cannabis went from levy-exempt to levy-liable at 7.5% in 2023. Sources: https://mcclibraryfunctions-stage.azurewebsites.us/api/ordinanceDownload/13359/1261167/pdf (Ord. No. 54 (2023) indexed text) ; https://theonefeather.com/2021/08/06/council-passes-medical-marijuana-ordinance/
  • 9% current reporting: Citizen Times / Cardinal Pine (19 Oct 2026, enterprise reporting): “Sales generated more than $5 million in tribal sales tax revenue during 2026… Using simple math and the tribe’s 9% tax rate, that comes out to … more than $55.6 million in product out the door over roughly nine months.” Source: https://cardinalpine.com/news/cannabis/great-smoky-cannabis-company-nc-legalization/
  • Resolution: the 7.5% figure is not “wrong” — it was the general levy rate that applied when the medical exemption was removed. The 9% figure is the rate the enterprise and 2026 reporting apply to cannabis sales in 2026. What is still missing is the enrolled tribal-code pinpoint that sets a 9% cannabis rate (a Ch. 105 amendment or a Ch. 17 tax section post-dating Ord. No. 54). Until that pinpoint is opened, state the resolution as: current cannabis tax rate reported as 9% (2026); general tribal levy 7.5% (§105-1); code pinpoint for 9% [VERIFY]. Do not present 7.5% as the current cannabis rate, and do not present 9% as a code-cited rate.
  • State tax: no NC state sales tax is collected on tribal cannabis sales on the Boundary on any source found; the 9% is a tribal sales tax, retained by the Tribe (and, via Ord. No. 112 (2026), partly distributed per capita). NC’s ordinary 4.75% + local sales tax applies to hemp products off tribal land (locked baseline), not to tribal marijuana sales on tribal land.

R1-09 — Purchase limits 1 oz/day vs 1.5 oz/transaction: EXPLAINED, not fully resolved — they are different measures, and the operational cap may be stricter than the code cap

Measure Figure Source type Status
Retail sale cap, per transaction, flower 1.5 oz Enrolled Ch. 17 text (CCB PDF, §17 retail duties) Code cap — strongest source
Retail sale cap, per day / per month, THC products 2,500 mg THC/day; 10,000 mg THC/month Same Ch. 17 provision Code cap — strongest source
Purchase limit, per day / per month, flower (medical launch) 1 oz/day; 6 oz/month 2021 launch reporting (NORML, Cannabis Business Times, Merry Jane summaries of Ord. No. 539) + 2024 medical summaries Launch-era medical limit; may have been superseded or may operate alongside the 1.5 oz transaction cap
Dispensary operational cap (2026 secondary guide) 1 oz flower per visit; 6 oz/month; 2,500 mg/day; 10,000 mg/month concentrates Secondary retail guide (LegalClarity, 2026) Operational practice; if true, the store enforces 1 oz/visit even though the code allows 1.5 oz/transaction

Explanation: there is no arithmetical contradiction between “1.5 oz per transaction” and “1 oz per day” — a customer could in principle make a 1.5 oz transaction only if the daily cap did not bind, and a 1 oz daily cap would bind first. The real question is which cap the CCB/dispensary currently enforces: code transaction cap, launch daily cap, or operational visit cap. R1-09 outcome: PARTIALLY RESOLVED as an explanation (different units: transaction vs day vs month vs THC-mg); STILL OPEN as to the single currently enforced flower cap — obtain the current CCB retail regulation / dispensary posted limits (the code itself requires limits to be “posted clearly and conspicuously within the retail facility”). Do not quote “1 oz/day” as the current code cap, and do not quote “1.5 oz” as a daily allowance without the “per transaction” qualifier.

R1-10 — CCB fee schedule, licence-type list, private-licence availability, seed-to-sale vendor: PARTIALLY RESOLVED (patient/agent/facility fees found in indexed text; licence list and private marijuana licences STILL OPEN)

No. 63 (2024) amended these maxima for adult-use licences is STILL OPEN — the indexed §17-69 text is captioned to Ord. No. 539 (medical era).

Licence-type list — PARTIAL. Medical-era types evidenced: cultivation facility, processing facility, retail facility (dispensary), laboratory, agent card, patient card. Adult-use-era types: the CCB About page says it “issues licenses for marijuana businesses and hemp stores” (plural, undifferentiated). A complete current adult-use licence-type list (cultivation / manufacturing / retail / testing / transport / hemp store, and whether each is a distinct licence) was not retrieved — STILL OPEN.

Private-licence availability — SPLIT FINDING.

  • Hemp stores: private licences EXIST. Multiple privately owned hemp/CBD stores operate on trust lands (e.g., Native Cloud, two stores, owner Robert Mark “Bertie” Saunooke, enrolled member — One Feather, July 2024). The June 2024 floor amendment that would have banned private hemp stores was reversed by Res. No. 239. The October 2026 proposal would restrict future hemp-store licences to enrolled members / member-owned businesses / tribal-owned businesses — which presupposes private licences exist today. Source: https://theonefeather.com/2024/07/01/dinilawigi-tribal-council-reverses-decision-on-hemp-stores-adult-use-cannabis-ratified/?utm_source=rss&utm_medium=rss&utm_campaign=dinilawigi-tribal-council-reverses-decision-on-hemp-stores-adult-use-cannabis-ratified ; https://theonefeather.com/2026/10/22/ordinances-submitted-during-annual-council-deal-with-bear-hunting-hemp-stores-and-more/
  • Marijuana facilities: NO private marijuana facility licence has been evidenced. The only marijuana cultivator, processor and retailer evidenced is Qualla Enterprises, LLC (tribal entity). No CCB public licence register, no second marijuana licensee, and no CCB announcement of private marijuana licensing was found. That is absence of evidence, not proof of a tribal-entity monopoly in code — Ch. 17 on its face provides for facility licences generally. STILL OPEN: does the CCB accept private marijuana-facility applications, and if so on what terms/caps? Do not advise a client that a private marijuana licence is obtainable on the Boundary on current evidence.

10.1.7 Legal conflict with NC law once products leave tribal land — statutes and official positions

Statutes (NC side). Ch. 90, §§90-87 (marijuana/hemp definitions), 90-94 (Schedule VI), 90-95 (possession/manufacture/sale/trafficking penalties, including §90-95(d) suspended-sentence provision and §90-95(h) trafficking >10 lb), §20-138.1 (impaired driving). No tribal-purchase, reciprocity, or transport exception appears in those provisions as summarised in Run 1. The hemp exclusion (§90-87(13a)–(13b), S.L. 2022-32) does not help marijuana: a product above 0.3% delta-9, or marijuana-derived, is marijuana under NC law regardless of where it was bought.

Official positions — what was found and what was not.

  • Found (state executive, advisory): Governor Stein’s Advisory Council (co-chaired by a DA) recommends a statewide regulated market and treats the Cherokee operation as a working regulatory model — i.e., the state executive’s advisory apparatus is not seeking to shut the tribal programme down, and its CEO (Forrest Parker, Qualla Enterprises) sits on the Council. Source: https://www.citizen-times.com/story/news/local/2026/10/19/cherokee-cannabis-industry-nc-legalization/92175880007/
  • Found (federal, historical threat, not action): Rep. Chuck Edwards (federal, not state) threatened to withhold federal highway funds over the 2023 referendum (reported secondarily, Oct 2026). No withholding action was evidenced. Source: https://www.mmjdaily.com/article/9884589/cherokee-cannabis-operation-gives-nc-marijuana-council-a-working-regulatory-model/
  • NOT found (official sources only, as tasked): no published NC Attorney General opinion, no DPS/ALE directive, no SBI bulletin, and no district-attorney or sheriff statement (from an official .gov / office source) addressing EBCI products off tribal land was located in Run 2. Secondary reporting uniformly assumes off-reservation illegality, but assumption is not an official position. This is a stated gap, not a clearance: enforcement discretion by county is unmapped.
  • No state or federal action aimed at the programme was found as an enacted measure, lawsuit, or enforcement action as of 24 Oct 2026. The programme has operated openly for over two years (medical Apr 2024, adult-use Sept 2024) without a located state/federal shutdown attempt. That operational history is evidence of tolerance, not of legal immunity.

10.1.8 Federal law position — including R1-07 (do tribal licences count as “state-licensed”?)

Federal baseline restated. 91 FR 22714 (28 Apr 2026) “places in schedule III (i) those FDA-approved drug products that contain [delta]9-THC falling within the CSA’s definition of marijuana … and (ii) marijuana subject to a state medical marijuana license.” The rule’s “Existing State Regulatory Systems” discussion describes, over three decades, “forty U.S. states [that] have legalized the sale and use of marijuana for medical purposes as a matter of state law” through “a licensing regime that restricts cultivation, manufacture, and distribution to entities approved by a designated state agency — typically a department of health, department of agriculture, or a dedicated cannabis regulatory authority.” Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm?utm_source=copilot.com ; https://justice.gov/opa/media/1437751/dl?rcode=scic-1004%3Fpage%3D

R1-07 outcome: STILL OPEN — and the text as retrieved leans against a simple “yes.”

  • The rule’s operative phrase is “state medical marijuana license” / “state-issued licenses.” No tribal licence, tribal programme, or tribal regulatory system is mentioned in the excerpts retrieved. The rule’s justification (Attorney General review of “these state systems”) is framed entirely in state terms.
  • Secondary legal analysis (Vicente LLP, 2026) makes the same point and argues the omission was likely oversight, not intentional exclusion, noting tribal programmes’ compliance frameworks under the (now-rescinded) Wilkinson Memo and noting that in compact states (Minnesota, Michigan) tribal licensees already engage with state licensees — but argument is not guidance: “there is no reason for excluding Tribal cannabis programs from the Order’s reference to ‘state-issued licenses’” is advocacy, and the article itself asks whether the omission was oversight or intentional exclusion. Source: https://vicentellp.com/insights/cannabis-rescheduling-and-tribal-cannabis-programs-doj-order-implications-for-tribal-operators/
  • No DOJ/DEA guidance, FAQ, or Federal Register correction stating that tribal licences count as “state-licensed” for 91 FR 22714 was located in Run 2. No EBCI-specific federal determination was located.
  • Consequences of the open question (do not overstate): (a) EBCI medical marijuana, if not “state-licensed,” remains Schedule I federally even after 28 Apr 2026, with 280E still applying to the tribal enterprise’s medical sales (tribal tax treatment itself is a separate tribal-sovereignty question — [VERIFY] with tribal tax counsel; do not assume 280E applies to a tribal government entity in the same way as to a private licensee); (b) EBCI adult-use marijuana is Schedule I regardless, because the rule covers medical only; (c) the federal medical-marijuana rider (through 11 Dec 2026, federal baseline) by its terms bars DOJ spending against state medical programmes — whether it covers a tribal programme is the same unresolved “state vs tribal” question, and no rider text retrieved in this run answers it. R1-07 therefore remains [VERIFY] / STILL OPEN, with the burden on anyone claiming Schedule III treatment for EBCI product to produce a DOJ/DEA statement.

Other federal positions. No federal cannabis banking law (federal baseline) — hence cash/debit-only retail. No USCIS/HUD update for the April 2026 rule was located for NC (cf. federal Run 2). Federal hemp law (2018 Farm Bill, PL 119-37/119-103) applies to EBCI hemp stores only to the extent tribal law incorporates it; Ch. 17A is tribal law, not a USDA plan (see §10.2 on AMS plans).

10.1.9 Any state or federal action aimed at the programme — summary

None located as an enacted action. No NC statute, no federal statute, no lawsuit, no injunction, no enforcement raid, and no funding withholding against the EBCI programme was found as of 24 Oct 2026, beyond the historical federal highway-funds threat noted in §10.1.7 (threat, not action) and the EBCI’s own internal protest/resolution process over hemp stores (tribal, not state/federal). The programme’s risk is therefore legal-status risk (off-reservation illegality + federal Schedule I for adult-use + unresolved Schedule III question for medical), not shutdown-action risk on current evidence.

10.2 Any other federally recognised tribes in North Carolina, and state-recognised tribes — with cannabis/hemp operations?

None of the newly recognised tribes has an evidenced cannabis/hemp operation. The table below separates recognition (proved) from operations (not found).

10.2.2 AMS plan-review check — Run 1 gap R1-18

R1-18 outcome: RESOLVED as a negative for Lumbee/Catawba, with a verification caveat. The state table retrieved in full contains no tribal-plan entry for Lumbee or Catawba, and the tribal-plan portion of the AMS page was only partially retrieved in search excerpts. The 17 Feb 2026 date post-dates Lumbee federal recognition (30 Jan 2026) by 18 days, so a Lumbee plan submitted in that window would be unlikely but not impossible to miss. Therefore: no Lumbee or Catawba AMS hemp plan was found on the 17 Feb 2026 list; full tribal-table confirmation [VERIFY] before stating the negative as absolute. EBCI hemp is not an AMS-plan matter (tribal Ch. 17A regulation on trust lands; growers on trust lands for marijuana are tribal-licensed, not USDA-licensed — do not conflate EBCI marijuana cultivation with USDA hemp licensing).

Why Lumbee/Catawba recognition does not create a second Cherokee. EBCI’s programme rests on (a) trust land, (b) a tribal government with a code, court and police, and (c) a CCB licensing apparatus built over 2021–2024. Lumbee has (as of Oct 2026) federal recognition but no evidenced trust-land base for a dispensary, no cannabis code, and no regulator; Catawba has an SC reservation and an NC gaming footprint but no NC trust land identified for cannabis and no cannabis code. Recognition is a precondition, not a programme.

10.3 Interstate issues

10.3.1 Hemp shipments into and out of North Carolina — the federal protection and its limits

The federal rule (verbatim, 2018 Farm Bill §10114).

“(a) Rule Of Construction. — Nothing in this title or an amendment made by this title prohibits the interstate commerce of hemp (as defined in section 297A of the Agricultural Marketing Act of 1946 (as added by section 10113)) or hemp products. (b) Transportation Of Hemp And Hemp Products. — No State or Indian Tribe shall prohibit the transportation or shipment of hemp or hemp products produced in accordance with subtitle G of the Agricultural Marketing Act of 1946 (as added by section 10113) through the State or the territory of the Indian Tribe, as applicable.”

Source: https://harris-sliwoski.com/cannalawblog/transporting-hemp-across-state-lines-is-still-risky/ ; https://cultivalaw.com/affirm-legality-interstate-transportation-hemp/

USDA implementation (7 CFR §990.63, via USDA guidance summaries): “No State or Indian Tribe may prohibit the transportation or shipment of hemp or hemp products lawfully produced under a State or Tribal plan approved under subpart B of this part, under a license issued under subpart C of this part, or under 7 U.S.C. 5940 through the State or territory of the Indian Tribe, as applicable.” Source: https://www.fmcsa.dot.gov/sites/fmcsa.dot.gov/files/2020-07/White_Paper_from_GenCanna_Global_Regarding_Transport_of_Hemp.pdf (USDA rule discussion reproduced therein).

What §10114 protects — and what it does not.

  • Protects: through-shipment of hemp/hemp products lawfully produced under a USDA-approved state/tribal plan or a USDA (subpart C) licence, through a state/tribe — even a state that prohibits hemp production. NC hemp grown under a USDA licence therefore has federal through-shipment protection across, e.g., a prohibition state.
  • Does not protect: (a) marijuana (anything above the applicable hemp definition, or marijuana-derived product) — §10114 is hemp-only; (b) destination-state retail sale that violates destination law — §10114(b) says “through,” and §10114(a) says the Farm Bill title does not prohibit interstate commerce, but neither pre-empts a destination state’s retail, labelling, age, or testing rules for products sold there; (c) non-compliant hemp (no licence, no compliant test, “hot” hemp >0.3% delta-9 under current NC/federal definition) — the protection is expressly conditioned on “produced in accordance with subtitle G”; (d) intoxicating hemp consumables after the federal redefinition — from 11 Dec 2026 (federal baseline), a product that meets NC’s current delta-9-only hemp definition may fail the federal total-THC + 0.4 mg/container definition, and its §10114 protection for federal purposes will be judged under the new federal definition, not NC’s old one. This is the single largest interstate risk in this file for NC shippers in late 2026.
  • Practical risk that §10114 does not remove: roadside seizure / lab-delay risk. Hemp and marijuana are visually and olfactorily indistinguishable; a compliant shipment can still be detained pending lab results, and a shipper’s remedy is after-the-fact. Best practice evidenced in industry guidance (not NC law): bill of lading + cargo manifest, copy of USDA licence, COA, sealed containers, driver training, law-enforcement coordination where available. That guidance is operational advice, not a legal safe harbour.

NC law on shipments (state side). NC has no shipment-specific hemp statute beyond the Ch. 90 definitions and the general criminal provisions: hemp/hemp products (≤0.3% delta-9) are excluded from marijuana and are lawful to possess/transport in NC; marijuana is not. NC has no hemp-transport licence, manifest, or checkpoint regime. Inbound hemp from a USDA-plan state (e.g., VA, SC, TN — all USDA-approved-plan states per the 17 Feb 2026 list: VA Approved Plan, SC Approved Plan, TN Approved Plan) is lawful in NC if it meets NC’s 0.3% delta-9 definition; inbound marijuana from any state (including VA, where possession is legal) is unlawful in NC. Outbound NC hemp to a state with a total-THC or per-serving rule (e.g., GA’s regulated hemp framework, VA’s hemp rules) must meet destination law for sale there, even if it meets NC law.

No NC hemp-shipment seizure dataset, no ALE transport-enforcement guidance, and no NC–neighbour hemp-transport memorandum was located — [UNAVAILABLE].

Neighbour summary in one line: Virginia is the only neighbour where adult possession is lawful — and even there retail is not yet operating — while Tennessee, South Carolina and Georgia mirror NC’s prohibition for marijuana and share its hemp framework. For every neighbour, the NC rule for travellers is identical: what you buy lawfully on the Qualla Boundary becomes unlawful the moment you leave it, in NC and in every neighbouring state (except VA possession, subject to the NC/federal legs above).

10.3.3 Interstate laws, compacts and agreements — explicit negative

Finding: NONE found. No interstate cannabis compact, no hemp compact, no reciprocity agreement, no mutual-recognition agreement, and no NC–neighbour (or NC–tribal, other than the EBCI’s own internal law) cannabis agreement was located in Run 1 or Run 2. Specifically:

  • No NC–VA, NC–TN, NC–SC, or NC–GA cannabis/hemp compact or memorandum.
  • No NC–EBCI cannabis compact (contrast: Catawba has a gaming compact with NC; EBCI has gaming compacts; neither is a cannabis compact, and neither was found to contain a cannabis provision).
  • No EBCI–state medical-card reciprocity agreement with NC (EBCI’s medical reciprocity, where it exists, is tribal recognition of out-of-state/tribal cards for tribal sales, not a state agreement).
  • No interstate hemp-transport agreement beyond the federal §10114 / 7 CFR §990.63 protection itself, which is federal law, not an NC agreement.
  • The Advisory Council’s September 2026 draft discussion of a future state system (including a possible §835-style interstate-compact concept in other states’ debates) is not an NC compact and must not be cited as one.

If a future Run locates a compact, it will be a new finding, not a correction — the negative above is stated as of 24 October 2026 on the sources searched.

10.3.4 Interstate checklist for an NC hemp shipper / EBCI customer (practical, not legal advice)

  1. Hemp shipper (NC → other state): USDA licence + compliant COA (delta-9 ≤0.3% today; total-THC + per-container compliance from 11 Dec 2026 federally) + bill of lading/manifest + destination-law check (per-serving, per-package, age, labelling) for sale in the destination — §10114 covers through-shipment, not destination retail compliance.
  2. Hemp shipper (other state → NC): product must meet NC §90-87 (delta-9 ≤0.3%); VA/SC/TN/GA plan hemp generally will, but a product lawful in its home state under a total-THC rule is judged in NC under NC’s delta-9 rule until NC amends it.
  3. EBCI customer: buy/possess/consume only on tribal land; do not transport off the Boundary; do not drive impaired; do not give/transfer to under-21s (tribal criminal offence + NC offence off land); keep tribal purchase off the public highway in both senses — the product and the impairment.
  4. Employer / DOT note (federal, not interstate): DOT testing unchanged (federal Run 2 baseline); no NC rule was found that protects an employee for off-duty tribal purchase.

PART 11 — OPERATIONS RULES

11.0 The two regulatory worlds — and why they must not be merged

(A) Hemp in NC (outside tribal land) (B) EBCI programme (Qualla Boundary only)
Regulator USDA (growers) + general NC law (no hemp regulator) EBCI Cannabis Control Board (CCB), under Cherokee Code Ch. 17 (medical, Ord. No. 539, 2021) and Ch. 17A / Ord. No. 63 (2024) (adult-use + hemp)
Grower licence USDA hemp producer licence under 7 CFR Part 990, Subpart C Tribal licence; Ord. No. 63 as reported restricts non-laboratory cannabis facility licences to entities wholly owned by the Tribe or its wholly owned subsidiaries
Retail licence None (state) CCB licence required to sell cannabis or hemp/hemp products on tribal land
Testing USDA pre-harvest total-THC test only for growers; NO state finished-product testing mandate Pre-sale quality-assurance batch testing by a licensed cannabis laboratory is mandatory before sale to the public (17A CAR 6.03)
What leaves the boundary Hemp that meets the NC/federal definition may move in commerce; marijuana may not Cannabis lawfully bought on the Boundary becomes unlawful marijuana under NC law the moment it leaves tribal land (see §12.5)

State-licensed question [VERIFY]: Whether a CCB medical licence counts as "state-licensed" for the 28 April 2026 federal Schedule III rule has not been confirmed in any official EBCI, USDA, DEA or ATF source opened for this Part. Do not assume it does. Tribal land is not state land, and the fixed baseline says "state-licensed medical" — the tribal analogue is unconfirmed.

11.1 Testing

11.1.A Hemp — what is required, and what is not

Required (growers only): pre-harvest total-THC testing.

  • Under 7 CFR Part 990, a USDA licensee's lot must be sampled within 30 days before anticipated harvest and tested for total delta-9 THC. Total THC = (%THCA × 0.877) + %delta-9 THC.
  • The sampling agent — not the producer — collects the sample. Producers may not sample their own growing facilities (7 CFR 990.3(a)(2)(i) plan requirement, applied in the USDA plan at §§990.24–990.26).
  • Sampling protocol (plan standard): samples from flowering tops when present, approximately 5–8 inches from the main stem / terminal bud / central cola; method sufficient at 95% confidence that no more than 1% of plants in the lot exceed the acceptable hemp THC level, or an approved performance-based method.
  • After testing: harvest no later than 30 days after sample collection; if harvest is not completed in 30 days, a second pre-harvest sample is required (7 CFR 990.26(b)). Harvested lots shall not be commingled with other lots or material (990.26(c)). Lots meeting the acceptable level may enter commerce (990.26(d)); lots that do not are subject to §990.27 (see §11.5). A producer who believes a result is in error may request additional pre-harvest testing, by the original or another laboratory (990.26(f)).
  • A sample result exceeding the acceptable hemp THC level is conclusive evidence the lot is not in compliance (990.25, as summarised in the eCFR rendering of Part 990).
  • Source: 7 CFR Part 990, Subpart C (eCFR). Accessed 2026-10-24. https://www.ecfr.gov/api/renderer/v1/content/enhanced/2024-04-23/title-7?subtitle=B&chapter=IX&part=990

Not required (state law): finished-product testing.

  • North Carolina imposes NO state testing mandate for hemp-derived consumables sold outside tribal land: no required potency panel, no pesticide/heavy-metal/microbial/solvent panel, no batch, labelling or certificate-of-analysis (COA) mandate in current law. This is the Run 1 baseline and it was not contradicted by any official source opened for Run 2.
  • Voluntary third-party COAs are common in the trade, and the absence of a mandate is precisely why ALE enforcement (see §11.1.D and Gap R1-13) proceeds, when it does, as controlled-substance enforcement for products testing over the delta-9 limit, not as a testing-regime violation.
  • Proposed testing mandates in HB 328 (earlier versions), HB 607 and SB 328 are NOT law. HB 607's summary describes detailed testing for cannabinoids, pesticides, heavy metals and contaminants with per-serving/package limits; SB 328/HB 607 licensing versions are pending. Do not cite them as current requirements.

DEA-registered labs — currency check (Run 1 names verified).

The USDA AMS "Hemp Analytical Testing Laboratories" (DEA laboratories) page, filtered to NC, still lists all four Run 1 names. That is a search-result rendering; the live page should be re-checked before any commercial reliance because the AMS list is updated without a dated changelog on the page face.

Delta 9 Analytical Raleigh, NC Still listed — AMS DEA-labs page, NC filter
NMS Labs – WIN Winston-Salem, NC Still listed — AMS DEA-labs page, NC filter
Research Triangle Park Laboratories, Inc. Raleigh, NC Still listed — AMS DEA-labs page, NC filter
USDA National Science Laboratories Gastonia, NC Still listed — AMS DEA-labs page, NC filter

Source: USDA AMS, Hemp Analytical Testing Laboratories. Accessed 2026-10-24. https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/dea-laboratories?field_lab_location_administrative_area=NC

DEA-registration enforcement delay — fixed baseline applied: After 31 December 2022 USDA licensees were to use only DEA-registered labs (7 CFR 990.25), but USDA has repeatedly delayed enforcement; the current delay runs to 31 December 2026. Until that date a non-DEA lab test is not automatically invalid for USDA purposes on registration grounds alone — all other sampling/testing requirements still apply. A lab's DEA registration does not by itself make it NC-licensed, ISO-accredited, or approved for finished-product testing, none of which NC requires.

Failed tests and recalls (hemp, outside tribal land):

  • There is NO NC hemp recall statute, NO NC hemp recall register, and NO state power to order a hemp-product recall found in this research. The grower-level consequence of a failed pre-harvest test is disposal or remediation under §990.27 (see §11.5), not a consumer recall.
  • For finished products, the only recall-like mechanisms are general law: FDA/FTC consumer-protection and adulteration/misbranding law for foods/drugs/cosmetics, NC consumer-protection law, and voluntary retailer/manufacturer recalls. No NC hemp-specific recall procedure was located [VERIFY — absence confirmed only to the extent of searches in this Part; a future NCDA&CS or NCDHHS recall page, if published, would supersede this].
  • A product that tests over 0.3% delta-9 is not "recalled" as hemp — it is treated as marijuana under NC controlled-substances law, with seizure/prosecution risk (see §12.1 and R1-13).

11.1.B EBCI programme — testing

Medical cannabis laboratories — Cherokee Code (Ord. No. 539).

The Board shall establish standards for and certify one or more cannabis laboratories to test cannabis and cannabis products to be sold within tribal jurisdiction. Such a laboratory must be able to:

"(i) The concentration therein of THC and cannabidiol. (ii) The presence and identification of microbes, molds, and fungus. (iii) The composition of the tested material. (iv) The presence of chemicals in the tested material, including, without limitation, pesticides, heavy metals, herbicides, or growth regulators."

and must demonstrate the validity and accuracy of its methods. To obtain a laboratory licence an applicant must apply under §17-52, pay the required fee, and "become accredited pursuant to standard ISO/IEC 17025 … within one year after licensure."

Source: EBCI Ord. No. 539 (2021), Cherokee Code §17-54, as reproduced in the CCB's Ord. No. 63 compilation PDF. Accessed 2026-10-24. https://ebci-ccb.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/08/2024-6-27-Ord-No-63-2024-Adult-Use-Cannabis-and-Hemp.pdf — note: the PDF text is OCR-degraded in places; pinpoints for fee amounts and the full laboratory section should be re-verified against the Cherokee Code publisher's clean text [VERIFY — fee amount and full lab section].

Hemp retail — 17A CAR Chapter 6 (effective 30 January 2025 version located).

  • 17A CAR 6.02: "A licensee must ensure that the hemp and hemp products it sells and offers [for] sale meet the laboratory testing requirements of this chapter. Licensees are neither required nor permitted to perform testing required by this chapter pursuant to their license, rather the testing must be completed by a licensed cannabis testing laboratory."
  • Testing may be performed by "any cannabis laboratory which is licensed to perform analytical testing on cannabis in the jurisdiction in which it is located. Testing by a laboratory which is not licensed to analyze cannabis is insufficient."
  • Impartiality safeguards: a laboratory may not offer a different fee schedule or waive payment for failing/undesirable results; no refunds/rebates/alternate compensation for failing results; the licensee may not have a financial/ownership interest in, common ownership with, or operational control over the laboratory; the Board may reject any laboratory to protect health, safety and welfare.
  • 17A CAR 6.03(a): "Before hemp or hemp products may be sold to the public it must pass quality assurance batch testing by a cannabis laboratory. No hemp or hemp product of any kind or type may be sold to a consumer without complying with the quality assurance batch testing of this chapter."
  • 17A CAR 6.03(b): tests "may include moisture content, potency analysis, foreign matter inspection, microbial screening, pesticide, and other chemical residue and metals screening and residual solvents levels." A laboratory may retrieve samples from another facility's premises and transport them directly to the laboratory, making multiple stops only to retrieve samples, with samples secured and uncontaminated at all times. The Board may inquire into and verify collection/sampling processes.
  • 17A CAR 6.03(c) then sets out a product-by-test "Action Levels" table. The table itself was NOT legible in the text rendering available for this Part [VERIFY — full action-levels table, including numeric limits for each analyte/product type, must be obtained from the clean 17A CAR PDF before quoting any limit].
  • Source: 17A CAR (30 Jan 2025), Ch. 6, via Municode Library ordinance download. Accessed 2026-10-24. https://mcclibraryfunctions-stage.azurewebsites.us/api/ordinanceDownload/13359/1352061/pdf

EBCI cannabis (marijuana) — testing in practice:

  • Great Smoky Cannabis Company (Qualla Enterprises, tribally owned) operates a seed-to-sale system tracking cultivation → processing → laboratory testing → retail, and products must pass applicable quality-assurance testing before reaching the sales floor; depending on product, testing may evaluate potency, pesticides, heavy metals, microbial contaminants and other quality measures. Source: Visit Smokies, "Leaves of a Different Nature" (updated 6 days before Accessed 2026-10-24 — secondary, descriptive). https://www.visitsmokies.org/leaves-of-a-different-nature/
  • No public EBCI list of currently certified cannabis laboratories, and no public EBCI failed-test / recall register, was located in this Part [VERIFY / UNAVAILABLE]. Do not name a specific EBCI testing laboratory as currently certified without a CCB source.

Failed tests / recalls (EBCI):

  • No EBCI-specific recall regulation text was opened in full for this Part. The Board's general powers include suspension/revocation and disciplinary action, inspection, and enforcement mechanisms (Ord. No. 539 §17-… Board regulations), and hemp retailers are "responsible for the content, safety, and quality of the hemp and hemp products [they sell]" (17A CAR Ch. 6). The operational consequence of a failed batch test under 6.03 is that the batch may not be sold — it does not pass. Whether failed batches must be destroyed, remediated, or returned, and on what documentation, is [VERIFY — not stated in the 6.02/6.03 excerpts located].
  • Cultivation and processing are NOT authorised under a hemp-retail licence: 17A CAR 5.01–5.02 provide that hemp cultivation on tribal lands is governed by the EBCI Hemp Regulatory Plan in Title 16, Ch. 1 of Cherokee Administrative Regulations, carried out by the EBCI Natural Resources Department in cooperation with USDA, and that a retail licensee must acquire hemp/hemp products "in a form which is processed, tested, packaged, and ready for sale" — it may not cultivate or process under that licence (it may amend labelling/packaging with Board approval if safety is not compromised).
