11.1.D ALE hemp enforcement — what is actually evidenced (see also Gap R1-13)

No official ALE hemp-enforcement dataset, dashboard, seizure total, or annual report was located. What is evidenced is episodic, secondary-reported enforcement:

  • A Pennsylvania Senate GOP staff compilation on state enforcement (2025) states NC ALE "has been actively cracking down on vape and tobacco shops selling Delta-8 THC products that exceed the legal THC limit," with multiple businesses targeted, arrests and raids, particularly after community complaints and for products marketed to children. That is a secondary summary, not an ALE publication.
  • Carolina Hemp Hut (Hillsborough/Durham): February 2024 ALE enforcement action (seizures + controlled buys involving THCA flower); September 2025 press release reporting a conditional-discharge (G.S. 90-96) plea offer structured as an Alford plea, under review, Orange County Case 24-393-195 as cited in that release. The underlying testing/storage dispute (decarboxylation over time, storage/transport methods) is the defendant's account, not a court finding.
  • The Hempary, Graham, NC: raided by police in 2025 and since closed, as reported in Indy Week / The Assembly investigation (2025). No ALE case number or seizure weight was published in the sources opened.
  • Conclusion for R1-13: STILL OPEN as an official-data gap (see Gaps list).

11.2 Licence rules — transfers, ownership changes, renewals, relocation, revocation, insurance/bonding

11.2.A Hemp — USDA licence (the only grower licence in NC)

Text — 7 CFR 990.21–990.22 (verbatim operative language):

"Licenses will be valid until December 31 of the year three [years] after the year in which the license was issued." (§990.22(b)(2)) "Licenses may not be sold, assigned, transferred, pledged, or otherwise disposed of, alienated or encumbered." (§990.22(b)(3)) "USDA hemp producer licenses must be renewed prior to license expiration. Licenses are not automatically renewed. Applications for renewal shall be subject to the same terms, information collection requirements, and approval criteria … for initial applications unless there has been an amendment to the regulations in this part or the law since approval of the initial or last application." (§990.21(b)) "A license modification is required if there is any change to the information submitted in the application including, but not limited to, sale of a business, the production, handling, or storage of hemp in a new location, or a change in the key participants under a license." (§990.21(c))

Reconciliation — transfer vs modification: §§990.22(b)(3) and 990.21(c) sit in tension and are often misread. The licence itself may not be sold/assigned/transferred. A sale of the business or a change in key participants (defined at §990.1 as a sole proprietor, a partner in a partnership, or a person with executive managerial control in a corporation) triggers a modification requirement — it does not automatically transfer the licence. Secondary commentary (Vicente LLP, hemp M&A) notes the interim/final rule is silent on the modification process and that an equity sale of the licensed entity is the most plausible pathway, but that is commentary, not USDA guidance. Practical rule for this dossier: do not describe a USDA hemp licence as transferable. Describe it as non-transferable, modifiable on sale / key-participant / location change, and renewable only on a fresh-equivalent application before expiry.

Sources: 7 CFR 990.21, 990.22. Accessed 2026-10-24. https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/7/990.21 ; https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/7/990.22

Other USDA licence conditions:

  • Felony ban: a person with a state or federal felony conviction relating to a controlled substance is subject to a 10-year ineligibility restriction on participating in/producing hemp under the USDA plan (§990.20(b) / §990.22(a) criteria), subject to the exceptions in the rule text [VERIFY — exact exception wording].
  • Criminal-history report for the applicant / all key participants, dated within 60 days of application, is required for a complete application (§990.20).
  • Multiple licences: an applicant producing in more than one state/tribal territory may hold more than one licence; an operation in a state/tribal-plan jurisdiction must be licensed under that plan, not the USDA plan (§990.22(c)). For NC growers this matters only if they also grow in a plan state or on EBCI land under the tribal plan.
  • Denial: USDA must give written reasons; appeal under Subpart D (§990.22(b)(4)).
  • Violations / revocation: Subpart C compliance (§990.28) and Subpart D appeals/violations govern negligent vs culpable violations; negligent violations (e.g. failure to provide a land description, failure to obtain a licence, producing over the limit without culpable intent) are addressed through corrective-action plans, while culpable violations can lead to licence revocation and ineligibility. The full negligent/criminal-violation taxonomy was not re-quoted here because the eCFR rendering truncated §990.28 in the fetch [VERIFY — §990.28 full text before quoting negligent-violation consequences].
  • Insurance / bonding minimums (USDA hemp): NONE. 7 CFR Part 990 imposes no insurance or surety-bond minimum for USDA hemp producers. Crop insurance, if any, is via USDA Risk Management Agency hemp crop-insurance products (a separate programme, not a licence condition) and private policies — not a Part 990 licence rule. Do not invent a dollar minimum.
  • State hemp licence rules: NONE, because there is no state licence. No NC transfer, renewal, relocation, revocation, insurance or bonding rule exists for hemp producers/processors/retailers outside tribal land. General business law (entity registration, local zoning, sales-tax registration) still applies, but it is not a hemp-licence regime.

11.2.B EBCI programme

  • Facility licences — tribal ownership restriction: As reported from Ord. No. 63 proceedings, "There shall be no cannabis facility license, other than a license for a cannabis laboratory, issued to any person or entity other than an entity wholly owned by the Tribe organized under Tribal law or one of its wholly owned subsidiaries." That is a secondary report of a floor amendment (Cherokee One Feather, 7 June 2024). The clean Ord. No. 63 text should be checked before quoting it as the operative restriction [VERIFY — pinpoint in Ord. No. 63].
  • Board regulation power: The Board may issue regulations under Cherokee Code Ch. 150 covering financial requirements for licensees, investigative/enforcement mechanisms, cultivation/processing/manufacture/transport/distribution/testing/study/advertising/sale requirements, signage/marketing/display/advertising restrictions, application forms, suspension/revocation/disciplinary procedures, and safe/healthful operation including diversion/theft protection, facility oversight, record-keeping, agent conduct, and facility security (Ord. No. 539, Board regulations section).
  • Transfers / ownership / renewal / relocation / revocation / insurance-bonding (EBCI): No complete EBCI licence-transfer, renewal-term, relocation, or insurance/bonding schedule was located in a clean, quotable CCB regulation text in this Part. The Board's suspension/revocation power is established; the grounds, notice, hearing, term length, renewal fee, transferability, and any insurance/bond minimum for each facility type are [VERIFY — require the current CCB licence regulations / application packet]. Do not import NC ABC, NC medical, or another tribe's terms.
  • Hemp retail licences (EBCI): A licence under Cherokee Code Ch. 17A is required to sell hemp/hemp products on tribal land; it does not authorise cultivation or processing (17A CAR 5.01–5.02, above). Renewal/transfer terms for hemp-retail licences were not legible in the excerpts located [VERIFY].
  • Agent cards: Cultivation without a valid licence, valid agent card, or valid medical patient card is prohibited conduct (Ord. No. 63 prohibited-conduct list, see §12.5). Agent-card eligibility, fee and renewal are [VERIFY].

11.3 Cultivation rules — hemp

11.3.A USDA hemp cultivation (NC growers)

  • Plot / FSA registration: Producers must report hemp crop acreage to the Farm Service Agency (FSA), consistent with 7 CFR 990.7. The plan must collect a legal description of the land, including geospatial location to the extent practicable, and a licence/authorization identifier for each producer (§990.3(a)(1), (a)(10)). In practice this means: USDA licence application lists each production location (field / greenhouse / indoor site) with acreage/square footage; FSA acreage reporting (Form FSA-578 / farm records) is the federal farm-record counterpart. A grower who moves production to a new location must seek a licence modification (§990.21(c)) — see §11.2.A.
  • Lot discipline: Each lot is a contiguous area / batch as defined in the licence application and sampling plan; harvested lots shall not be commingled (§990.26(c)).
  • Sampling before harvest: See §11.1.A — within 30 days before anticipated harvest, by a sampling agent, flowering tops 5–8 inches, 95%/1% confidence standard.
  • Indoor vs outdoor: 7 CFR Part 990 does not impose a separate indoor/outdoor hemp rule set in the excerpts located. Indoor (greenhouse / indoor) production is licensable if the location is listed, sampled, and reported like any other location; the sampling protocol's "flowering tops" language applies whether grown indoors or outdoors. No NC indoor-specific hemp rule (lighting, ventilation, setback) was located. General NC law (building, fire, electrical, pesticide-applicator, water) still applies to indoor facilities as it would to any agricultural/industrial building [VERIFY — no hemp-specific indoor rule found; absence is the finding].
  • Variety / seed: No NC or USDA approved-variety list for hemp was located in this Part. Seed must be from hemp varieties; "verified propagules for cultivation if the seeds originate from hemp varieties" appear in the NC hemp-products definition (S.L. 2022-32 summary). No NC seed-certification mandate for hemp was located [VERIFY].
  • Research cultivation: See §11.9 — research producers must hold a USDA licence, but hemp for research not entering commerce is exempt from §§990.24/990.26 sampling if the producer adopts a USDA-approved alternative sampling method meeting the 95% confidence standard (§990.21(d)(1)).

11.3.B EBCI cultivation

  • Marijuana cultivation: Only under a CCB licence (or, for home cultivation, an enrolled-member medical card — see below). Tribal-government control of sale of all forms of cannabis on tribal lands is stated in Ord. No. 63 proceedings.
  • Home cultivation (medical, enrolled members only): Secondary reporting (LegalClarity, summarising CCB/One Feather material) states enrolled EBCI members holding a medical card and living on tribal land may grow up to 8 plants, no more than 4 flowering at any time, out of plain view and secured against minors/unauthorised access. Other NC residents holding an EBCI medical card may NOT home-grow, even with a valid card. This 8/4 figure is secondary and was not verified against clean Cherokee Code text in this Part [VERIFY — home-cultivation plant count and security wording].
  • Hemp cultivation on tribal lands: Governed by the EBCI Hemp Regulatory Plan (Title 16, Ch. 1, Cherokee Administrative Regulations), carried out by the EBCI Natural Resources Department in cooperation with USDA (17A CAR 5.01(a)). The plan text itself was not opened in this Part [VERIFY — plan's plot, sampling and reporting rules].
  • No NC cultivation rule applies on the Boundary for tribal-licensed cultivation; NC's general prohibition on marijuana cultivation (G.S. 90-95(a)(1), (a)(2)) does not, by its terms, govern tribally licensed conduct on trust land, but it does govern the same conduct off the Boundary.

11.4 Security requirements

11.4.A Hemp (USDA / NC)

  • USDA hemp: NO standalone security rule in 7 CFR Part 990 located. There is no federal hemp fence, camera, alarm, locked-storage, or seed-to-sale security mandate in Part 990 comparable to state medical-cannabis security rules. What Part 990 does impose is: accurate land/location records, lot separation, sampling-agent access, inspection access, and 3-year record retention (see §990.71 reporting / §990.28 compliance — full retention period to be verified against §990.71 text [VERIFY — retention period pinpoint]). A producer must allow USDA/state/tribal inspectors/auditors access during reasonable business hours.
  • NC hemp (state): NO hemp-specific security rule. General law applies: theft/trespass law, any local zoning condition, any lease condition, and any private insurer's requirements. A hemp retailer or processor that also holds an ABC, food, or general business licence must meet that licence's security conditions, if any — none is a hemp rule.
  • Practical consequence: Security claims in NC hemp marketing ("DEA-compliant vault," "state-approved security plan") have no NC hemp-security standard to comply with. Treat such claims as private/contractual, not regulatory.

11.4.B EBCI programme

  • General security power: Ord. No. 539 Board regulations include "(v) Provisions for the security of facilities" and "(i) The manner of protecting against diversion and theft without imposing an undue burden" — the full sub-paragraphs were OCR-degraded in the compilation PDF [VERIFY — full security text].
  • Seed-to-sale: Great Smoky Cannabis Company operates a seed-to-sale tracking system (cultivation → processing → testing → retail) per Visit Smokies. Whether seed-to-sale is a CCB regulation mandating a specific vendor (e.g. Metrc) or a Qualla Enterprises operational choice was not confirmed [VERIFY — vendor / mandate].
  • Hemp retail security: No hemp-retail-specific security rule (cameras, safes, transport) was legible in the 17A CAR excerpts located [VERIFY]. Hemp-retail licensees must abide by all applicable tribal laws on the premises and in the course of duties (17A CAR Ch. 6 preamble excerpt).
  • Transport security (EBCI): Transport of marijuana/hemp/hemp products in the passenger area of a motor vehicle other than in the manufacturer's unopened original container is prohibited conduct on tribal land (Ord. No. 63 list) — see §12.5. That is a consumer-transport rule, not a licensed-transport security rule; licensed transport (facility-to-facility) rules were not located [VERIFY].

11.5 Waste disposal — including non-compliant hemp destruction

11.5.A Hemp — non-compliant plants (7 CFR 990.27, verbatim)

"(a) Cannabis plants exceeding the acceptable hemp THC level constitute marijuana, a schedule I controlled substance under the Controlled Substances Act (CSA), 21 U.S.C. 801 et seq., and producers must either use a DEA-registered reverse distributor or law enforcement to dispose of non-compliant plants or ensure the disposal of such cannabis plant on site at the farm or hemp production facility. (b) Producers must notify USDA of their intent to dispose of or remediate non-conforming plants and verify disposal or remediation by submitting required documentation. (c) If a producer elects to perform remediation activities, an additional sampling and testing of the post-remediated crop must occur to determine THC concentration levels."

Source: 7 CFR 990.27. Accessed 2026-10-24. https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/7/990.27

Approved disposal methods (USDA "Hemp Disposal Activities" guidance, as summarised in plan documents): plowing under, mulching/composting, disking, bush mowing / shredding, deep burial, and burning — in each case rendering the material non-retrievable / unusable. A producer may also use a DEA-registered reverse distributor or law enforcement. The producer bears resampling/remediation/disposal costs. Records of disposal/remediation must be available to inspectors/auditors during reasonable business hours (§990.28 / plan practice). Documentation is via the reverse distributor's documentation or the USDA Hemp Plan Producer Disposal Form.

Remediation: Typically removal of flower material (highest THC concentration) while retaining stalks/leaves, or other USDA-accepted remediation, followed by re-sampling/re-testing. If the post-remediation lot still fails, it must be disposed of. Remediation is an election, not a right to sell a failed lot.

General hemp waste (compliant material): No NC or USDA hemp-specific waste rule for compliant stalks, leaves, spent biomass, or processing waste was located. Compliant hemp waste is agricultural/industrial waste under general NC environmental law (see §11.6); it is not hazardous waste by virtue of being hemp, and it is not "marijuana waste" if it meets the hemp definition. Processors handling solvents or other chemicals must follow those chemicals' waste rules (see §11.6).

Laboratory waste: Laboratories should have an effective disposal procedure for non-compliant samples as part of internal SOPs (plan practice). No NC lab-waste rule specific to hemp was located [VERIFY].

11.5.B EBCI programme — waste

  • No EBCI waste-disposal regulation text (failed-batch destruction, facility waste, laboratory sample disposal) was opened in full for this Part. The Board's safe/healthful-operation and inspection powers would be the authority for any such rule, but the rule itself is [VERIFY / UNAVAILABLE in the excerpts located].
  • For hemp grown under the EBCI Hemp Regulatory Plan, USDA-plan disposal/remediation principles would be expected to apply by analogy (plan must include a disposal/remediation procedure under §990.3(a)(6)), but the tribal plan's specific methods were not opened [VERIFY].
  • Do not cite another state's cannabis-waste rule (e.g. rendering unusable + mixing with non-cannabis waste) as an EBCI rule.

11.6 Environmental rules — water, pesticides, energy

11.6.A Water

  • Hemp (NC/USDA): NO hemp-specific water rule located. No NC hemp irrigation permit, water-use reporting, or hemp-specific discharge rule was found. General NC water law applies as to any crop: riparian / groundwater withdrawal law, any applicable water-use registration for large withdrawals, erosion/sedimentation and stormwater rules for land disturbance, and any local water/sewer conditions for indoor/processing facilities. A hemp grower is not exempt from those general rules, and no hemp exemption was located.
  • EBCI: No EBCI cannabis/hemp water rule was located [VERIFY]. Tribal environmental authority on trust land is a tribal/federal question; no CCB water rule was opened.

11.6.B Pesticides — the most commonly misstated area

What is firmly established:

  • EPA has historically stated there are no pesticides registered by EPA specifically for use on cannabis, and no tolerances established for marijuana or hemp (EPA PPDC hemp session paper, 2019). That 2019 position is old; it must not be quoted as the 2026 position without the update below.
  • NCDA&CS Pesticide Division guidance (August 2018, reported via NC State Extension / Morning Ag Clips): "There are no EPA labeled pesticides or herbicides for hemp" in the sense of hemp-specific labels at that time; however, FIFRA §25(b) minimum-risk pesticides that are labelled for industrial hemp, or labelled broadly enough that industrial hemp can be considered covered, may be used in NC, provided (for consumable by-products) ingredients are approved for food crops and the product is labelled for food crops, and provided no label language prohibits application to industrial hemp. Label language supersedes general guidance.
  • Source (2018 guidance, via Extension reporting): https://m.farms.com/news/what-pesticides-can-i-apply-to-my-industrial-hemp-in-nc-138385.aspx ; https://www.morningagclips.com/pesticide-applications-to-industrial-hemp-in-north-carolina/ — both secondary reports of NCDA&CS guidance, Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • NCDA&CS current hemp page confirms the Department "no longer offers registration services for hemp processors" and that NC "no longer possesses the regulatory authority to oversee or administer a regulatory system for the licensing or regulation of hemp producers and processors," referring growers to USDA. Source: NCDA&CS, Plant Industry – Hemp in NC. Accessed 2026-10-24. https://www.ncagr.gov/divisions/plant-industry/hemp-nc

What is NOT firmly established for 2026:

  • A secondary NC hemp overview (2025/2026) claims "The EPA has approved [REMOVED — EPA count unverified; see audit table] for use on hemp plants." That figure was NOT verified against an EPA list in this Part and conflicts in spirit with the older "no EPA-registered hemp pesticide" position, because EPA has since allowed hemp as a listed crop on some labels via label amendments. Treat the "[REMOVED — EPA count unverified]" figure as [VERIFY] — do not quote it as an EPA count until the EPA hemp pesticide list is opened.
  • NC pesticide-applicator licensing: General NC pesticide law (G.S. Chapter 143, Article 52; NCDA&CS Pesticide Section) requires applicator licensing/certification for restricted-use pesticides and for commercial application, and label compliance is the law (FIFRA + NC law: "the label is the law"). Whether a particular hemp grower's application requires a commercial applicator licence depends on the product's classification and whether application is for hire — that is general pesticide law, not a hemp rule. No hemp-specific applicator category was located [VERIFY — applicator category pinpoint].
  • EBCI pesticide testing vs pesticide use: EBCI testing screens for pesticides/herbicides/growth regulators (Ord. No. 539; 17A CAR 6.03(b) "pesticide, and other chemical residue" screening), but no EBCI approved-pesticide list or application rule was located [VERIFY]. Testing for residues is not the same as authorising use.

11.6.C Energy

  • Hemp (NC/USDA): NO hemp-specific energy rule located. No NC energy-efficiency, lighting, or carbon rule for hemp cultivation/processing was found. General building/energy codes and utility rules apply as to any facility.
  • EBCI: No EBCI cannabis energy rule was located [VERIFY].
  • Do not import another state's energy rule (e.g. Vermont Rule 2.5 PPE standard) into NC.

11.7 Sampling, giveaways, discounts, promotions

11.7.A Hemp (outside tribal land) — current law: no rule

  • Sampling (consumer samples): No NC statute or rule prohibits or authorises free hemp samples as such. The absence of a rule means a sample is governed by the same law as a sale: if the sample is hemp (≤0.3% delta-9), it is not a controlled substance; if it is over the limit, giving it away is delivery of marijuana (G.S. 90-95(a)(1)). General food/drug law (no adulterated/misbranded product, no unapproved drug claims) and any local ordinance still apply.
  • Proposed bans (NOT law): Earlier HB 328 versions (Chapter 18D) would have prohibited knowingly distributing samples of hemp-derived consumable products "in or on a public street, sidewalk, park, or public building" (§18D-101(a)(2) in the 4th-edition / committee texts). The conference-report text's sample provisions were not separately verified in this Part [VERIFY — conference text on samples]. In any event, none of it is law.
  • Discounts / promotions: No NC hemp-specific discount, coupon, loyalty, bundle, or advertising rule exists. General advertising law (no false/misleading claims, no unapproved disease claims for foods/drugs, FTC Act, NC Unfair and Deceptive Trade Practices Act G.S. 75-1.1) applies. No NC rule prohibits hemp advertising to adults, and no NC rule requires health warnings on hemp products.
  • Youth-targeted marketing: No NC hemp-specific ban on youth-targeted packaging/advertising exists in current law (contrast the ALE enforcement focus, in practice, on products marketed to children — that focus is enforcement discretion under controlled-substances law, not a marketing statute).

11.7.B EBCI programme

  • No EBCI giveaway/discount/promotion regulation text was opened in full. The Board may impose "reasonable restrictions on the signage, marketing, display, and advertising of cannabis facilities" (Ord. No. 539), provided it does not require a facility to obtain Board approval before using a logo/sign/advertisement (as summarised in the OCR text — pinpoint [VERIFY]).
  • What is prohibited conduct (Ord. No. 63 list) that bounds promotions: providing/giving/transferring marijuana to a person under 21; selling without a CCB licence; consuming in public; possessing/consuming within 100 feet of a school, daycare, church, hospital, tribal government building, public park, playground, community club building, public swimming pool, or community facility (Ch. 17 definition). A "free sample" given in public or to a minor would fall within those prohibitions; a discount to an adult 21+ on tribal land, in a licensed facility, is not separately prohibited in the texts located [VERIFY — discount/loyalty rule, if any].
  • Hemp retail promotions: No 17A CAR promotion/discount rule was legible in the excerpts located [VERIFY].

11.8 Drug testing rules for licence holders and staff — CSERA (Gap R1-19)

11.8.A CSERA — pinpoint RESOLVED

Citation: North Carolina Controlled Substance Examination Regulation Act (CSERA), G.S. Chapter 95, Article 20, §§95-230 to 95-235. Enforced by the NC Department of Labor, Wage and Hour Bureau.

§95-230 Purpose (verbatim):

"The General Assembly finds that individuals should be protected from unreliable and inadequate examinations and screening for controlled substances. The General Assembly also finds that employers who test employees for controlled substances shall use reliable and minimally invasive examinations and screenings and be afforded the opportunity to select from a range of cost-effective and advanced drug testing technologies. The purpose of this Article is to establish procedural and other requirements for the administration of controlled substance examinations." (1991, c. 687, s. 1; 2001-487, s. 66(a).)

Source: G.S. 95-230 (Justia 2025 rendering; official ncleg Article 20 PDF via proxy fetch). Accessed 2026-10-24. https://law.justia.com/codes/north-carolina/chapter-95/article-20/section-95-230/

§95-231 Definitions (operative pinpoints):

  • "Approved laboratory" means a clinical chemistry laboratory performing controlled-substances testing that has demonstrated satisfactory performance in the forensic urine drug testing programmes of HHS or the College of American Pathologists for the type of tests/controlled substances evaluated (§95-231(1)).
  • "Controlled substance" is as defined in G.S. 90-87(5) or a metabolite thereof (§95-231(1a)) — so marijuana (Schedule VI) and its metabolites are within CSERA.
  • "Examiner" means a person/firm/corporation doing business in NC, including state/county/municipal employers, who is the employer/prospective employer and who performs or has performed by an approved laboratory a controlled substance examination (§95-231(2)).
  • "Examinee" means an employee or applicant requested/required to submit to examination (§95-231(3)).

§95-232 Procedural requirements (summary of operative rules, from the Article 20 text):

  • Collection under reasonable, sanitary conditions; individual dignity preserved to the extent practicable; collection reasonably calculated to prevent substitution/interference; samples for prospective/current employees may be collected on-site or at an approved laboratory.
  • Screening vs confirmation: a positive screening result for a current employee must be confirmed (secondary testing) unless the examinee signs a waiver agreeing to the preliminary result; current-employee results shall only be uncovered at an approved laboratory (prospective-employee single-use on-site screening is treated differently in secondary summaries — pinpoint [VERIFY — §95-232(c)–(e) full text]).
  • Chain of custody must be followed.
  • Examinee rights: written notice of rights/responsibilities when the sample is taken; employer pays for the original test; examinee may request a retest at the same or another approved laboratory (examinee bears retest cost in secondary summaries); employer must notify the employee of a positive result and retest rights within 30 days of the positive laboratory result (secondary summary — pinpoint [VERIFY — §95-232 notice period]).
  • CSERA does NOT require any employer to test. It regulates how testing, when done, must be done. It does not specify mandatory testing circumstances (pre-employment, random, post-accident, reasonable suspicion, follow-up are employer-policy choices, not CSERA mandates). It does not create a cannabis-specific accommodation, and it does not protect lawful hemp or tribal-cannabis use: a lawful EBCI purchase gives no CSERA protection, and a positive marijuana test, even from lawful hemp use, is a positive controlled-substance result under CSERA's definitions.
  • Penalties: Secondary summaries cite G.S. 95-234 civil penalties up to $250 per affected examinee, maximum $1,000 per investigation. That pinpoint was not verified against clean §95-234 text in this Part [VERIFY — §95-234 penalty text].

Gap R1-19 disposition: RESOLVED as to pinpoint (Art. 20, §§95-230–95-235, purpose and definitions quoted above); STILL OPEN as to the full §95-232 confirmation/notice and §95-234 penalty pinpoints, which require the clean ncleg Article 20 PDF.

11.8.B Who CSERA does and does not cover

Employer / worker CSERA applies?
NC private employer testing NC employees (including hemp retailers, processors, farms) Yes — if it tests, it must follow CSERA
NC state/county/municipal employer Yes — expressly included in "examiner"
Federal DOT-regulated safety-sensitive testing (CDL drivers, etc.) DOT rules (49 CFR Part 40) govern the federal test; CSERA is not a substitute for DOT compliance. DOT testing is unchanged by the April 2026 Schedule III rule per the fixed federal baseline; oral-fluid testing cannot be implemented until HHS certifies ≥2 labs
EBCI tribal employer on tribal land (CCB licensees, Qualla Enterprises) Not confirmed. Tribal sovereign employment is not automatically subject to NC CSERA. No EBCI employee drug-testing rule was located [VERIFY — tribal employment policy, if published]
Independent contractors / volunteers Depends on "employee/applicant" status — CSERA's "examinee" is an employee or applicant; contractor testing is a contract question [VERIFY — case law, if any]

11.8.C No NC cannabis-specific employment protection

  • No NC statute requires an employer to accommodate marijuana use, hemp-derived THC use, or a positive test, and no NC statute prohibits adverse action for lawful off-duty hemp use. The only employment-related cannabis provision located in Run 1/Run 2 is the absence of one. HB 607 summaries state the bill would clarify employers need not accommodate hemp-derived product use — that clarification is in a pending bill, not law, and its absence from current law does not create an accommodation duty.
  • Federal protections (e.g. DOT, federal-contractor drug-free workplace) are separate and are not NC rules.

11.9 Research and university cannabis/hemp work

11.9.A Hemp research — the land-grant programme

  • Historical authority: Former G.S. 106-568.55 (Article 50E, pilot programme) authorised NC State University and NC A&T State University as licensed growers for industrial-hemp research on commercial uses. That pilot authority ended with the pilot (31 Dec 2021) and Article 50E's repeal (30 June 2022). Current hemp research is under USDA licences, not a state pilot licence.
  • NC A&T (current, officially reported): N.C. A&T Industrial Hemp Team page lists faculty including Guochen Yang (horticulture, co-PI on the Golden LEAF-funded "Training Farmers and Extension Agents through the Pilot Industrial Hemp Program at N.C. A&T") and research specialist John Ivey (production). Recent CAES Research reporting (2025/2026) describes a shift from CBD-dominated research to fibre and seed: Arnab Bhowmik (soil sciences, Soil Sustainability Lab) leading fibre/seed work; Beatrice Dingha (entomology) on hemp intercropping with cowpeas/okra/squash/watermelon under a $230,000 Southern SARE grant, including pollinator/soil-nitrogen work; Obed Quaicoe (agribusiness economics) on financial risk. Sources: https://www.ncat.edu/caes/agricultural-research/industrial-hemp-program/hemp-team.php ; https://caesresearch.news/hemp-take-2-research-program-shifts-to-fiber-varieties-as-demand-grows/ ; https://caesresearch.news/hemp-research-helping-farmers-navigate-a-new-field/ — all Accessed 2026-10-24. Publication lists / peer-reviewed outputs for these projects were not inventoried in this Part [VERIFY — publication list, if required for Run 2 completion].
  • NC State Extension: NC State Extension's "Industrial hemp is North Carolina's newest crop!" (2017, Debbie Roos, Growing Small Farms) and ongoing Extension county-meeting / field-day work are the historical record; no current NC State hemp research programme page with a 2025/2026 project list was opened in this Part [VERIFY — current NC State hemp project list].
  • USDA research licence rule (§990.21(d)): Producers producing hemp for research must obtain a USDA licence; hemp for research not entering commerce is exempt from §§990.24/990.26 sampling if the producer adopts a USDA-approved alternative sampling method meeting the 95% confidence standard; licensees must still dispose of non-compliant plants under §990.27 and report disposal under §990.71; only DEA-registered research institutions may keep hemp testing over 0.3% until the end of the study (§990.21(d)(3)).

11.9.B Marijuana research — UNC system and other institutions

  • No NC state marijuana research licence type exists. Marijuana research in NC requires federal DEA Schedule I researcher registration (21 CFR Part 1301) and, where applicable, FDA / NIDA / institutional approvals. No UNC-system marijuana research programme, licence, or publication inventory was located in this Part [VERIFY / UNAVAILABLE — no UNC cannabis research programme page found; absence is not proof none exists, only that none was located].
  • Hemp vs marijuana research distinction: Hemp research (≤0.3% delta-9, USDA-licensed) does not need DEA Schedule I registration unless the research will hold over-threshold material (see §990.21(d)(3) above). Marijuana research does, even if the institution also holds a USDA hemp licence.
  • EBCI research: The CCB's regulation power includes "study" of cannabis (Ord. No. 539), but no EBCI research licence type, research programme, or publication was located [VERIFY].

PART 12 — CONSUMER AND SOCIAL RULES

12.1 Paraphernalia and smoke/vape shop rules

12.1.A The statutes — Article 5B, Chapter 90

G.S. 90-113.21 — definition. "Drug paraphernalia" includes, but is not limited to: (1) planting/propagating/cultivating/growing/harvesting kits; (2) manufacturing/compounding/converting/producing/processing/preparing kits; (3) isomerisation devices; (4) testing equipment for identifying/analysing strength, effectiveness or purity; (5) scales/balances; (6) diluents/adulterants (quinine, hydrochloride, mannitol, mannite, dextrose, lactose); (7) separation gins/sifters for marijuana; (8) blenders/bowls/containers/spoons/mixing devices; (9) capsules/balloons/envelopes/containers for packaging small quantities; (10) containers/objects for storing/concealing; (11) hypodermic syringes/needles/objects for parenteral injection; (12) objects for ingesting/inhaling/introducing marijuana, cocaine, hashish or hashish oil into the body (including pipes, bongs, water pipes, roach clips, etc. as listed in §90-113.21). Courts consider factors in §90-113.21(b) including owner statements, proximity to controlled substances, and residue.

G.S. 90-113.22A — possession of marijuana drug paraphernalia (verbatim operative text):

"(a) It is unlawful for any person to knowingly use, or to possess with intent to use, drug paraphernalia to plant, propagate, cultivate, grow, harvest, manufacture, compound, convert, produce, process, prepare, test, analyze, package, repackage, store, contain, or conceal marijuana or to inject, ingest, inhale, or otherwise introduce marijuana into the body. (b) A violation of this section is a Class 3 misdemeanor. A violation of this section shall be a lesser included offense of G.S. 90-113.22. (c) Notwithstanding the provisions of subsection (a) of this section, it is not unlawful for (i) a person who introduces a controlled substance into his or her body, or intends to introduce a controlled substance into his or her body, to knowingly use, or to possess with intent to use, testing equipment for identifying or analyzing the strength, effectiveness, or purity of that controlled substance or (ii) a governmental or nongovernmental organization that promotes scientifically proven ways of mitigating health risks associated with drug use and other high-risk behaviors to possess such testing equipment or distribute such testing equipment to a person who intends to introduce a controlled substance into his or her body." (2014-119, s. 3(b); 2019-159, s. 2.2.)

Source: G.S. 90-113.22A (ncleg PDF). Accessed 2026-10-24. http://library.ncleg.gov/EnactedLegislation/Statutes/PDF/BySection/Chapter_90/GS_90-113.22A.pdf

Companion offences (for the table below):

  • G.S. 90-113.22(a): knowing use/possession with intent to use paraphernalia for a controlled substance other than marijuana = Class 1 misdemeanour. §90-113.22(c) needle/sharp-object alert-before-search exemption (no charge/prosecution for the needle/sharp object or residual amounts if the person alerts the officer before search).
  • G.S. 90-113.23(a): manufacture/sale/delivery of drug paraphernalia = Class 1 misdemeanour (secondary summary; pinpoint [VERIFY — §90-113.23 full text]). G.S. 90-113.23(c): delivery to a minor at least 3 years younger = Class I felony (secondary summary; pinpoint [VERIFY]).
Conduct Statute Class
Possess/use marijuana paraphernalia with intent 90-113.22A Class 3 misdemeanour
Possess/use non-marijuana paraphernalia with intent 90-113.22 Class 1 misdemeanour
Manufacture / sell / deliver paraphernalia (general) 90-113.23(a) Class 1 misdemeanour [VERIFY pinpoint]
Deliver paraphernalia to minor ≥3 years younger 90-113.23(c) Class I felony [VERIFY pinpoint]
Possess/use drug-testing equipment (fentanyl test strips, etc.) for personal use / harm-reduction distribution 90-113.22A(c) exception Not unlawful (within the exception)

Hemp-paraphernalia trap: The statute turns on intent to use for marijuana, not on the object's label. A pipe sold as a "tobacco pipe" or "hemp pipe" can still be marijuana paraphernalia if the State proves knowing intent to use it for marijuana. Conversely, an object used solely for lawful hemp (≤0.3% delta-9) is not, by that use alone, marijuana paraphernalia — but the practical enforcement problem is that hemp flower and marijuana are visually/olfactorily indistinguishable without lab testing, and a pipe with marijuana residue is evidence of marijuana intent. Secondary practitioner commentary stresses exactly this: do not assume a "legal hemp" purchase guarantees the product tests ≤0.3% delta-9.

Smoke / vape shops — no hemp-specific shop licence:

  • NC has NO smoke-shop licence, NO hemp-retail licence, and NO vape-shop cannabis licence. A smoke/vape shop is a general retail business: entity registration, sales-tax registration, local zoning / business registration (where a locality requires general registration — not a hemp fee), and, if it sells tobacco/vapour products, compliance with NC tobacco/vapour law (G.S. Chapter 14, Article 52A; G.S. 14-313 definitions; age 21 for tobacco/vapour under federal Tobacco 21 / NC law as applied — tobacco age is not a hemp age rule).
  • Local smoke-shop rules are cross-referenced to Run 1 Part 3: no statewide smoke-shop zoning list was located in Run 1, and no new statewide list was located in Run 2. Individual localities may impose zoning / distance / signage conditions on tobacco/vape retailers under Ch. 160D; any such condition is a local tobacco/retail rule, not a cannabis rule, unless the ordinance expressly names hemp/cannabis. Wake County's hemp-specific zoning (1,000-ft buffers, 2023/2024) is the only adopted hemp-specific zoning among the big eight found in Run 1.
  • ENDS / PACT Act note: NCDOR's Jenkins/PACT Act notice treats zero-nicotine e-liquids and CBD/THC/hemp vape pens as ENDS products for PACT reporting purposes (NCDOR notice, 19 March 2021). That is a tobacco-tax reporting classification, not a hemp licence. Source: https://www.ncdor.gov/documents/files/notice-new-jenkinspact-act-reporting-requirements-products-delivered-through-aerosolized-solution/open — Accessed 2026-10-24.

12.2 Minimum age for hemp-derived THC products — currently none statewide (verified)

  • Current law: NO statewide minimum age to buy, possess, or use hemp or hemp-derived THC products (delta-8, delta-10, THCA flower, hemp delta-9 edibles/drinks, CBD) in North Carolina. No age-verification mandate, no ID-check rule, and no underage-possession offence for hemp products exists in current NC law. This was confirmed by multiple 2025/2026 secondary sources and by the absence of any age provision in the current hemp definition (S.L. 2022-32) — and it is the position stated in the April 2026 NC Advisory Council on Cannabis Interim Report as summarised in JD Supra (2026): "No minimum age requirement. There is no state law requiring retailers to verify a buyer's age before selling intoxicating hemp products."
  • Contrast — age rules that DO exist and are often confused with a hemp age rule:
  • Tobacco / vapour products: 21 (federal Tobacco 21, applied in NC).
  • Alcohol: 21 (G.S. Chapter 18B).
  • EBCI cannabis (medical and adult-use): 21+ (medical card: NC resident 21+ with a qualifying condition; adult-use: 21+ with government photo ID). EBCI hemp retail on tribal land is under CCB licensing; the 21+ rule for cannabis does not automatically create a statewide hemp age rule.
  • Medical hemp extract (epilepsy, G.S. 90-94.1): caregiver / patient framework, not a retail age rule.
  • PROPOSED 21+ only (NOT law):
  • HB 328 (earlier Chapter 18D versions): §18D-101(a)(1) — "Sell a hemp-derived consumable product to a person who is under 21 years of age," with ID demand if the retailer has reasonable grounds to believe the purchaser is under 30, and internet age verification + adult-signature delivery. Conference-report summaries (2026) describe the conference text as prohibiting sales to under-21s, making under-21 sale/possession a Class 2 misdemeanour in the conference text (written to start 15 July 2026 in one summary; effective-date conflict [VERIFY — conference text effective dates]), with escalating civil penalties ($2,500 / $7,500 / $15,000 / $25,000 for repeat underage-sale violations within 3 years; $10,000 / $25,000 / $50,000 for prohibited-product sales). None of this is law until/unless the House adopts the conference report.
  • SB 328 / HB 607 (pending licensing versions): also 21+ with ALE licensing, fees, and testing — pending, not law.
  • School provisions in HB 328 / HB 468 / HB 802 drafts would prohibit hemp-derived consumable use on school property and confiscate products from students under 21 — also in pending bills, not current law (see §12.10).
  • Federal age: No federal minimum age for hemp products exists in the 2018 Farm Bill definition; the PL 119-37/119-103 redefinition does not, in the fixed baseline, impose a purchase age. Do not cite a federal 21+ hemp rule.

12.3 Impaired driving — law and testing

12.3.A The statute (verbatim)

G.S. 20-138.1 Impaired driving:

"(a) Offense. — A person commits the offense of impaired driving if he drives any vehicle upon any highway, any street, or any public vehicular area within this State: (1) While under the influence of an impairing substance; or (2) After having consumed sufficient alcohol that he has, at any relevant time after the driving, an alcohol concentration of 0.08 or more. The results of a chemical analysis shall be deemed sufficient evidence to prove a person's alcohol concentration; or (3) With any amount of a Schedule I controlled substance, as listed in G.S. 90-89, or its metabolites in his blood or urine. (b) Defense Precluded. — The fact that a person charged with violating this section is or has been legally entitled to use alcohol or a drug is not a defense to a charge under this section. … (d) Sentencing Hearing and Punishment. — Impaired driving as defined in this section is a misdemeanor. Upon conviction … punishment in accordance with G.S. 20-179. (e) Exception. — … the word 'vehicle' does not include a horse." (1983, c. 435, s. 24; 1989, c. 711, s. 2; 1993, c. 285, s. 1; 2006-253, s. 9.)

Source: G.S. 20-138.1 (ncleg HTML). Accessed 2026-10-24. http://www.ncleg.net/EnactedLegislation/Statutes/HTML/BySection/Chapter_20/GS_20-138.1.html

"Impairing substance" — G.S. 20-4.01(14a) [VERIFY pinpoint — definition not re-quoted from clean text in this Part]: Secondary summaries state it includes alcohol, a controlled substance, or any drug capable of impairing physical/mental faculties. Marijuana, as a Schedule VI controlled substance, is within that definition for prong (a)(1).

The Schedule I qualification — critical to "no per-se THC limit":

  • Prong (a)(3) is a per-se / "any amount" prong, but only for Schedule I substances listed in G.S. 90-89. Marijuana is Schedule VI in NC (G.S. 90-94), not Schedule I. Therefore (a)(3) does NOT, by its terms, create a per-se marijuana DWI in NC. A marijuana DWI must be proved under (a)(1): the State must prove the driver was "under the influence of an impairing substance" — i.e. appreciably impaired — not merely that THC/metabolites were present.
  • This is why the locked baseline ("impairment-based, no per-se THC limit") is correct for marijuana, even though (a)(3) exists. Secondary practitioner commentary (National Drug Screening, NC page) states the same point explicitly: "The 'any amount' rule in the impaired driving statute applies to Schedule I substances, and marijuana is Schedule VI in North Carolina."
  • Schedule III nuance [VERIFY]: The fixed federal baseline moves state-licensed medical marijuana to Schedule III federally (28 Apr 2026). That federal move does NOT amend G.S. 90-89 (Schedule I list) or G.S. 90-94 (Schedule VI list) by itself. Whether NC would need to conform its schedules, and whether a future NC schedule change could affect (a)(3)'s application to any cannabis product, is unconfirmed. Do not state that (a)(3) now applies, or does not apply, to Schedule III medical marijuana in NC without a NC schedule amendment or court decision.
  • Legal entitlement is no defence: §20-138.1(b) — lawful EBCI purchase, lawful hemp purchase, a medical card, or a prescription is not a defence to impaired driving. See also §12.5 (tribal purchase gives no driving protection).

12.3.B How impairment is proved — DRE, SFST, chemical analysis

  • Standardised Field Sobriety Tests (SFSTs): Horizontal gaze nystagmus, walk-and-turn, one-leg stand — designed for alcohol, used in drug cases as observations of psychomotor impairment, not as a THC measurement. Poor SFST performance plus driving behaviour plus admissions/observations can support (a)(1); SFSTs alone do not prove THC concentration.
  • Drug Recognition Expert (DRE) — 12-step protocol: A DRE is a law-enforcement officer certified under IACP/NHTSA training who administers a 12-step evaluation (interview, pulse, pupil size, muscle tone, temperature, etc.) to categorise the impairing substance. NC Rule of Evidence 702(a1) permits DRE expert testimony on impairment/category "notwithstanding any other provision of law" when the witness holds current DRE certification — but State v. Moore (NC Court of Appeals, 2025), as summarised by UNC School of Government / Forensic Resources, held Rule 702(a)(3) still applies: the proponent must show the DRE reliably applied principles/methods to the facts (e.g. all protocol steps reliably administered, DRE personally examined the defendant). Source: Forensic Resources, Drug Recognition Experts. Accessed 2026-10-24. https://forensicresources.org/forensic-disciplines/drug-recognition-experts/
  • Chemical analysis (G.S. 20-139.1): Blood (and, in limited circumstances, urine) analysis for drugs/metabolites; breath analysis does not detect THC. Blood THC/metabolites can remain detectable days/weeks after impairment ends, so presence ≠ impairment under (a)(1) — the State must still prove appreciable impairment through driving, observations, SFST/DRE, and expert testimony linking the substance to impairment. No NC statute sets a blood-THC nanogram threshold for impairment [VERIFY — no per-se blood-THC number in G.S. 20-139.1 or 20-138.1].
  • Implied consent / refusal: G.S. 20-16.2 implied-consent and refusal consequences apply to chemical analysis generally; the drug-specific refusal / licence consequences were not re-verified in this Part [VERIFY — §20-16.2 drug-test refusal pinpoint].
  • Sentencing: Misdemeanour under G.S. 20-179 levels (A1, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5) with grossly aggravating / aggravating / mitigating factors; impaired-driving convictions are NOT eligible for expunction under G.S. 15A-145.5(a1) (see §12.4).
  • EBCI driving: Consuming marijuana/hemp/hemp products while driving on a highway/public vehicular area (Cherokee Code Ch. 20 definition) is prohibited conduct on tribal land (Ord. No. 63 list). Tribal impaired-driving law, if any, beyond that prohibition was not opened [VERIFY — Cherokee Code impaired-driving provision, if any].

12.4 Expungement and record clearing for marijuana offences

12.4.A The 5-year vs 3-year conflict — RESOLVED in favour of 3 years (current text)

Run 1 carried a 5-year vs 3-year wait conflict for a single nonviolent misdemeanour under G.S. 15A-145.5. The current official text resolves it: 3 years.

G.S. 15A-145.5(c)(1)a (verbatim, ncleg PDF):

"For expunction of one nonviolent misdemeanor, three years after the date of the conviction or when any active sentence, period of probation, or post-release supervision has been served, whichever occurs later."

G.S. 15A-145.5(c)(1)b: more than one nonviolent misdemeanour — 7 years after the last conviction (other than an unlisted traffic offence) or after sentence/probation/post-release supervision served, whichever is later.

G.S. 15A-145.5(c)(2): up to three nonviolent felonies — (a) one nonviolent felony (other than G.S. 14-54(a) breaking/entering): 10 years after conviction or after sentence/probation/post-release supervision served, whichever is later; (a1) one G.S. 14-54(a) felony: 15 years; (b) two or three nonviolent felonies: 20 years after the most recent listed conviction or after supervision served, whichever is later.

Source: G.S. 15A-145.5 (ncleg PDF). Accessed 2026-10-24. https://www3.ncleg.gov/EnactedLegislation/Statutes/PDF/BySection/Chapter_15A/GS_15A-145.5.pdf ; UNC School of Government, Older Nonviolent Misdemeanor and Felony Convictions (same waits). https://www.sog.unc.edu/resources/microsites/relief-criminal-conviction/older-nonviolent-misdemeanor-and-felony-convictions

Why the 5-year figure appears in some secondary sources: Older versions of §15A-145.5 and some practitioner pages still state 5 years for a single misdemeanour (and 5 years for multiple misdemeanours in the same-session / pre-2021 summaries). Those pages are out of date against the current text. The 5-year figure should NOT be used for petitions filed now. Where a secondary page in this research stated 5 years (e.g. one expungement-lawyer page), it is superseded by the ncleg text above.

Other §15A-145.5 limits (current text, summarised):

  • Same-session multiple convictions are treated as one conviction (§15A-145.5(b)).
  • A person previously granted expunction under §15A-145.5 is not eligible for relief for an offence committed after the previous order (with (c4)/(c5) exceptions for pre-1 Dec 2021 grants — pinpoint [VERIFY — (c4)/(c5) text]).
  • Impaired driving is NOT eligible: §15A-145.5(a1) — "An offense involving impaired driving as defined in G.S. 20-4.01(24a) is not eligible for expunction." Since 1 Dec 2021, an impaired-driving conviction does not bar expunction of other convictions, but it cannot itself be expunged under §15A-145.5.
  • Excluded "nonviolent" offences include, among others, certain Chapter 90 felonies involving methamphetamine, heroin, or possession with intent to sell/deliver cocaine (§15A-145.5(a)(5) excerpt) — marijuana possession felonies under §90-95(a)(3) are not in that exclusion as excerpted, but the full (a) exclusion list must be checked for any specific felony before advising eligibility [VERIFY — full §15A-145.5(a) list].
  • Good moral character, no pending charges, no unpaid restitution, and no new convictions (other than minor traffic) during the wait are also required in secondary summaries — pinpoints [VERIFY — §15A-145.5(c) conditions beyond timing].
