Two or three nonviolent felonies — 20 years G.S. 15A-145.5(c)(2)b
Impaired driving (any) G.S. 20-138.1 conviction Not eligible under §15A-145.5 G.S. 15A-145.5(a1)
Trafficking (>10 lbs) Class H/G/F/D felony Not eligible as "nonviolent" in practice — trafficking is a drug-trafficking felony with mandatory minimums; eligibility would require the full (a) exclusion analysis and is not assumed here [VERIFY — trafficking eligibility] G.S. 90-95(h); 15A-145.5(a)

Note on §90-113.22A in §15A-145.2(c): UNC SOG Table 16 lists paraphernalia under §90-113.22 "and possibly §90-113.22A" — the "possibly" reflects that §15A-145.2(c)'s text predates / does not expressly name §90-113.22A in all versions. Do not assume a §90-113.22A conviction is automatically eligible under §15A-145.2(c) without checking the current §15A-145.2(c) text [VERIFY — §90-113.22A inclusion].

12.4.C Conditional discharge — G.S. 90-96 (verbatim operative text)

"(a) Whenever any person who has not previously been convicted of (i) any felony offense under any state or federal laws; (ii) any offense under this Article; or (iii) an offense under any statute of the United States or any state relating to those substances included in Article 5 or 5A of Chapter 90 or to that paraphernalia included in Article 5B of Chapter 90 of the General Statutes pleads guilty to or is found guilty of (i) a misdemeanor under this Article by possessing a controlled substance included within Schedules I through VI of this Article or by possessing drug paraphernalia as prohibited by G.S. 90-113.22 or G.S. 90-113.22A or (ii) a felony under G.S. 90-95(a)(3), the court shall, without entering a judgment of guilt and with the consent of the person, defer further proceedings and place the person on probation upon such reasonable terms and conditions as it may require, unless the court determines with a written finding, and with the agreement of the District Attorney, that the offender is inappropriate for a conditional discharge for factors related to the offense."

Probation may be imposed even for an offence whose prescribed punishment is only a fine, notwithstanding G.S. 15A-1342(c). Terms may include a DHHS-approved drug-education programme or Treatment for Effective Community Supervision (TECS). On violation, the court may enter adjudication of guilt; on fulfilment, the court shall discharge and dismiss. Source: G.S. 90-96 (ncleg PDF). Accessed 2026-10-24. https://www.ncleg.gov/EnactedLegislation/Statutes/PDF/BySection/Chapter_90/GS_90-96.pdf

Interaction with expungement: A §90-96 discharge/dismissal is not a conviction. Expunction of the dismissed charge follows the dismissal-expunction route (and, for under-21 first offenders, §15A-145.2(a)), not the §15A-145.5 conviction waits. A person who has already used §90-96 once cannot use it again for a later first-offence claim (the "has not previously been convicted" + one-use structure).

12.4.D No automatic / mass expungement in NC

  • NC has NO automatic expungement for marijuana convictions, NO Second Chance Act-style automatic clearing for marijuana (contrast DC, noted in Run 1 for DC only), and NO pending NC mass-expungement law located in this Part. Every route above is petition-based, county-by-county, with AOC forms (AOC-CR-266 for §15A-145.2(c); AOC-CR-268 for dismissed paraphernalia, etc.).
  • Tribal convictions / tribal-court records on the Boundary are not NC court records and are not expunged under Chapter 15A. Tribal record-clearing, if any, is under tribal law [VERIFY — Cherokee Code record-clearing, if any].

12.5 Consumption on tribal land vs elsewhere; cannabis tourism to Cherokee; what visitors can take home

12.5.A On tribal land — what is lawful

Under Ord. No. 63 (adult-use) and Ord. No. 539 (medical), as summarised in the prohibited-conduct list (One Feather, 25 Aug 2026, quoting the ordinance; and 7 June 2024 Council report), it is unlawful on tribal land to:

  • Possess marijuana under age 21; provide/give/transfer marijuana to a person under 21;
  • Cultivate without a valid licence / valid agent card / valid medical patient card;
  • Operate a marijuana facility without a CCB licence; sell marijuana without a CCB licence; sell hemp/hemp products without a CCB licence;
  • Possess marijuana where possession is prohibited by conspicuous posting by the owner/possessor;
  • Consume marijuana in public;
  • Possess or consume marijuana within 100 feet of a school, child daycare, church, hospital, tribal government building, public park, playground, community club building, public swimming pool, or community facility (Ch. 17 definition);
  • Transport marijuana/hemp/hemp products in the passenger area of a motor vehicle other than in the manufacturer's unopened original container;
  • Consume marijuana/hemp/hemp products while driving on a highway/public vehicular area (Cherokee Code Ch. 20 definition).

Inverse — lawful consumption on tribal land: Private, non-public consumption by an adult 21+ (or a medical patient under the medical rules) in a place not within 100 feet of the listed facilities and not posted against possession, is the lawful core. A private residence on tribal land, a private hotel room on tribal land that does not prohibit possession, and other private non-public places are the practical lawful-consumption locations. No public consumption lounge / on-premises consumption endorsement for EBCI facilities was located in this Part — do not assume Great Smoky Cannabis Co. has a consumption lounge. The only lawful on-premises consumption model in the wider dossier (DC retailer safe-use endorsement) has no EBCI analogue located [VERIFY — EBCI consumption-lounge / on-site use rule, if any].

12.5.B Elsewhere in NC — public consumption prohibition in practice

  • There is NO NC statute that says, in one sentence, "public consumption of marijuana is prohibited," because marijuana possession/use is prohibited everywhere off tribal land (G.S. 90-95). Public consumption off the Boundary is therefore not a separate public-consumption offence — it is possession (and, if smoked in a smoke-free place, a smoke-free-law violation) plus the underlying possession offence.
  • Smoke-free law overlay: NC's smoke-free restaurants/bars/lodging law (G.S. 130A-497 et seq.) prohibits smoking (tobacco smoking as defined) indoors in restaurants, bars and lodging establishments, with signage requirements (24 sq. in., Division complaint line, "G.S. 130A-497", smokefree.nc.gov). Local governments may additionally prohibit smoking/tobacco in local-government buildings, grounds, public places, parks, etc. (NCDHHS Tobacco Prevention and Control Branch, current policy, updated 20 Feb 2025). Hemp smoking is not tobacco smoking, but a hemp/marijuana smoker in a smoke-free restaurant/bar/lodging establishment will in practice be removed for violating the establishment's smoke-free policy and, for marijuana, for possession.
  • Proposed public-consumption ban (NOT law): The NC Advisory Council on Cannabis (Governor Stein's council, final recommendations due 31 Dec 2026) discussed on 29 Sept 2026 a draft recommendation to prohibit consumption of all THC products in any public place (restaurants/bars, beaches, parks, event venues, public buildings), with a possible narrow exception for licensed restaurants/bars serving THC beverages for on-premises consumption only. The Council has no rulemaking authority; none of this is law. Sources (secondary, Sept–Oct 2026): https://cannabusiness.law/north-carolina-cannabis-council-considers-broad-ban-on-public-thc-consumption/ ; https://www.citizen-times.com/story/news/2026/10/02/nc-cannabis-council-considers-public-consumption-policy-for-thc-products/92059355007/ — Accessed 2026-10-24.

12.5.C Tourism to Cherokee — the boundary trap (the single most important consumer warning in this Part)

  • What visitors can do lawfully: An adult 21+ with valid government photo ID may enter Great Smoky Cannabis Co. (91 Bingo Loop Road, Cherokee — address as published by Visit Smokies) on the Qualla Boundary, purchase cannabis under tribal law (subject to the purchase-limit conflict noted in the baselines), and consume it privately on tribal land in compliance with the 100-foot / public-consumption / posted-prohibition rules.
  • What visitors CANNOT lawfully do: Take any cannabis product off the Qualla Boundary. The moment a visitor leaves tribal land, NC law applies in full: possession of ≤0.5 oz = Class 3 misdemeanour (fine up to $200, suspended sentence, no active jail for a first offence as a matter of §90-95(d)(4) — see §12.4 / R1-02); >0.5–1.5 oz = Class 1 misdemeanour; >1.5 oz = Class I felony; >10 lbs = trafficking. The full 1.5 oz tribal purchase, if carried off the Boundary, is a Class 1 misdemeanour, not a felony.
  • Why the boundary is treacherous: The Qualla Boundary is not a single contiguous block; it is scattered tracts across several counties (Swain, Jackson, Cherokee, Graham, Haywood, Macon as generally described). A visitor can leave tribal jurisdiction without realising it, especially on back roads. State/local law enforcement are aware of the dispensary and that customers drive away with cannabis. Great Smoky Cannabis Co.'s own warning, as quoted in JD Supra (2026), is in bold red font: "[U]pon leaving the Qualla boundary, individuals are subject to state laws, including those regarding cannabis."
  • Federal lands nearby: Great Smoky Mountains National Park, Blue Ridge Parkway, and national forests are federal lands where federal marijuana law applies regardless of tribal/state status. Consuming or possessing cannabis there is a federal offence risk, separate from NC law. Visitors should check current policies with Great Smoky Cannabis Co. before arriving, as hours/details change.
  • Transport risk in a vehicle: Off the Boundary, transport is possession/delivery/transport under G.S. 90-95, not a "transport in original container" safe harbour — NC has no such safe harbour for marijuana. On the Boundary, transport in the passenger area other than in the manufacturer's unopened original container is itself prohibited conduct.
  • Employment / drug-test risk: A lawful tribal purchase gives no protection against a NC employer's drug test or adverse action (see §11.8.C).

12.5.D Neighbouring-state positions (brief — for the "take home" question)

A visitor who drives home across a state line faces that state's law plus federal interstate-transport law. This is a summary only; each state's full position is outside Parts 11–12.

Neighbour Position as of Accessed 2026-10-24 (summary)
Tennessee (west) No medical/adult-use programme; marijuana illegal; hemp ≤0.3% delta-9 only under TN hemp law. Taking Cherokee marijuana into TN = TN marijuana possession.
Georgia (south) No adult-use; narrow low-THC oil (≤5% THC) medical programme only. Taking Cherokee marijuana into GA = GA marijuana possession.
South Carolina (south) No medical/adult-use programme; only Julian's Law low-THC/CBD exception (≤0.9% THC, >15% CBD, severe epilepsy); hemp ≤0.3% delta-9 under SC Hemp Farming Act. Taking Cherokee marijuana into SC = SC marijuana possession.
Virginia (north) Adult-use possession legal (1 oz, §4.1-1100 as in Run 1 VA baseline) but retail sales not operating until 1 July 2027 under HB 30 (2026); medical retail operating. Taking Cherokee marijuana into VA: possession within VA's adult-use limit is not a VA possession offence, but interstate transport into VA remains a federal offence and the product was not obtained through VA's lawful channels — do not treat VA possession legality as a transport safe harbour.
Federal (interstate) Interstate transport of marijuana is a federal CSA offence regardless of the destination state's possession law.

12.6 Landlord and tenant rules

Headline: NO cannabis-specific landlord/tenant statute in NC.

  • No NC statute addresses marijuana/hemp use, smoking, or cultivation in rented housing as a standalone subject. There is no NC "medical marijuana tenant protection," no "landlord may not prohibit hemp use" rule, and no "tenant may cultivate" rule.
  • What governs instead — general NC landlord/tenant law (G.S. Chapter 42):
  • Lease as contract: A landlord may prohibit smoking (including marijuana/hemp smoking, vaping, and cultivation) by lease term, house rule, or smoke-free policy, and may make violation a lease default. Standard NC lease forms treat violation of criminal laws, possession/manufacture/delivery of a controlled substance/marijuana/paraphernalia on the premises, and illegal drugs/paraphernalia found on the premises as defaults. A tenant who grows marijuana off tribal land is also committing a criminal offence on the premises, which is a stronger default ground than smoking alone.
  • Eviction procedure: NC eviction is court-only (G.S. Chapter 42, Article 7; summary ejectment in small claims court). For illegal-activity / lease-violation evictions, proper notice (including, where applicable, a notice to quit / demand for compliance as the lease and statute require) and a court hearing are required; self-help lockout is unlawful. The landlord must prove the violation at the hearing. A tenant may contest, and either party may appeal within 10 days of judgment (secondary summary of NC eviction procedure — pinpoint [VERIFY — G.S. 42-26 / 42-28 notice periods for drug-activity evictions]).
  • Retaliatory eviction: NC law does not approve retaliatory eviction for a tenant's good-faith complaints / repair requests (G.S. 42-37.1). A drug-activity eviction that is in fact retaliation for a repair complaint is contestable on that ground.
  • Public / assisted housing: Federally assisted housing (public housing, Section 8 / Housing Choice Voucher) is subject to federal drug law and HUD rules: marijuana use, even if lawful under tribal or another state's law, can be grounds for denial/termination in federally assisted housing. Secondary summaries state this applies "even if the marijuana was purchased legally on tribal lands." That federal overlay is not a NC landlord/tenant rule, but it is the practical rule for assisted tenants. [VERIFY — current HUD / PHA policy for the specific housing authority].
  • Hemp use in rented housing: Lawful hemp (CBD, hemp edibles, hemp flower ≤0.3% delta-9) is not a controlled substance, so hemp use alone is not a criminal-law lease default. A landlord may still prohibit smoking (of any substance) and cultivation (as a property-damage / lease-use restriction) by lease term. A tenant's hemp use that creates smoke, odour, or damage can be a lease violation on those grounds, not on controlled-substance grounds. No NC case law on hemp-specific tenancy disputes was located [VERIFY].
  • Cultivation in rented housing: Growing marijuana off tribal land is a criminal offence and, in a rental, will typically also breach lease clauses on illegal activity, alterations, moisture/mould, electrical load, and insurance. Growing hemp under a USDA licence in rented agricultural land is a lease-use question (does the agricultural lease permit hemp? does it require FSA / licence compliance?) — no NC agricultural-lease hemp rule was located.
  • EBCI housing: Housing on tribal land under tribal housing authority / tribal lease rules is under tribal law, not Chapter 42. No EBCI housing cannabis rule was located [VERIFY].
  • Smoking definition in leases: Many NC leases / property-manager rules define "smoking" to include inhaling/exhaling/burning tobacco or medical marijuana and carrying lighted pipes/hookahs/e-cigarettes/vaporisers. That is a private lease definition, not a statute, but it is enforceable as a lease term if the tenant agreed to it.

12.7 (Reserved — no separate Part 12 item; see §12.8 for guns, §12.9 for pets, §12.10 for schools)

12.8 Gun ownership conflicts

Federal — fixed baseline only (do not expand):

  • 18 U.S.C. 922(g)(3) prohibits possession of firearms/ammunition by a person who is an "unlawful user of or addicted to any controlled substance." ATF's interim final rule effective 22 January 2026 rewrote 27 CFR 478.11 to require regular use continuing into the present (per the fixed federal baseline in the wider dossier). US v. Hemani, No. 24-1234 (18 June 2026, Gorsuch), held a status-only §922(g)(3) prosecution of a regular marijuana user for in-home possession, with no danger showing, inconsistent with the Second Amendment as applied (narrow holding). Schedule III alone does not remove medical users from §922(g)(3) text, and no ATF safe harbour for medical users was located in the federal baseline.
  • Schedule III nuance for NC [VERIFY]: Whether a person whose only cannabis use is (a) lawful hemp (not a controlled substance), (b) EBCI medical cannabis, or (c) federally Schedule III state-licensed medical cannabis is an "unlawful user" under §922(g)(3) is unconfirmed in any ATF/DEA source opened for this Part. Hemp use alone is not use of a controlled substance. EBCI medical use is lawful under tribal law but remains unlawful under federal law for adult-use / non-Schedule III purposes, and the Schedule III question for tribal licences is the same [VERIFY] as §11.0. Do not advise any individual that tribal or medical use is gun-safe.

NC — pistol / purchase-permit law as it interacts:

  • Pistol purchase permits REPEALED: Senate Bill 41 (2023), enacted 29 March 2023 after veto override, repealed G.S. 14-402 through 14-405 and 14-407.1 (pistol purchase permit system). ATF Open Letter to NC FFLs (August 2023) confirms NC pistol purchase permits may no longer be used as an alternative to a NICS check. Source: ATF Firearms Open Letters (Aug 2023). https://www.atf.gov/rules-and-regulations/open-letters/firearms-open-letters?field_document_type_value%5Bopen_letter%5D=open_letter&og_group_ref_target_id%5B186%5D=186
  • Current NC purchase route: Handgun purchases from licensed dealers now use the federal NICS point-of-sale check (as long guns always have). NC concealed handgun permits (G.S. 14-415.10 et seq.) are unaffected by SB 41 and continue to serve as a NICS alternative where ATF recognises them. Private handgun transfers require no NC NICS check under current law, but criminal penalties still apply to knowingly transferring to a person who may not lawfully possess.
  • NC concealed handgun permit disqualifiers: A concealed handgun permit applicant must meet federal and state eligibility; unlawful controlled-substance use is a federal disqualifier that flows through NICS / the permit process. No NC statute creates a cannabis-specific concealed-carry disqualifier separate from the federal one, and no NC statute creates a cannabis-user concealed-carry exemption. The interaction is therefore federal-law-driven: a NC sheriff / NICS denial based on §922(g)(3) status is a federal determination, not a NC cannabis rule.
  • No NC gun rule invented: NC has no statute that says "marijuana users may/may not own guns in NC" beyond the general incorporation of federal disqualifiers in the concealed-permit and transfer framework. Any NC gun advice must start from §922(g)(3) + Hemani + ATF's current Form 4473 / rule, not from NC cannabis law.

12.9 Pet and veterinary products

Headline: NO FDA-approved cannabis drug for animals; NO NC veterinary cannabis rule located.

  • FDA position (federal, applies in NC): FDA has not approved any cannabis-derived product for therapeutic/nutritional use in animals. CBD products marketed with disease claims for animals (anxiety, pain, arthritis, inflammation, etc.) are unapproved new animal drugs under the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act and require a New Animal Drug Application (NADA). FDA issued warning letters in June 2025 (and earlier rounds) to manufacturers/retailers of CBD pet items, including companies operating in NC, for drug claims without approval, and for food-producing-animal use where FDA cannot set withdrawal periods because it lacks data on CBD dosage, interactions, side effects, and residue depletion. Sources (secondary summaries of FDA letters): JD Supra, FDA warning letters for food-producing animals; Cannabis Regulations AI, FDA veterinary CBD 2025 policy. Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • NCDA&CS position (2019, still the clearest NC statement located): NCDA&CS Assistant Commissioner for Consumer Protection (Joe Reardon, Feb 2019, as quoted by a NC veterinary practice): "Under federal Food and Drug Administration laws, CBD is considered a drug. North Carolina state laws mirror federal laws. This means that CBD cannot legally be added to any human food or animal feed that is for sale. The product can also not make health claims, including statements that the product may prevent, treat, or cure any disease." That 2019 statement predates the current hemp market and should be treated as the NCDA&CS food/feed position, not as a current NC pet-CBD retail ban — hemp pet supplements (non-food, non-drug-claim) occupy the same grey area as human CBD supplements. No updated NCDA&CS pet-CBD guidance was located [VERIFY — current NCDA&CS pet-CBD / animal-feed guidance].
  • NC Veterinary Medical Board: No NC Veterinary Medical Board guidance on CBD/THC discussion, recommendation, or prescription for animals was located in this Part [VERIFY / UNAVAILABLE]. In the absence of a NC board rule, veterinarians are governed by general veterinary practice law (G.S. Chapter 90, Article 11), FDA drug law, and professional-liability considerations. Secondary veterinary commentary notes many veterinarians will discuss safety/interactions but will not recommend or sell unapproved hemp/cannabis pet products, and that FDA-approved human drugs (e.g. Epidiolex) may be used extra-label in animals when medically appropriate, while unapproved pet CBD products may not be "prescribed" as approved drugs.
  • THC toxicity in pets: THC is toxic to dogs/cats (ataxia, lethargy, vomiting, urinary incontinence, in severe cases seizures/coma). Human cannabis edibles (especially chocolate, xylitol-containing products) compound the risk. This is veterinary-medical fact, not a NC rule, but it is the reason pet-CBD products marketed as "THC-free" or "broad-spectrum" must be verified by COA — and why a pet owner giving a human hemp-THC product to a pet risks both animal harm and a controlled-substance issue if the product is marijuana.
  • Food-producing animals: CBD/hemp in animal feed for food-producing animals is the highest-risk category (residue in meat/milk/eggs, no withdrawal period). FDA's position is that it cannot approve such use on current data. No NC feed rule authorising hemp/CBD in commercial animal feed was located.
  • Investigational development (Oct 2026, secondary): FDA Center for Veterinary Medicine determined (Oct 2026) that a CBD/THC oral solution for bone-cancer pain in dogs (CannEpil, Endovia/Lupvindol) qualifies as a "minor use" eligible for conditional approval — it has NOT been approved or conditionally approved. This is noted only to prevent the error of calling it an approved pet drug. Source: Marijuana Herald, 5 Oct 2026. https://themarijuanaherald.com/2026/10/fda-says-cbd-thc-drug-for-bone-cancer-pain-in-dogs-can-pursue-conditional-approval-pathway/

12.10 Hemp/CBD use in schools

Headline: NO current NC statute authorises hemp extract in schools; school drug policies and the tobacco/hemp-use prohibition framework govern.

12.10.A Current law — what exists now

  • G.S. 115C-407 (Article 29A) — tobacco use prohibition (current law): Governing bodies of public school units must adopt a written policy prohibiting tobacco use (including vapour products as defined in G.S. 14-313) at all times in school buildings, facilities, on school campuses, on school buses/transportation, and on other school property owned/operated by the unit, and at school-sponsored events as specified. The policy must include notice, signage, and enforcement elements. This is a tobacco prohibition in current law; hemp is not in the current §115C-407 text as enacted.
  • School drug policies / Codes of Student Conduct: Each public school unit's Code of Student Conduct prohibits possession/use of controlled substances (including marijuana) and paraphernalia on school property / at school events, with disciplinary consequences under G.S. 115C-390 et seq. (suspension, expulsion for serious drug offences, with due-process and alternative-education provisions). Hemp products that are not controlled substances are not automatically "drugs" under those codes, but a hemp product that tests as marijuana, or a hemp-THC product that impairs a student, will be treated as a drug violation in practice, and schools may prohibit any hemp-derived consumable by local policy (see pending bills below — local policies may already do so without a state mandate; no statewide inventory of local school hemp policies was located [VERIFY — local policy inventory]).
  • Hemp extract / CBD for a student (epilepsy) — no school-administration rule located: NC's epilepsy hemp-extract exception (G.S. 90-94.1, <0.9% THC, ≥5% CBD, intractable epilepsy, neurologist care) does not, in its text located, create a school-administration right, a school-nurse administration duty, or a student self-carry right. Whether a student may possess/administer hemp extract at school is therefore a matter for the individual school's medication policy (G.S. 115C-375.1 / 115C-375.2 framework for prescription / non-prescription medication administration, parental consent, health-care-provider authorisation) and the student's 504/IEP plan, if any. No NC school hemp-extract administration guidance was located [VERIFY — NCDHHS / DPI school-health hemp guidance, if any]. Do not state that schools must, or must not, allow hemp extract — the rule is "no specific rule located; general medication + drug policies govern."
  • Cherokee Central Schools: Schools on the Boundary under tribal / BIE governance are not governed by G.S. Chapter 115C in the same way; no Cherokee Central Schools cannabis/hemp policy was located [VERIFY].

12.10.B Proposed school hemp rules (NOT law — HB 328 / HB 468 / HB 802 drafts)

Pending-bill summaries describe provisions that WOULD, if enacted, extend §115C-407 to hemp:

  • HB 328 (as filed / committee versions): Amends §115C-407 to also prohibit use of hemp products (defined to include delta-8, delta-9, CBD and synthetic counterparts designed/manufactured/sold to be inhaled or otherwise consumed) by any person in school buildings/facilities/campuses/other school property; changes the implementing entity to governing bodies of public school units; conforming changes to §115C-562.5 for nonpublic schools accepting scholarship-grant students. Applies beginning 2025–26 school year if enacted.
  • HB 468 / HB 802 committee versions: Enact §115C-407.1 (policy prohibiting use of hemp-derived consumable products, as defined in proposed G.S. 18D-100, in school buildings/grounds/buses/school-sponsored events) and §115C-391.5 (Code of Student Conduct confiscation for students under 21 found in possession of nicotine/hemp/vapour products, with 24-hour parent notice, prevention materials, education programme for first offence, discipline for second, community service option for third+; DHHS website awareness information by 15 Sept 2025).
  • All of the above is pending-bill text, not current law. The school-hemp prohibition that a parent/student will actually face today is the local school unit's own policy, not §115C-407 as amended by a pending bill.

12.10.C Staff / adult use on school property

  • Staff possession/use of marijuana on school property is a controlled-substance offence + employment-discipline matter (see §11.8). Staff use of lawful hemp (CBD) off duty is not a school-law offence, but on-duty impairment, on-property smoking/vaping (tobacco/hemp vapour policy), and any on-property possession in violation of local policy are discipline matters. No NC staff hemp-use rule separate from general drug-free-workplace / CSERA / school-policy rules was located.

12.11 Cross-cutting consumer notes (Part 12, for completeness)

  • No NC cannabis consumer-complaint portal: NC has no cannabis ombudsman, no hemp-consumer complaint form, and no CCB-equivalent for off-Boundary hemp. Consumer complaints go to general channels: NC Attorney General Consumer Protection, NCDA&CS (food/feed/adulteration), FDA MedWatch (adverse events for drugs/foods), and local law enforcement (controlled-substance complaints).
  • No NC hemp product liability / warranty rule: Product liability for hemp products is general NC tort / warranty law; no hemp-specific strict-liability or recall-compensation rule exists.
  • Age, labelling, packaging — all absent (state): No NC hemp labelling mandate (THC content, serving, batch, warnings, child-resistant packaging) exists in current law. The only labelling/packaging mandates that bind NC hemp sellers are general food/drug/cosmetic labelling law and, for EBCI hemp retail, 17A CAR labelling/packaging requirements (full text [VERIFY]).
  • Online / mail-order hemp: No NC online hemp-sale licence, age-gate, or shipping rule exists in current law. Proposed Chapter 18D versions would have required internet age verification + adult-signature delivery — pending, not law. Interstate shipment of hemp that meets the federal definition is protected by the 2018 Farm Bill interstate-commerce provision; shipment of marijuana (including over-limit hemp) is a federal/state offence.

RUN 1 GAPS — DISPOSITIONS FOR GAPS FALLING IN PARTS 11–12

R1-02 — G.S. 90-95(d)/(h) pinpoint text — RESOLVED (in substance)

G.S. 90-95(d)(4) (Schedule VI possession, via the HB 576 / current-text rendering):

"A controlled substance classified in Schedule VI shall be guilty of a Class 3 misdemeanor, but any sentence of imprisonment imposed must be suspended and the judge may not require at the time of sentencing that the defendant serve a period of imprisonment as a special condition of probation. If the quantity of the controlled substance exceeds one-half of an ounce (avoirdupois) of marijuana or one-twentieth of an ounce (avoirdupois) of the extracted resin of marijuana, commonly known as hashish, the violation shall be punishable as a Class 1 misdemeanor. If the quantity … exceeds one and one-half ounces (avoirdupois) of marijuana, or three-twentieths of an ounce (avoirdupois) of the extracted resin of marijuana, commonly known as hashish, or if the controlled substance consists of any quantity of synthetic tetrahydrocannabinols or tetrahydrocannabinols isolated from the resin of marijuana, the violation shall be punishable as a Class I felony."

G.S. 90-95(h)(1) (trafficking): >10 to <50 lbs (in the current-text summaries: "in excess of 10 pounds, but less than 50 pounds" in older drafting; "10 to 50 pounds" in current summaries) = Class H felony, mandatory minimum 25 months, fine ≥$5,000; 50–2,000 lbs = Class G, 35 months, ≥$25,000; 2,000–10,000 lbs = Class F, 70 months, ≥$50,000; ≥10,000 lbs = Class D, 175 months, ≥$200,000. The exact "in excess of / or more" boundary wording for the 10-lb threshold should be re-checked against the clean §90-95(h) text before quoting a boundary case [VERIFY — (h)(1) boundary wording].

The (d) pinpoint above is sufficient to close R1-02 for possession; the (h) boundary wording remains a minor [VERIFY].

R1-05 — Delta-8 / converted cannabinoids: NC court decision or AG opinion — PARTIALLY RESOLVED / STILL OPEN for NC-specific authority

  • What was found: Anderson v. Diamondback Investment Group, LLC, 4th Circuit (2024), originating in NC, held hemp-derived products including synthetically derived THCs (THC-O at issue) are covered by the Farm Bill hemp definition provided they are under the 0.3% delta-9 threshold, rejecting DEA's position that such products are illegal scheduled substances. JD Supra (2026) and The Assembly (2026) both report this holding, with The Assembly noting the 4th Circuit held "delta-9 is the only metric that counts" under the Farm Bill definition at that time.
  • What was NOT found: No NC state-court decision (NC Court of Appeals / NC Supreme Court) on delta-8/converted cannabinoids, and no NC Attorney General formal opinion on delta-8, was located in this Part. The 4th Circuit decision is federal appellate law binding in NC federal courts and persuasive in NC state courts, but it is not a NC state-court decision or an AG opinion.
  • Disposition: R1-05 is PARTIALLY RESOLVED (4th Circuit authority identified) and STILL OPEN for a NC state-court / AG-opinion pinpoint. The federal redefinition (PL 119-37/119-103, effective 11 Dec 2026 for most products) will in any event supersede the Anderson delta-9-only framework for federal purposes; NC state law, unless HB 328 or a successor is enacted, will still use the delta-9 test after that date, creating the state/federal conflict noted in §12.2.

R1-13 — ALE hemp enforcement data — STILL OPEN

No official ALE dataset, annual total, seizure weight, arrest count, or case list was located. Episodic secondary evidence only (see §11.1.D): PA Senate GOP summary of ALE crackdowns, Carolina Hemp Hut Feb 2024 action + Sept 2025 plea-offer release, Hempary Graham 2025 raid/closure. An ALE Public Records Act request / DPS annual report would be required to close this gap. STILL OPEN.

R1-15 — Sales-tax details (NCDOR sources) — STILL OPEN

No NCDOR hemp-specific sales-tax guidance, ruling, or FAQ was opened in this Part. Secondary sources agree in substance (ordinary 4.75% + local sales tax applies to hemp products; no hemp excise; no 280E decoupling), and a 2024 fiscal note for a proposed 10.5% hemp-derived consumable excise (HB 563, 2023 session, not enacted) was located, but that is not NCDOR guidance and the proposed excise is not law. The Run 1 sales-tax baseline (4.75% + local) is therefore carried forward as a secondary-supported baseline, not as an NCDOR-confirmed pinpoint. STILL OPEN for NCDOR pinpoint.

R1-19 — CSERA pinpoint — RESOLVED (pinpoint) / STILL OPEN (full procedural/penalty text)

Resolved: Chapter 95, Article 20, §§95-230–95-235; §95-230 purpose quoted verbatim; §95-231 definitions (approved laboratory, controlled substance, examiner, examinee) identified. Still open: clean-text pinpoints for §95-232(c)–(e) confirmation/notice/retest details and §95-234 penalties ($250/$1,000 figure is secondary). See §11.8.

R1-22 — FDA-approved-drug Schedule VI amendment, SB 448 (2022) pinpoint — RESOLVED

SB 448, S.L. 2022-9, "Amendments to Schedule VI of the Controlled Substances Act." NC General Assembly bill summary: "S.L. 2022-9 automatically allows a prescription drug approved under federal law and classified as a Schedule VI controlled substance in North Carolina, to be lawfully used. This act became effective July 14, 2022, and applies to prescription drugs approved by the FDA on or after that date." House passage 92–9 (June 2022, Carolina Journal via Richmond Observer); Senate passage unanimous 10 May 2021 (as reported). The mechanism, as described in 2021 reporting, is automatic approval when FDA approves the drug, DEA makes corresponding federal schedule changes, and the NC Commission for Mental Health, Developmental Disabilities and Substance Abuse Services does not object within 30 days — that 30-day / Commission mechanism should be re-verified against the session law text before quoting it as the operative mechanism [VERIFY — S.L. 2022-9 mechanism wording]. The existence, session-law number, and effective date are resolved. Source: https://ncleg.gov/Legislation/SummariesPublication/Summary/2022/12/S448-SMBP-123(sl)-v-3/ — Accessed 2026-10-24.
